Fat Lie theory
Lennart Obster

Abstract
. We discuss a new point of view of representation theory of Lie groupoids and algebroids: fat Lie theory. The category of fat extensions is introduced, as well as the category of abstract 2 2 -term representations up to homotopy (ruths) — the intrinsic objects behind usual (split) 2 2 -term ruths. We obtain a one-to-one correspondence between them, and relate to the well-known equivalence between 2 2 -term ruths and VB-groupoids/algebroids. On the other hand, we show that fat extensions of groupoids correspond to general linear PB-groupoids. The differentiation procedure of fat extensions is discussed, as well as the functorial aspects of all mentioned correspondences. In particular, we upgrade the one-to-one correspondence between general linear PB-groupoids and VB-groupoids of Cattafi and Garmendia to an equivalence of categories. Fat extensions are intimately related to another notion we introduce: core extensions. We show that they correspond to vertically/horizontally core-transitive double groupoids, generalising work by Brown, Jotz-Lean and Mackenzie. This way, we also realise regular fat extensions as general linear double groupoids.



Introduction

The representation theory of Lie groupoids and algebroids has been widely studied by now, and many different viewpoints have been proposed; see, for example,
[
30
,
18
,
32
,
4
,
5
,
33
,
62
,
19
,
26
,
11
,
31
]
. The adjoint representation can be seen as a
2
-term representation up to homotopy
[
4
,
5
]
(we abbreviate representation up to homotopy to ruth from now on), and as a VB-groupoid/algebroid
[
53
,
54
,
43
]
through the tangent bundle. The two points of view are related in a very precise way, for there is an equivalence of categories between the categories of
2
-term ruths and VB-groupoids/algebroids that sends the adjoint ruth of a groupoid/algebroid to (an isomorphic copy of) the tangent bundle of the groupoid/algebroid
[
32
,
33
,
25
]
.

In this work, we propose another point of view, axiomatising a structure that has been around, but has not yet been explicitly mentioned in the literature. We introduce a category of
fat extensions
, both for Lie groupoids and for Lie algebroids, that is equivalent to the category of
2
-terms ruths and of VB-groupoids/algebroids. The structure has indeed been around in different contexts, for the main example is the first
jet groupoid
or first
jet algebroid
, together with its canonical cochain complex representation; see, for example,
[
8
,
55
,
21
,
9
,
18
]
. But in its generality presented here, it seems that the type of structure we will consider appeared first in
[
32
]
as the
fat algebroid
of a VB-algebroid, and in
[
33
]
as the
fat groupoid
of a VB-groupoid. Especially the fat algebroid (and its generalisations) have been studied in different contexts
[
57
,
29
,
52
,
47
,
38
,
58
,
41
]
. The fat groupoid appears, for example, in
[
38
,
41
,
31
]
.

The main goal of this work is to establish equivalences between the category of fat extensions and the following other models presented in the literature:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{$2$-term ruths}\}}

We also explain the connection to
[
62
]
and
[
26
]
. The discussion about the infinitesimal analogue of the correspondences is kept shorter, and we only briefly comment on the notion of “general linear PB-algebroid”. In turn, we also discuss the Lie functor — the differentiation procedure. The work presented here reveals many new concepts that become directly related to the representation theory of Lie groupoids and algebroids. On the other hand, we motivate throughout that, for many purposes, fat Lie theory is a natural and useful perspective on the representation theory in Lie theory.

We start the motivation of this work through two tangential projects that will be mentioned several times. One is joint work with João Nuno Mestre and Luca Vitagliano (to appear in
[
50
]
) and one is joint work with Ioan Mărcuţ and João Nuno Mestre (to appear in
[
44
]
).

The topic of
[
50
]
is to develop further the theory of (infinitesimally) multiplicative tensors on Lie groupoids and algebroids. In that project, we consider tensor products of VB-groupoids and VB-algebroids, as well as their associated linear cohomology theory. While working on that, it became clear that (infinitesimally) multiplicative tensors can be thought of as natural objects defined on the fat groupoid and the fat algebroid. In fact, there are at least two natural ways to take tensor products of fat extensions, and one in particular will show up in this work. Namely, morphisms of fat extensions can naturally be viewed as, what we call here,
invariant
cochains. Moreover, from the description presented in this work, it is immediate that maps of fat extensions define
1
-cocycles in a canonically defined cochain complex — a
3
-term ruth. Such reformulations to the theory of fat extensions show that the language is especially well-suited for such representation theoretic aspects like taking taking tensor products. While we make these points more precise, especially when discussing the functorial aspects, we discuss the relationship between tensor products of VB-groupoids,
2
-term ruths and fat extensions in greater detail in
[
50
]
.

Although we summarise the results already in this work, concerned with the theory of
abstract ruths
for Lie groupoids (this should be compared to the infinitesimal counterpart discussed in
[
46
]
). The main goal of that project, however, is to showcase different perspectives on the construction of certain homotopy operators for ruths, and how to do so
explicitly
. This should be compared to
[
61
]
. General homotopy theoretic methods (perturbation theory) are used to construct the desired homotopy operators for ruths, and this is especially satisfactory when understood abstractly. However, for the applications we have in mind, it is important to understand the analytical properties of the homotopy operators. Therefore, it is useful to pass to
split
ruths (which become ruths in the “usual sense”). Understanding the interplay between abstract and split ruths then becomes crucial. We start this work with a summary on abstract ruths, and we put an emphasis on abstract
2
-term ruths.

But motivation for fat Lie theory is presented already in the literature: usually, when the fat groupoid or algebroid is used, it is because the language of fat extensions helps in thinking about the problem at hand. Take, for example, the appearance of the fat groupoid in
[
31
]
. The
frame bundle
of a VB-groupoid, one of the main objects of study in
[
31
]
, is defined using the fat groupoid. The reason is simple: the structure of the frame bundle in terms of fat extensions uses only actions that are naturally associated to the fat groupoid and its canonical representations. Another example is by reinterpretation of
[
3
,
47
]
. Namely, the
Weil algebra
of a Lie algebroid, the infinitesimal counterpart of the
Bott-Shulman-Stasheff complex
of a Lie groupoid
[
10
]
, is efficient to write down using the jet algebroid and its canonical representations. The conditions appearing in the definition of the Weil algebra of
[
3
]
are natural in terms of the jet algebroid. The work
[
47
]
presents generalisations, and this has to do with possible generalisations of this work to the setting of LA-groupoids and double algebroids (see also
[
37
]
and
[
34
]
). A clear future direction of this work is therefore to describe “enriched” versions of the equivalences described above.

On the other hand, we will make first steps towards “higher” versions of the equivalences discussed above. In
[
27
]
, a higher version of the equivalence between VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths is discussed. More precisely, it is proved that the categories of so-called higher vector bundles (VB-
n
-groupoids), equipped with a normal weakly flat cleavage, and split (
n+1
)-term ruths are equivalent. Our reformulation of ruths seems to suggest that the appearance of “normal weakly flat cleavages” has to do with the fact that split ruths are considered instead of abstract ruths. So, a clear future direction of this work is to use the present work as a stepping stone towards formulating the correct categories and establish the equivalences above in such a “higher” generality. This also relates to
[
50
]
, for tensor products of VB-groupoids and VB-algebroids should be considered in such a higher context.

We now give a description of the contents of the work.

Description of the work

Before we explain the contents, let us give a brief introduction to the fat extension of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
. The fat groupoid

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightrightarrows M

can be seen as the Lie groupoid of “pointwise bihorizontal distributions” or “pointwise linear bisections”. That is, the bisections of
\widehat{V}_{G}
comprise the
linear bisections
of
V_{G}
. The groupoid structure of
\widehat{V}_{G}
is reminiscent of the usual group structure of bisections of a Lie groupoid. Moreover, the canonical action of the group of bisections on a groupoid defines a linear groupoid action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
V_{G}
. The restriction of this action to the (core)
2
-term cochain complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
of
V_{G}
, which sits naturally inside
V_{G}
as an action VB-groupoid
C_{M}\ltimes V_{M}
, reveals the structure of a
cochain complex representation
of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The short exact sequence of VB-groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M}\ltimes V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

descends, by passing to pointwise linear bisections, to a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

On the one hand, this reveals that
\widehat{V}_{G}
is an extension of
G
by the
bundle of invertible homotopies

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\coloneq\{h_{x}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\mid 1+\partial h\textnormal{ is invertible}\}.

On the other hand, it shows that the two pieces of data on
\widehat{V}_{G}
defined above — the cochain complex representation and the extension — are compatible in a precise way (we come back to this later). This identifies
\widehat{V}_{G}
as a
fat extension
of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

We now discuss the contents of this work. As mentioned above, our first goal in Section
2
is to explain an abstract point of view on ruths of Lie groupoids. Our viewpoint is related to the remark made after Definition 3.9 in
[
5
]
and also with the approach on ruths of Lie groupoids taken in
[
59
]
. However, our approach is to view ruths of Lie groupoids as intrinsically defined objects. The structure of ruth comes not with one, but two differentials: one of degree
+1
and one of degree
-1
— see Definition
2.1
. The reason for including the discussion is that, with this point of view on ruths, the equivalence of categories described in
[
33
,
25
]
can be understood canonically as the functor

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}\qquad V_{G}\mapsto C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}=C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}

using the VB-complex from
[
16
]
or the projectable complex from
[
33
,
19
]
. Although a more complete discussion will appear in
[
44
]
, we believe the point of view we take is useful, and clarifies that all equivalences appearing in this work are canonical, and come with a “split” version. In the special case of
2
-terms, these split categories are equivalent to the intrinsic models via the forgetful functor. We will comment on this several times.

After the introduction on abstract ruths, we formulate the category of fat extensions in Section
3
using the language of VB-groupoids. That is, we study properties of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid, and we will explain what structure turns it into a fat extension — see Definition
3.16
. We also explain that we can encode the data of a fat extension using the
fat category
instead. We call such objects
fat category extensions
— see Definition
3.22
. Fat category extensions are similar to fat extensions, but they are the intrinsic models for
weak representations
(see
[
62
]
). Having both fat extensions and fat category extensions in mind will turn out to be useful. Afterwards, we will describe the typical constructions of VB-groupoids in terms of fat extensions. For example, the dual of a fat extension (see Definition
3.45
) dualises simply its cochain complex representation, and the analogue of fiber products of VB-groupoids become a type of space of “invertible blockmatrices” for fat extensions (see Definition
3.37
). In essence, such phenomena seem to make the theory of fat extensions better suited, for example, for considering (infinitesimally) multiplicative tensors.

This introduction to the theory of fat extensions, together with the initial step of reformulating the theory of ruths of groupoids, will allow us to formulate the canonical one-to-one correspondences of categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}}

The functorial aspects of these equivalences are also discussed, but delayed until Section
7
. As mentioned above, the step from VB-groupoids to abstract
2
-term ruths is to associate to a VB-groupoid either its VB-complex or its projectable complex. The step from VB-groupoids to fat extensions is discussed in Section
4
. Associating an abstract
2
-term ruth of a fat extension is by taking its
invariant complex
(see Definition
4.14
). In other words, we reformulate the abstract
2
-term ruth of a VB-groupoid using the fat groupoid (see Proposition
4.17
). The complex turns out to be a type of
relative complex
as introduced in
[
56
]
. When interpreting the jet groupoid as a fat extension, the result is the adjoint ruth. This complex can be seen as the deformation complex of the Lie groupoid (see
[
19
]
), and it is the global counterpart of the deformation complex of a Lie algebroid in terms of the jet algebroid (see
[
57
]
). A small section, Section
10
, is dedicated to this new description of the deformation complex.

We then proceed: in
[
31
]
, it is shown that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
general linear PB-groupoids
(PB-groupoids with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid) and VB-groupoids. The fat groupoid is used explicitly in
[
31
]
; in hindsight, the fat groupoid shows up in
[
31
]
because of passing through the correspondence with the category of fat extensions:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}}

We will go through these correspondences in detail in Section
5
, and slightly reformulate the correspondence between VB-groupoids and general linear PB-groupoids. Moreover, part of this work is to formulate the category of general linear PB-groupoids that turns the above correspondences into equivalences of categories. This is done in Section
7
. The step

\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}

produces from a PB-groupoid a type of complex of
GL
-equivariant cochains.

Now, this relation between general linear PB-groupoids and fat extensions is rather similar to the discussion of
[
12
]
(and
[
36
]
), where double groupoids (double algebroids) are discussed and how double transitive groupoids (double transitive algebroids) are equivalently described by so-called
core diagrams
. The suspicion seems justified, for general linear PB-groupoids sometimes come with a
gauge double groupoid
. However, the double groupoids in question are not double transitive groupoids. Still, a generalisation of
[
12
]
shows that
core-transitive
double groupoids are equivalently described by, what we call here,
core extensions
(see Definition
A.3
). The generalisation should be seen as a clarification of the concluding remarks in
[
12
]
. Core extensions are generalisations of core diagrams of
[
12
]
, but they are also generalisations of so-called
crossed modules
of Lie groupoids as in
[
42
,
1
,
39
]
. The proof of the generalisation of the main result of
[
12
]
is postponed to Appendix
A
. There we also show that, as expected, core extensions of general linear double groupoids are exactly fat extensions. In the main body, in Section
5
, we do still explain what the concepts mentioned above mean.

After the discussion on the many equivalent ways of organising the information of the above categories in the setting of Lie groupoids, we turn to the infinitesimal side of things in Section
8
. That discussion is kept brief, but the essential points will be made. In particular, a new model for the linear complex of a VB-algebroid will be discussed in terms of an invariant complex of the fat algebroid (see Definition
8.19
). The discussion on the equivalences between
2
-term ruths, VB-algebroids and fat extensions is short, but rather complete. Although clear indications will be given about the steps to “PB-algebroids” and “core-transitive double algebroids”, these concepts are not fully developed and deserve a more thorough treatment elsewhere. The concept of core extension for Lie algebroids is also introduced (see Definition
8.27
). After that, in Section
9
, we discuss the Fat Lie theory to differentiate from global fat extensions to infinitesimal fat extensions. Part of that discussion appears in
[
29
]
, but we elaborate on some aspects. We also write the Van Est map of
[
6
,
16
]
in terms of invariant cochains.

Our discussion in Appendix
A
points towards an interesting new direction: can we describe PB-groupoids/algebroids in terms of “core data”? With an eye on defining geometric structures on Lie groupoids and algebroids, this seems an interesting direction that will be pursued elsewhere.

The content of the sections is as follows:

•

In Section
1
we recall some of the basic structure behind VB-groupoids. For the reader that is new to this topic, see, for example,
[
53
,
54
,
43
,
32
,
13
,
33
]

•

In Section
2
we explain our point of view on abstract ruths. We give a rather complete summary, but to keep the discussion short, the proofs of the statements presented there are kept to a minimum. The equivalence between VB-groupoids and abstract
2
-term ruths is Theorem
2.13
. In Proposition
2.12
we also demonstrate how abstract ruths can be “split” to recover the usual notion of ruths.

•

In Section
3
we give the necessary background on the structure behind the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid. In particular, we give an account on the bundle of invertible homotopies (see Section
3.2
). The main definition of fat (category) extension of groupoids will be discussed here (Definition
3.16
and Definition
3.22
), and so will examples of fat extensions (see Section
3.6
).

•

In Section
4
we explain the one-to-one correspondence between VB-groupoids and fat extensions (Proposition
4.5
) and we do the same for split
2
-ruths and fat extensions (see Section
4.3
, but also Section
6.2
) as well as for abstract
2
-term ruths and fat extensions (See Corollary
4.11
and Proposition
4.17
).

•

In Section
5
we define (general linear) PB-groupoids. Our setup is slightly different from
[
31
]
, but we comment on the differences. In the rest of the section, we set the correspondences between general linear PB-groupoids and fat extensions (See Proposition
5.20
), as well as between fat extensions and general linear double groupoids (in the regular case; the details for double groupoids are left for Appendix
A
). Here, we also present the model of
2
-term ruths in terms of general linear PB-groupoids (Proposition
5.27
). Since our definition of general linear PB-groupoid is slightly different than in
[
31
]
, we also show how general linear PB-groupoids correspond to VB-groupoids (Proposition
5.23
).

•

In Section
6
we make some other types of comments on the correspondences. Firstly, the vanishing theorem for
2
-term ruths of
[
5
,
16
]
is proved using the invariant complex of a fat extension (Proposition
6.1
). Secondly, the “split” category of fat extensions is introduced. That is, we define the notion of
fat splitting
(see Definition
6.2
), which corresponds to a cleavage on the level of VB-groupoids (see Proposition
6.3
). Thirdly, we explain the connection to the work
[
26
]
(see Section
6.3
).

•

In Section
7
we go into the functorial aspects. We introduce the definition of a fat extension through the language of VB-groupoids. That way, we will naturally set the equivalence of categories between VB-groupoids and fat extensions (Theorem
7.13
). We also comment on the equivalence of categories between split
2
-term ruths and fat extensions. Afterwards, using the language of VB-groupoids again, we will introduce morphisms of general linear PB-groupoids, and show that the categories of VB-groupoids and general linear PB-groupoids are equivalent (Theorem
7.22
). We will then also comment on the equivalence between general linear PB-groupoids and fat extensions (Theorem
7.24
).

•

In Section
8
we explain the infinitesimal picture. That is, we introduce fat extensions using the language of VB-algebroids, we give an abstract definition of fat extension, and then we go through the correspondences between abstract and split
2
-term ruths, VB-algebroids and fat extensions (Proposition
8.26
and Theorem
8.32
). Except for a small comment, “general linear PB-algebroids” and “general linear double algebroids” are not discussed.

•

In Section
9
we explain how, abstractly, fat extensions of groupoids differentiate, in a functorial way, to fat extensions of algebroids (Proposition
9.1
). Then we explicitly work out the details for the case of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid (see Section
9.3
; for this step, see also
[
29
]
). After that, we write the Van Est map for invariant cochains in terms of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid (Theorem
9.10
).

•

In Section
10
we make a small comment that we get a new model for the deformation cohomology of a Lie groupoid. In particular, we explain there that deformations of Lie groupoids give rise to
2
-cocycles (this is entirely analogous to the construction of
[
19
]
).

•

In Appendix
A
we introduce the notion of vertical/horizontal core extensions and prove that they correspond to vertically/horizontally core-transitive double groupoids (Theorem
A.9
). There is also a discussion on PB-groupoids that come with a gauge double groupoid. Proposition
A.21
shows that regular fat extensions correspond the a type of core extension.

1.
Notation for VB-groupoids

We develop here the relevant notation for VB-groupoids that we use.

1.1.
Notation for groupoids

We denote the structure maps of a groupoid
G\rightrightarrows M
by
(\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t},\mathbf{m},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{u})
, but we often simplify the notation to

\mathbf{m}(g,g^{\prime})=gg^{\prime}\qquad\mathbf{i}(g)=g^{-1}\qquad\mathbf{u}(x)=1_{x}.

We also use the division map of
G
, denoted by

\overline{\mathbf{m}}(g,g^{\prime})=g(g^{\prime})^{-1}.

The groupoid
G
has an underlying simplicial manifold (the nerve):
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(\bullet)}:\cdots\textnormal{ }G^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}

We denote the face maps by
1
1

1

That is,
d_{k}
“skips” the
k
-th basepoint.

d_{k}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\coloneq\begin{cases}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})&\textnormal{if }k=0\\
(g_{1},\dots,g_{k}g_{k+1},\dots,g_{\bullet})&\textnormal{if }1\leq k\leq\bullet-1\\
(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet-1})&\textnormal{if }k=\bullet\end{cases}

(for
\bullet=1
,
d_{0}=\mathbf{s}
and
d_{1}=\mathbf{t}
) and the degeneracy maps by
2
2

2

That is,
u_{k}
“repeats” the
k
-th basepoint.

u_{k}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\coloneq(g_{1},\dots,g_{k},1_{\mathbf{s}g_{k}},g_{k+1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\textnormal{ for all }0\leq k\leq\bullet.

We put
C^{\bullet}G=C^{\infty}G^{(\bullet)}
and

\delta\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\sum_{k=0}^{\bullet+1}(-1)^{k}d_{k}^{*}.

Then
CG
becomes a differential graded algebra with
\delta
and the product
3
3

3

If we view
f
and
g
as having degree
\bullet
, and
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
as having degree
\bullet^{\prime}
, we can interpret the sign as a Koszul sign rule. Similarly, our sign convention for
\delta
can be explained this way.

f\cdot f^{\prime}(g,g^{\prime})\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet\cdot\bullet^{\prime}}f(g)f^{\prime}(g^{\prime}),

where
f\in C^{\bullet}G
,
f^{\prime}\in C^{\bullet^{\prime}}G
and
(g,g^{\prime})\in G^{(\bullet+\bullet^{\prime})}
.

Lastly, we write

\mathbf{s}^{(\bullet)}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\coloneq\mathbf{s}g_{\bullet}\qquad\mathbf{t}^{(\bullet)}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\coloneq\mathbf{t}g_{1}\qquad\textnormal{pr}_{k}^{(\bullet)}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\coloneq g_{k}

and if
E_{M}\rightarrow M
is a vector bundle, we write

C^{\bullet}(G;E)\coloneq\Gamma(\mathbf{t}^{(\bullet)})^{*}E_{M}.

1.2.
Notation for VB-groupoids

We denote a VB-groupoid over
G\rightrightarrows M
by
V_{G}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

The structure maps for
V_{G}
and for
G
are denoted by the same symbols. We then write, for all
g\in G
,

\mathbf{s}_{g}:(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\qquad\mathbf{t}_{g}:(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}

for the induced linear maps on fibers. The scalar multiplication of
V_{G}
and of
V_{M}
are both denoted by
h^{\lambda}
, but we often simplify to

h^{\lambda}v=\lambda v.

For all
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
and
(v,w)\in V_{G}^{(2)}
we then have

\lambda(v\cdot w)=(\lambda v)\cdot(\lambda w).

That is, scalar multiplication by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a groupoid isomorphism
V_{G}\rightarrow V_{G}

[
13
]
.

For the core of the VB-groupoid we always use the right-core

C_{M}\coloneqq\ker\mathbf{s}|_{M}

and we denote

\partial\coloneq C_{M}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}}V_{M}

for the
2
-term complex associated to
V_{G}
.

Writing

\ell_{g}:(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{-0_{g}\cdot c^{-1}}(V_{G})_{g}\qquad r_{g}:(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\xrightarrow{c\cdot 0_{g}}(V_{G})_{g},

the dual VB-groupoid
V_{G}^{*}\rightrightarrows C_{M}^{*}
has source and target map given by
\mathbf{s}=\ell_{g}^{*}
and
\mathbf{t}=r_{g}^{*}
, and the other structure maps are given by

\mathbf{m}(\varphi_{g},\varphi_{h})(v\cdot w)=\varphi_{g}v+\varphi_{h}w\qquad\mathbf{i}(\varphi_{g})(v^{-1})=-\varphi_{g}v\qquad\mathbf{u}\varphi_{x}=\varphi_{x}|_{(C_{M})_{x}}.

In particular,
V_{G}^{*}
has

\partial^{*}:V_{M}^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}^{*}}C_{M}^{*}

as its associated
2
-term complex.

Lastly, we mean by a cleavage
\Sigma
a unital splitting of the exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sigma}

The cleavage
\Sigma
induces a unital splitting
\overline{\Sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}^{*}C_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{\Sigma}}

given by
\overline{\Sigma}_{g}v=(\Sigma_{g^{-1}}v)^{-1}
. The duals of these exact sequences are the corresponding exact sequences for the target and the source map, respectively, of the dual VB-groupoid.

1.3.
The fat groupoid and the fat extension of a VB-groupoid

One of the main points of this work is the observation that, equivalently, we can describe a VB-groupoid
V_{G}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
through its so-called fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightrightarrows M
, where

\widehat{V}_{G}=\{H_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\mid\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}=1\textnormal{ and }\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}.

Although we leave the full discussion for Section
3
, here is a summary. A VB-groupoid turns out to be equivalent to the data of, what we call, a
fat extension
. In short, the fat extension is defined out of the Lie groupoid map

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G\qquad H_{g}\mapsto g,

which is a surjective submersion. The kernel of this map is a bundle of Lie groups, denoted
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\subset\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
, and called the
bundle of invertible homotopies
for reasons that will be clarified in Section
3.2
. Its structure of bundle of Lie groups only depends on the cochain complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, which, in turn, is canonically a cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. But
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is also canonically a cochain complex representation of
\widehat{V}_{G}
, and the short exact sequence of Lie groupoids over
M
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

is compatible with these cochain complex representations. By this we mean that, on the one hand, the inclusion
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}
is equivariant with respect to the cochain complex representations and, on the other hand, the two natural conjugation actions of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
agree. Together, the cochain complex representation of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, the exact sequence above, and the compatibility between these two structures that we described is called the fat extension associated to
V_{G}
. So, the extension is of a very particular form, and our abstract definition of fat extension keeps track of this form.

1.4.
The linear complex and the VB-subcomplex

The components of the nerve of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
are vector bundles: for all
\bullet\geq 0
,

V_{G}^{(\bullet)}\rightarrow G^{(\bullet)}

is a vector bundle (with structure defined “componentwise”). The simplicial maps of
V_{G}
are vector bundle maps. In other words, the nerve of
V_{G}
is a simplicial vector bundle over the nerve of
G
. In particular, the
1
-homogeneous cochains
4
4

4

We wrote
\mathbb{R}_{>0}
for the open interval
(0,\infty)\subset\mathbb{R}
.

(1.1)

C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}=\{f\in C^{\infty}V_{G}^{(\bullet)}\mid\textnormal{ for all }\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0},\textnormal{ }\lambda^{*}f=\lambda\cdot f\}\subset C^{\bullet}V_{G}

(i.e. the linear functions on the nerve) form a subcomplex called the linear complex.

The VB-complex is a subcomplex of the linear complex given by
5
5

5

The conditions for
f\in C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}
to be an element of
C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
can be rewritten as

f(0_{g_{1}},v_{2},\dots,v_{\bullet})=0\textnormal{ and }f(0_{g_{1}}\cdot v_{2},\dots,v_{\bullet+1})=f(v_{2},\dots,v_{\bullet+1}).

C_{\textnormal{VB}}^{\bullet}V_{G}=\{f\in C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}\mid f(0_{g_{1}},v_{2},\dots,v_{\bullet})=0\textnormal{ and }\delta f(0_{g_{1}},v_{2},\dots,v_{\bullet+1})=0\}.

Using this notation, the inclusion
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}\hookrightarrow C_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}
is a quasi-isomorphism. Moreover,
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}
is isomorphic to the complex of projectable cochains

C_{\textnormal{proj}}^{\bullet}V_{G}=\{c:G^{(\bullet)}\rightarrow V_{G}\mid c(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\in(V_{G})_{g_{1}}\textnormal{ and }\mathbf{s}c(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})=\mathbf{s}c(1_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})\}

with differential given by

\delta=(-1)^{\bullet}(-\overline{\mathbf{m}}(d_{1}^{*},d_{0}^{*})+\textstyle\sum_{k=2}^{\bullet+1}(-1)^{k}d_{k}^{*}).

Without giving explicitly the differential, this complex appeared in
[
33
]
. For
V_{G}=TG
, we have
C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}=C_{\textnormal{def}}G
, and this complex appeared in
[
19
]
.

In terms of a representation up to homotopy (we write ruth for short),
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}
is isomorphic to the ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
associated to
V_{G}

\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}=C^{\bullet}(G;C_{M})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(G;V_{M})

with differential given by
6
6

6

The structure of the ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is denoted by
\partial,R_{1},R_{2}
. Defining this structure out of
V_{G}
uses a cleavage; we will recall the construction in Section
6.2
.

\delta=\partial+R_{1}+R_{2}+(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\bullet+1}(-1)^{k}d_{k}^{*}.

We view the dual ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}^{*}
as being concentrated in positive degrees as well (this is a non-standard convention; we come back to this in the next section).

We describe a new model for the VB-complex in Section
4
, which comes from understanding VB-groupoids as a fat extension. In Section
2
, however, we adopt a point of view on ruths which allows us to see all the different models of VB-complexes canonically as ruths. The model
\mathcal{E}_{G}
will then be called a split ruth.

2.
Abstract representations up to homotopy of Lie groupoids

The following section is kept short for it will appear in
[
44
]
. Still, we would like to emphasise the main points. We think the point of view we take is useful, and we stress on the natural appearance of the fat groupoid in the main example (see Proposition
2.12
).

2.1.
The definition of an abstract ruth

Before we explain the definition, we will use that, on
CG
, there is a natural map that is not so often considered explicitly:

\nu:C^{\bullet}G\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}G\qquad\nu\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\sum_{k=0}^{\bullet}(-1)^{k}u_{k}^{*}.

Of course, viewing
G
as a simplicial manifold, it is clear that the fact that
\delta^{2}=0
only takes into account the structure of the face maps. The properties of the degeneracy maps imply that

\nu^{2}=0

and, because of the compatibility of the face maps with the degeneracy maps, we have
7
7

7

Here and later, the notation
[\cdot,\cdot]
is used for the graded commutator. In this case, the equation
[\delta,\nu]=0
means that
\delta\nu+\nu\delta=0
.

[\delta,\nu]=0.

Observe that the normalised cochains in
CG
form the kernel of
\nu
in odd degrees. In even degrees, this fact is true when considering cochains
f
such that
f|_{M}=0
; that is,
8
8

8

The usual hat notation
\widehat{C}G
for the normalised complex conflicts notation that we use later.

(CG)_{\nu}=\{f\in\ker\nu\mid f|_{M}=0\}.

We now take the following approach to ruths:

Definition 2.1
.

An
abstract representation up to homotopy
(ruth)
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a finitely generated projective right graded
CG
-module together with maps

\delta:\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet+1}\qquad\nu:\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet-1}

satisfying the Leibniz rule: for all
c\in\mathcal{E}_{G}
and
f\in CG
,

\delta(c\cdot f)=\delta(c)\cdot f+(-1)^{c}c\cdot\delta f\qquad\nu(c\cdot f)=\nu(c)\cdot f+(-1)^{c}c\cdot\nu f

and satisfying
9
9

9

That is,
\delta^{2}=0
,
\eta^{2}=0
, and
[\delta,\eta]=0
.

(\delta+\nu)^{2}=0.

To clarify: a
CG
-module
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is finitely generated projective if we can find a split short exact sequence of
CG
-modules
10
10

10

Here,
C^{\bullet}G[-\ell]=C^{\bullet-\ell}G
, so it is concentrated in degrees
\bullet\geq\ell
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textstyle\bigoplus_{k\in I}CG[-\ell_{k}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{E}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where
I
is a finite indexing set, and all
\ell_{k}\geq 0
. Alternatively, this is equivalent to the existence of a
CG
-module
\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathcal{K}\oplus\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a free
CG
-module.

2.2.
Locally free sheaves of
CG
-modules

In fact, we conjecture that a type of Serre-Swan theorem holds which relates higher vector bundles with abstract ruths (see Remark
2.14
). A weak version at least holds: the global sections functor sets an equivalence of categories between “locally free sheaves of
CG
-modules” and finitely generated projective
CG
-modules. We discuss next how we think of a (locally free) sheaf of
CG
-modules.

Remark 2.2
.

We can view
CG
as a graded sheaf (over
M
) of right
CG
-modules as follows. For each open set
U
of
M
, we set

G^{(\bullet)}U=(\mathbf{t}^{(\bullet)})^{-1}U=\{(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\in G^{(\bullet)}\mid\mathbf{t}g_{1}\in U\}.

We then write

C^{\bullet}G(U)=C^{\infty}(G^{(\bullet)}U).

The product of
CG
defines the structure of right
CG
-module on this sheaf. That is, for
f\in C^{\bullet}G(U)
and
f^{\prime}\in C^{\bullet}G
, we define
f\cdot f^{\prime}\in C^{\bullet+\bullet^{\prime}}G(U)
by

(f\cdot f^{\prime})(g,g^{\prime})\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet\cdot\bullet^{\prime}}f(g)\cdot f^{\prime}(g^{\prime}).

When we view
CG
as a sheaf in this way, the differential is only defined on the global sections. There are ways to think of the differential as being local which is interesting for developing further representation theory for non-Hausdorff Lie groupoids. This will be discussed in
[
44
]
.

Consider now a graded sheaf of
CG
-modules
\mathcal{E}_{G}
. That is, for all open sets
U\subset M
, we have a right
CG
-module
\mathcal{E}_{G}(U)
(altogether satisfying sheaf-like properties). Locally free means that, for all
x\in M
, there is an open subset
U\ni x
of
M
such that

\mathcal{E}_{G}(U)\cong\textstyle\bigoplus_{k\in I}CG[-\ell_{k}](U)

as sheaves of
CG
-modules, where
I
is a finite indexing set and all
\ell_{k}\geq 0
. The following result is a first step towards a type of Serre-Swan theorem.

Proposition 2.3
.

The global sections functor establishes an equivalence of categories

\{\textnormal{locally free sheaves of $CG$-modules}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\textnormal{finitely generated projective $CG$-modules}\}.

Although the proof is relatively straightforward, we discuss the proof only in
[
44
]
. In fact, the main ingredients of the proof will be discussed next.

Remark 2.4
.

Notice that
CG
is canonically filtered by ideals:

\mathcal{I}_{k}\coloneq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\bullet\geq k}C^{\bullet}G.

We write
\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{I}_{k}\cap C^{\bullet}G
. These ideals canonically define a filtration on a locally free sheaf of
CG
-modules / finitely generated projective
CG
-module
\mathcal{E}_{G}
:

\mathcal{F}_{k}\coloneq\mathcal{E}_{G}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{k}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\bullet\geq k}\{c\in\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}\mid c=\textstyle\sum_{m}c_{m}\cdot f_{m}\textnormal{ for some }f_{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{k}^{\bullet^{\prime}}\textnormal{ and }c_{m}\in\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet-\bullet^{\prime}}\}.

We write
\mathcal{F}_{k}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}
.

Proposition 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{G}
be a ruth. For all
\bullet\geq 0
, the quotients

\Gamma E_{M}^{\bullet}=\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{0}/\mathcal{F}^{\bullet}_{1}

form the space of sections of vector bundles
E_{M}^{\bullet}
over
M
. Moreover, the differential
\delta
descends to a cochain complex of vector bundles:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{M}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{M}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{M}^{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{M}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

(We assume that
E_{M}^{\bullet>n}=0
; in this case we call
\mathcal{E}_{G}
an
n
-term abstract ruth).

Remark 2.6
.

Notice that we are assuming that ruths are non-negatively graded. In particular, when dualising a ruth, we shift it to non-negative degrees. To be clear, given an
n
-term abstract ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
,

(\mathcal{E}_{G}^{*})^{k}\coloneq\textnormal{Hom}^{k}_{CG\textnormal{-lin}}(\mathcal{E}_{G},CG)=\{\varphi:\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}\rightarrow C^{\bullet+k}G\mid\textnormal{for all }f\in CG,\textnormal{ }\varphi(c\cdot f)=\varphi(c)\cdot f\}

is the dual ruth, which is concentrated in negative degrees. The structure maps are given by

\delta^{*}\varphi=\delta\varphi-(-1)^{\varphi}\varphi\delta\qquad\eta^{*}\varphi\coloneq\eta\varphi-(-1)^{\varphi}\varphi\eta.

But we will consider
\mathcal{E}_{G}^{*}[-n]
, which introduces a sign
(-1)^{n}
in the above equations. Instead of writing
\mathcal{E}_{G}[-n]
, we simply write
\mathcal{E}_{G}^{*}
. So, the dual construction we consider is a “shifted duality” and it aligns with the dualisation of VB-groupoids.

2.3.
The normalised complex of an abstract ruth

The quotient
CG/I=C^{\infty}M
can be thought of as coming from the unit map

1_{M}\hookrightarrow G.

viewed as a groupoid map (
1_{M}
denotes the unit groupoid). Now, we can always pullback an abstract ruth via a Lie groupoid map, and what Proposition
2.5
shows is that
\mathcal{E}_{G}|_{M}
is a complex of vector bundles. This allows us, in turn, to formulate what the normalised cochains of
\mathcal{E}_{G}
are.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{G}
be an abstract ruth. The
normalised complex

(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}
in
\mathcal{E}_{G}
, whose elements are called
normalised cochains
, is the differential graded
(CG)_{\nu}
-module given by

(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}\coloneq\{f\in\ker\nu\mid f|_{M}=0\}.

Using the inclusion
(CG)_{\nu}\hookrightarrow CG
(i.e. changing coefficients), we have:

Proposition 2.8
.

The inclusion of differential graded modules

(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}_{G}

is a quasi-isomorphism.

The proof will be discussed in
[
44
]
.

2.4.
Split ruths

Another way to put it, is that the splitting preserves normalised cochains. This is a non-trivial assumption (see Remark

Definition 2.9
.

A

of an abstract ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is a filtration preserving isomorphism

\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}\cong C(G;E_{M})^{\bullet}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\bullet}C^{k}(G;E_{M}^{\bullet-k})

of

We adopt the following terminology if we want to emphasise the difference:

Definition 2.10
.

A
split ruth
is a graded vector bundle
E_{M}^{\bullet\geq 0}
together with a differential
\delta=\textstyle\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\delta_{\ell}
on the graded
CG
-module

\mathcal{E}_{G}^{\bullet}=C(G;E_{M})^{\bullet}=\textstyle\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\bullet}C^{k}(G;E_{M}^{\bullet-k})

turning it into a differential graded
CG
-module, i.e. such that the Leibniz rule

\delta(c\cdot f)=\delta c\cdot f+(-1)^{c}c\cdot\delta f

is satisfied, and it is
unital
if
\delta
preserves normalised cochains.

Remark 2.11
.

Given a split ruth, we can define maps
R=(R_{\ell})
, where

R_{\ell}\in\Gamma\textnormal{End}^{\ell,1-\ell}E_{M}\qquad\textnormal{End}^{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}E_{M}\coloneq\textnormal{Hom}((\mathbf{s}^{(\ell)})^{*}E_{M}^{\bullet},(\mathbf{t}^{(\ell)})^{*}E_{M}^{\bullet+\ell^{\prime}})

are defined as

R_{\ell\neq 1}\coloneq\delta_{\ell}\qquad R_{1}\coloneq\delta_{1}-(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\bullet+1}(-1)^{k}d_{k}^{*}.

This uses the Leibniz rule and the fact that all
CG

(so of bidegree

, the associated

14
14

14

As mentioned in Section

, we think of

.

R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\cdot f(g,g^{\prime})=(-1)^{\ell\cdot f}R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}(g)f(g^{\prime})

That
\delta^{2}=0
then translates into the Maurer-Cartan equation

\delta_{\textnormal{ruth}}R+\tfrac{1}{2}[R,R]_{\textnormal{ruth}}=0.

The Maurer-Cartan equation can be taken literally as follows: the dgLa of ruths, that controls deformations, is given by

C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{ruth}}\coloneq\textstyle\bigoplus_{\ell\geq 0}\textnormal{End}^{\ell,\bullet-\ell}E_{M}

together with the graded Lie bracket and differential defined through the differential graded algebra
15
15

15

Notice that

and

.

R_{\ell}\cdot R_{\ell^{\prime}}(g,g^{\prime})=(-1)^{\ell\cdot R_{\ell^{\prime}}}R_{\ell}(g)R_{\ell^{\prime}}(g^{\prime})\qquad\delta_{\textnormal{ruth}}=(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\sum_{k=1}^{\ell}(-1)^{k}d_{k}^{*}

That is, the graded Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\textnormal{ruth}}
is the commutator. This differential graded algebra appeared in

2
]
. The deformation complex (which is a dgLa) of a ruth

is then

C_{\textnormal{def}}^{\bullet}R=C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{ruth}}

together with the same graded Lie bracket, but with

\delta_{R}\coloneq\delta_{\textnormal{ruth}}+[R,\cdot].

Notice that the Maurer-Cartan equation above gives rise to
non-unital ruths
; we didn’t take into account the condition that
\delta
preserves normalised cochains. From the split diagram above, it is readily verified that this happens precisely when
R_{1}(1)=1
and
u_{k}^{*}R_{\ell\geq 2}=0
for all degeneracies
u_{k}
.

2.5.
Towards a Serre-Swan theorem

Attempts to define ruths as finitely generated projective differential graded
CG
-modules can already be found in a remark in
[
5
]
(after Definition 3.9 there), and
[
59
]
. However, it seems that in the literature ruths of Lie groupoids have not properly been studied as “abstract” or “canonical” objects. The recognition of the map
\eta
as being part of the structure of a ruth is crucial for achieving this. We would like to emphasise that abstract ruths appear canonically in examples. This becomes especially helpful in more complicated examples, but we observe here that the VB-complex of a VB-groupoid is a valid (universal) example of a
2
-term abstract ruth. In essence, this was proved in
[
33
]
.

Proposition 2.12
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid. Then
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
is a
2
-term abstract ruth. Moreover, (unital) cleavages correspond to (unital) splittings.

Proof.

Indeed,
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
is finitely generated and projective as a
CG
-module, for a cleavage
\Sigma
sets an isomorphism

C_{\textnormal{VB}}^{\bullet}V_{G}\cong C^{\bullet}(G;V_{M}^{*})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(G;C_{M}^{*})\qquad f\mapsto(f_{V_{M}},f_{C_{M}}),

where
16
16

16

We use here that, if
f\in C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
, then
f(v_{g_{1}},\dots,v_{g_{\bullet}})\in\mathbb{R}
only depends on
(v_{1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})
.

f_{V_{M}}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})v\coloneq f(\overline{\Sigma}_{g_{1}}v,g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})\qquad f_{C_{M}}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})c\coloneq f(-c^{-1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

On the other hand, given a (unital) splitting of
C_{\textnormal{VB}}^{\bullet}V_{G}
, the (unital) cleavage
\Sigma
at
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
can be recovered as follows. Namely, there is a unique linear map
\overline{\Sigma}:\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M}\rightarrow V_{G}
with the property that, for all pairs
(f_{V_{M}},f)
related by the splitting,

f_{V_{M}}=f\circ\overline{\Sigma}.

That
\overline{\Sigma}
is a splitting (for
\mathbf{t}
) follows by realising that it is injective and, since the ruth splitting preserves the filtration, we must have

\overline{\Sigma}_{g}=\overline{\Sigma}_{g}\mathbf{t}_{g}\overline{\Sigma}_{g}.

If the ruth splitting is unital, then for all normalised
f
,

f\circ\overline{\Sigma}_{1}=0.

So,
\overline{\Sigma}_{1}
maps into the units of
V_{G}
, and therefore it is the unit map. This proves the statement.
∎

We will discuss a slightly different proof, which shows directly that
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
can be seen as a locally free sheaf of
CG
-modules. The main point is that, locally, we can use a special type of cleavage
H
, one for which all maps
\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}
are invertible. We can see such sections as local sections of the surjective submersion
17
17

17

See Section
1
for a short introduction to the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
and its surjective submersion
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
. In Section
3
we give a more thorough introduction

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G\qquad H_{g}\mapsto g.

That is,
H
is a smooth family of linear maps

g\mapsto(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{H_{g}}(V_{G})_{g}

such that, for all
g\in G
,
\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}=1
and
\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}
is a linear isomorphism.

Now, in the above isomorphism using the cleavage
\Sigma
, we used the linear isomorphisms

(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\oplus(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\qquad(c,v)\mapsto\ell_{g}c+\overline{\Sigma}_{g}v.

Given a local section
H
of
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
, we can instead use the linear isomorphism

(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\oplus(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\qquad(c,v)\mapsto\ell_{g}c+H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}v.

In fact, this observation leads to a whole new description of
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
in terms of the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
. This will be made more precise in Section
4.5
.

Alternative proof of Proposition
2.12
.

As said before, we will show that
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
is locally free as a sheaf of
CG
-modules (see Proposition
2.3
). In fact, given
U\subset M
and a local section
H
of
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
defined over
U
, then
H
defines an isomorphism

C_{\textnormal{VB}}^{\bullet}V_{G}(U)\cong C^{\bullet}(G;V_{M}^{*})(U)\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(G;C_{M}^{*})(U)\qquad f\mapsto(f_{V_{M}},f_{C_{M}}).

We describe the inverse map. Observe first that
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}=\Gamma V_{M}^{*}
. To construct elements of degree
1
, we construct out of
f_{V_{M}}
in
C^{1}(G;V_{M}^{*})
a linear function
f\in C_{\textnormal{lin}}^{1}V_{G}
:

f(v)=f_{V_{M}}(g)\mathbf{t}_{g}v.

Since
C^{1}(G;V_{M}^{*})\cong\Gamma V_{M}\otimes C^{\infty}G
, we see that every such function is locally generated by sections of
V_{M}^{*}
(sitting in degree
0
). Now, pick a local section
H
of
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
. Then, using
H
, sections
f_{C_{M}}
of
C_{M}^{*}
give rise to linear functions
f\in C_{\textnormal{lin}}^{1}V_{G}
:

f(v)=f_{C_{M}}\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}v,

where

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}:(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\qquad v\mapsto r_{g}((H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v)^{-1}-v^{-1}).

It is readily verified that both type of linear functions
f\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}
are elements of
C^{1}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
. Similarly, we obtain elements
C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
out of (local) sections of
C^{\bullet}(G;V_{M}^{*})
and out of (local) sections of
C^{\bullet-1}(G;C_{M}^{*})
. On the other hand, if
f\in C^{1}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
, then

f_{C_{M}}c=f(-c^{-1})

is a linear function on
C_{M}
, and

f_{V_{M}}(g)v=f(H_{g}(\mathbf{t}H_{g})^{-1}v)

defines a linear function on
\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M}
. We can now write

f(v)=f_{C_{M}}\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}v+f_{V_{M}}(g)\mathbf{t}_{g}v.

Similarly we can decompose elements of
C_{\textnormal{VB}}^{\bullet}V_{G}
. This sets the desired isomorphism, so
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
is locally free as a sheaf of
CG
-modules.
∎

The equivalence of categories between VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths can now be understood through the assignment

V_{G}\mapsto C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}\coloneq C_{\textnormal{VB}}(V_{G}^{*})\cong(C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G})^{*}.

The assignment on morphisms uses, for
\Phi:V_{G}\rightarrow V_{H}
a VB-groupoid map, the dual map

\Phi^{*}:V_{H}^{*}\rightarrow V_{G}^{*}

and then the pullback map

(\Phi^{*})^{*}_{\textnormal{VB}}:C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}\rightarrow C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{H}^{*}.

(or this pullback map first and then the dual map of ruths). This defines a functor, and it is an equivalence of categories
[
33
,
25
]
:

Theorem 2.13
.

The functor

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}\qquad V_{G}\mapsto C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}\qquad\Phi\mapsto(\Phi^{*})^{*}_{\textnormal{VB}}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Remark 2.14
.

Perhaps we should identify Theorem
2.13
truly as a Serre-Swan type theorem. That is, we can think of
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}
(or
C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}
) as a type of “space of sections” of the VB-groupoid
V_{G}
. A current work in progress is to understand generalisations of this fact. We expect to set a canonical Serre-Swan correspondence between higher vector bundles (see
[
27
]
) and abstract ruths. We believe that, in doing so, the choice of a “normal weakly flat cleavage” in
[
27
]
for higher vector bundles should relate to the above unital splittings of abstract ruths. The reason is that, indeed, the normal weakly flat cleavage attached to a higher vector bundle is used in
[
27
]
precisely to prove that the induced ruth is unital.

Remark 2.15
.

A particular instance of the Serre-Swan theorem is how the tensor product constructions of VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths relate (for the latter, see
[
2
]
). This is part of the work
[
50
]
.

2.6.
Cochain complex representations

We end this section by mentioning a special type of
2
-term ruth that is central to this work:

Definition 2.16
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid. A cochain complex representation, or strict ruth, is a split ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
such that
R_{\ell\geq 2}=0
.

A cochain complex representation of
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is a pair of representations of
G
on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
, such that the map
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is equivariant with respect to the actions. We write the abstract ruth associated to a cochain complex representation by

C(G;C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C_{\textnormal{VB}}(G\ltimes(V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})).

There are at least two more ways of describing a cochain complex representation that we want to discuss.

Remark 2.17
.

Given two vector bundles
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
, there is a type of general linear groupoid given by

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\coloneq\textnormal{GL }C_{M}\times_{M\times M}\textnormal{GL }V_{M}

which we can view as a fiber product of Lie groupoids.
18
18

18

We think here of
M\times M
as the pair groupoid, and we use the (surjective) anchor maps.
Now, the differential induces a map

[\cdot,\partial]:\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\textnormal{pr}_{1}^{*}C_{M},\textnormal{pr}_{2}^{*}V_{M})\qquad\Phi=(\Phi^{C_{M}},\Phi^{V_{M}})\mapsto\Phi^{V_{M}}\partial-\partial\Phi^{C_{M}}.

We can interpret the preimage of zero

\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\coloneq\{\Phi\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\mid\partial\Phi^{C_{M}}=\Phi^{V_{M}}\partial\}

as cochain isomorphisms
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

However, while
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
is a subgroupoid of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, it does not define a smooth Lie subgroupoid in general. In fact, the above commutator map is the restriction of a linear map, and then it is readily verified that it has constant rank precisely when
\partial
has constant rank. In any case, we can think of a cochain complex representation as a Lie groupoid map

G\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\dasharrow\textnormal{Hom}(\textnormal{pr}_{1}^{*}C_{M},\textnormal{pr}_{2}^{*}V_{M})

such that postcomposing with the map
[\cdot,\partial]
is zero.

Remark 2.18
.

Another way to organise the information of a cochain complex representation of a Lie groupoid
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is as a Lie groupoid map in the following way6. Consider first the general linear groupoid from Remark
2.17
:

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\coloneq\textnormal{GL }C_{M}\times_{M\times M}\textnormal{GL }V_{M}.

This groupoid acts (on the right) on “differentials”
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
by conjugating the differentials: given
\Psi_{y,x}=(\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}},\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
\partial_{y}\in\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
,

\partial_{y}\cdot\Psi_{y,x}\coloneq(\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}.

We write the resulting action groupoid by

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\coloneq\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\rtimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M}).

A cochain complex representation of
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
can now be seen as a groupoid map

G\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

whose basemap is the differential:

\partial:M\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}).

If we postcompose this map with the groupoid projection
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, then we recover the map we considered in Remark
2.17
.

3.
The fat extension of a VB-groupoid

We start this section by introducing the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid. In this generality, it seems to have first appeared in
[
33
]
.

3.1.
The fat groupoid

Let
V_{G}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
be a VB-groupoid. Consider the surjective linear map

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},V_{G})\rightarrow\textnormal{End }V_{M}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}

over
\mathbf{s}:G\rightarrow M
, and the preimage of the identity section

\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}=\{H_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\mid\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}=1\}.

Then
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
is an affine bundle over
G
19
19

19

The fiber over
g
is isomorphic to
\textnormal{Hom}((V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g},(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g})
.
and it is a Lie category (without boundary) over
M
(a thorough discussion on Lie categories is presented in
[
35
]
). The structure maps are given as follows: we set
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}H_{g}=\mathbf{s}g
,
\widehat{\mathbf{t}}H_{g}=\mathbf{t}g
and

(H_{g}\cdot H_{g^{\prime}})v=H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g^{\prime}}H_{g^{\prime}}v)\cdot H_{g^{\prime}}v.

The invertible elements of this Lie category are given by the following open subset of
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
:

\widehat{V}_{G}=\{H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\mid\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}

which becomes a Lie groupoid with

(H_{g})^{-1}v=(H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}v)^{-1}.

Definition 3.1
.

Given a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
, we call
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightrightarrows M
the fat groupoid (of
V_{G}
).

We can think of the elements of
\widehat{V}_{G}
as coming from (fiberwise) linear bisections of
V_{G}
.

Remark 3.2
.

Notice that there is a one-to-one correspondence between bisections of
\widehat{V}_{G}
and linear bisections of
V_{G}
. Indeed, a linear bisection
\sigma
of
V_{G}
is a bisection

V_{M}\rightarrow V_{G}\qquad x\mapsto\sigma x

that is a vector bundle map over a bisection
\sigma
of
G
.
20
20

20

Alternatively, it is a
0
-homogeneous bisection, i.e.
\lambda^{*}\sigma(=\lambda^{-1}\circ\sigma\circ\lambda)=\sigma
.
We can therefore, equivalently, view
\sigma
as the bisection

M\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad x\mapsto(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sigma x}(V_{G})_{\sigma x}

of
\widehat{V}_{G}
. There are also “core bisections” of
V_{G}
(over the identity bisection of
G
): given
\chi\in\Gamma C_{M}
, we can construct a bisection
\sigma_{\chi}
of
V_{G}
by setting

\sigma_{\chi}(v_{x})=1_{v_{x}}+\chi(x).

Applying the target to this expression gives the translation
v_{x}\mapsto v_{x}+\partial\chi(x)
.

Remark 3.3
.

In
[
33
]
the fat groupoid is denoted by
\widehat{G}
. However, we prefer to keep track of the dependence on
V_{G}
in the notation.

We end the section by mentioning a few examples. In Section
3.6
we discuss more examples and go into more detail.

Example 3.4
.

Probably the most well-known example of a “fat groupoid” is the jet groupoid
J^{1}G
:

J^{1}G=\{j^{1}_{x}\sigma\mid\sigma\in\textnormal{Bis}_{\textnormal{loc}}G\}.

In words, its elements consist of all possible values of
1
-jets of local bisections
\sigma
. The product is induced by the usual product of (local) bisections. We can realise
J^{1}G
as the fat groupoid
\widehat{TG}
of the VB-groupoid
TG
. Indeed, every
1
-jet of a local bisection takes the form

H_{g}:T_{\mathbf{s}g}M\rightarrow T_{g}G,

where
d\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}=1
and
d\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}
is a linear isomorphism. On the other hand, we can integrate such a linear map
H_{g}
to a local bisection
\sigma
with the property that
j^{1}_{\mathbf{s}g}\sigma=H_{g}
. This follows from standard proofs on the existence of local bisections through elements of
G
.

Example 3.5
.

Let
H\rightrightarrows N
be a Lie subgroupoid of
G\rightrightarrows M
. The jet groupoid
J^{1}G
contains the Lie subgroupoid

J^{1}_{H}G\coloneq\{j^{1}_{y}\sigma\in J^{1}G\mid y\in N,\im\sigma|_{N}\subset H\}\rightrightarrows N.

This subgroupoid is equal to
J^{1}H
only if
N=M
. How are
J^{1}H
and
J^{1}G
related if
N\neq M
? For now, we only observe that
J^{1}_{H}G
and
J^{1}H
are related via a restriction map

J^{1}_{H}G\rightarrow J^{1}H.

A similar phenomenon appears when considering
J^{1}_{\mathcal{N}}(G,H)
, the fat groupoid of

\mathcal{N}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{N}(M,N),

the normal bundle, which is a VB-groupoid over
H
.
21
21

21

The Lie groupoid structure of the normal bundle
\mathcal{N}(G,H)
is induced by that of
TG
.
Namely, there is again a natural map

J^{1}_{H}G\rightarrow J^{1}_{\mathcal{N}}(G,H).

In Section
3.6
, we will further clarify the relation between
J^{1}H
,
J^{1}G
and
J^{1}_{\mathcal{N}}(G,H)
.

Example 3.6
.

Let
E_{M}\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle. The general linear groupoid

\textnormal{GL }E_{M}=\{\Psi_{y,x}:E_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}E_{y}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}.

is the fat groupoid of the pair groupoid
E_{M}\times E_{M}\rightrightarrows E_{M}
, seen as a VB-groupoid over
M\times M\rightrightarrows M
.

The last example shows the extension of the “classical” analogues of the correspondences we aim to describe in Section
4
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{f.g.p. $C^{\infty}M$-mods}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Vector bundles}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{Principal $\textnormal{GL}$-bundles}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{\textnormal{fat extensions of $M\times M$ over $1_{E_{M}}$}\}}
Serre-Swan
Fr
GL
Gauge groupoid

The goal of the coming two sections is to discuss two essential pieces of information: the bundle of invertible homotopies, and the canonical representations of the fat groupoid. This is enough to understand the basic structure of fat extensions. In the sections afterwards we look at several consequences of the structure behind the fat groupoid and discuss, in this order, the fat category, examples, other models and dualisation of the fat groupoid and, lastly, the fat pairing.

3.2.
The bundle of invertible homotopies of a
2
-term complex

As mentioned before, if
V_{G}
is a VB-groupoid, then the groupoid map

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G\qquad H_{g}\mapsto g

is a surjective submersion. The kernel of this map is a bundle of Lie groups, and it can be identified with the open set of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
given by

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})=\{h_{x}:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{x}\mid 1+\partial h_{x}\textnormal{ is invertible}\}

(via
h_{x}\mapsto 1+h_{x}
). The unit is the zero map and

h_{x}\cdot h_{x}^{\prime}=h_{x}+h_{x}^{\prime}+h_{x}\partial h_{x}^{\prime}\qquad h_{x}^{-1}=-h_{x}(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}=-(1+h_{x}\partial)^{-1}h_{x}.

Elements
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
are homotopies, for they show that the isomorphism
22
22

22

Notice that we have defined two maps,
1+\partial h_{x}:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(V_{M})_{x}
and
1+h_{x}\partial:(C_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{x}
. The latter map is a linear isomorphism by the five-lemma.

1+[\partial,h_{x}]

(from
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
to itself) is homotopic to the identity map. The product can then be interpreted as a “composition of homotopies” (see also Section
4.3
):

Lemma 3.7
.

Suppose we are given complexes
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
and
C_{3}
(with differentials
\partial
) with the following homotopy data: let
23
23

23

We use a subscript
ji
to denote the relevant map
C_{i}\rightarrow C_{j}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Psi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta}

be maps satisfying

[\partial,\Phi]=0\qquad[\partial,\Psi]=0\qquad\Phi-\Psi=[\partial,\eta].

Then

\Phi_{32}\Phi_{21}-\Psi_{32}\Psi_{21}=[\partial,\eta_{32}\Phi_{21}+\Psi_{32}\eta_{21}]=[\partial,\eta_{32}\Psi_{21}+\Phi_{32}\eta_{21}].

Moreover,
[\partial,\eta_{32}\eta_{21}]=0
if and only if

\eta_{32}\Phi_{21}+\Psi_{32}\eta_{21}=\eta_{32}\Psi_{21}+\Phi_{32}\eta_{21}.

In that case, we call this map the composition of homotopies (of
\eta_{32}
and
\eta_{21}
).

The composition of homotopies of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is well-defined (and is equal to either side of the above formula). This composition actually defines a structure of Lie category on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
,
24
24

24

More precisely,
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
is a bundle of Lie monoids.
and
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
form precisely the invertible elements of this Lie category. Notice that the structure of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
as a bundle of Lie groups is independent of the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
.

Definition 3.8
.

Let

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}

be a
2
-term complex. We call the bundle of Lie groups

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})=\{h_{x}:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{x}\mid 1+\partial h_{x}\textnormal{ is invertible}\}

the
bundle of invertible homotopies
of the
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Remark 3.9
.

Although we write elements of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
as
h_{x}
, its product shows that a good way to think of the elements
h_{x}
is as
1+h_{x}
. This shows that the product of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is determined by a structure of Lie category on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
:

h_{x}\cdot h_{x}^{\prime}\coloneq h_{x}\partial h_{x}^{\prime}.

This product defines a Lie category, and we will elaborate on its structure in Section
3.5
.

That the elements of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
give rise to isomorphisms of the complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
can be interpreted as a cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
:

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(1+h_{x}\partial,1+\partial h_{x}).

This canonical representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
plays a central role in this work.

Before we move on, notice that elements
h_{x}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
formally have an inverse:

(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}=\textstyle\sum_{k\geq 0}(-1)^{k}(\partial h_{x})^{k}.

In particular, if
\partial h_{x}
is nilpotent, i.e.
(\partial h_{x})^{k}=0
for some
k
, then
1+\partial h_{x}
has a smooth inverse.

Remark 3.10
.

With cohomological perturbation theory in mind (see, for example,
[
23
,
40
,
48
]
and references therein), we can think of
\partial
as a perturbation of the zero differential. The identity map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

can then be perturbed, using
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, to an isomorphism
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

In fact, we can view
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
as a level set (of a section) of the canonical projection

\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

where

\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})=\{(\partial,h)\in\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\mid 1+\partial h\textnormal{ is invertible}\}.

We can also view
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
as a bundle of Lie groups, with the same product as we defined for
\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})
. This groupoid structure becomes very relevant in Section
5
.

Heuristically, a “small” perturbation
\partial
comes with fewer restrictions on
h_{x}
, while a “big” perturbation imposes more restrictions on
h_{x}
. For example, if
\partial=0
, then
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})=\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
. But if
\partial
is of full rank, then
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is determined by isomorphisms
\varphi_{x}
and left or right inverses of
\partial
.

The last observation of the above remark can be made much more precise as follows.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathbb{R}^{k}\xrightarrow{d}\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
be a linear map. The Lie group

\textnormal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\mathbb{R}^{k})=\{h:\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{k}\mid 1+dh\textnormal{ is invertible}\}

embeds into
\textnormal{GL}(k+\ell)
via

\textnormal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\mathbb{R}^{k})\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(k+\ell)\qquad h\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}1&h\\
0&1+dh\end{pmatrix}.

In fact, if
r=\rank d
, then
\textnormal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\mathbb{R}^{k})
is isomorphic to the semidirect product associated to the action of
\textnormal{GL}(r)
on upper diagonal blockmatrices
25
25

25

The non-trivial blocks are of the form
(k-r)\times r
,
(k-r)\times(\ell-r)
and
r\times(\ell-r)
.
given by

g\cdot\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{1}&h_{2}\\
0&1&h_{3}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{1}g^{-1}&h_{2}\\
0&1&gh_{3}\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

In particular, if
C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}
is a
2
-term complex, then
\partial
is regular if and only if
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is a Lie group bundle.
26
26

26

A Lie group bundle is a locally trivial bundle of Lie groups.

Proof.

The first statement is readily verified. To prove the second statement, we conjugate
d
so that
d:\mathbb{R}^{r}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{k-r}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{r}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\ell-r}
is in the canonical form
(x,y)\mapsto(x,0)
.
27
27

27

If we conjugate
d
to
AdB
, then the two models of
\textnormal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{\ell},\mathbb{R}^{k})
are isomorphic via
h\mapsto B^{-1}hA^{-1}
.
The subgroup of
\textnormal{GL}(k+\ell)
corresponding to
\textnormal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
consists now of invertible matrices of the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&h_{4}&h_{3}\\
0&1&h_{1}&h_{2}\\
0&0&1+h_{4}&h_{3}\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.

Notice that
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
and
h_{3}
can be taken arbitrary. The constraint on
h_{4}
is that
1+h_{4}
is invertible. Now, the map that sends a matrix as above to
1+h_{4}
is a Lie group homomorphism onto
\textnormal{GL}(r)
that is a surjective submersion. Its kernel consist of elements of the form

\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&h_{3}\\
0&1&h_{1}&h_{2}\\
0&0&1&h_{3}\\
0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix},

and this group is isomorphic to the group of upper triangular blockmatrices (by eliminating, from such elements, the first row and column). A direct computation shows that the (conjugation) action that defines the resulting semidirect product is given as in the statement.

The last statement follows by the constant rank theorem: if
\partial
is regular, then local frames adapted to
\partial
trivialise
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
locally.
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Notice that, if
\partial
is regular, we can split (non-canonically)

V_{M}\cong\textnormal{coker }\partial\oplus\im\partial\qquad C_{M}\cong\ker\partial\oplus\im\partial.

Therefore, there is a global non-canonical isomorphism between
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
and a type of semidirect product as in the above statement. Indeed, we can form a semidirect product (of Lie group bundles) of the isotropy Lie group bundle of
\textnormal{GL}(\im\partial)
, and the Lie group bundle of upper triangular blockmatrices, where now

h_{1}\in\textnormal{Hom}(\im\partial,\ker\partial)\qquad h_{2}\in\textnormal{Hom}(\textnormal{coker }\partial,\ker\partial)\qquad h_{3}\in\textnormal{Hom}(\textnormal{coker }\partial,\im\partial).

In general, given a
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, we can represent elements of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ni h_{x}
as blockmatrices

\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{x}\\
0&1+\partial h_{x}\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M}).

Another way is to use instead

\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{x}\partial&h_{x}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M})

which is related to the previous matrix as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{x}\partial&h_{x}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1&h_{x}\\
0&1+\partial h_{x}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{x}\partial&h_{x}\partial h_{x}(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}\\
0&(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}.

Similarly, we can depict elements of
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
, but such elements “carry a differential”.

Under the identification of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
with
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})
(by taking the dual), the structure of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
takes its “opposite” form:

h_{x}\cdot_{\textnormal{op}}h_{x}^{\prime}\coloneq h_{x}^{\prime}\cdot h_{x}=h_{x}+h_{x}^{\prime}+h_{x}^{\prime}\partial h_{x}\qquad(h_{x})_{\textnormal{op}}^{-1}\coloneq h_{x}^{-1}=-h_{x}(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}.

As happens for groups, bundles of groups are isomorphic to their opposites via the inverse:

Proposition 3.13
.

Let
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
be a
2
-term complex. The canonical map

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(h_{x}^{-1})^{*}

is an isomorphism of bundle of Lie groups.

In the coming section, we define the well-known canonical cochain complex representation of the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
. This cochain complex representation is compatible with the exact sequence of Lie groupoids
28
28

28

The short exact sequence can be seen as a restriction of the short exact sequence of Lie categories

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

in two ways. One is that the inclusion
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}
is equivariant with respect to the canonical cochain complex representations. Another is that the conjugation action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is induced by the representation on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
. This is precisely what encodes the fat extension associated to
V_{G}
(see the next section and Section
4
).

3.3.
Canonical representations of the fat groupoid

The action of
V_{G}
on itself induces an action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
V_{G}
: given
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
and
v\in(V_{G})_{g^{\prime}}
with
(g,g^{\prime})\in G^{(2)}
,

H_{g}\cdot v\coloneq H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}v)\cdot v.

In turn, this induces actions of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
: given
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
,
c\in(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
and
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

(3.1)

H_{g}\cdot c\coloneq r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}(\partial c)\cdot c)\qquad H_{g}\cdot v\coloneq\mathbf{t}_{g}(H_{g}v).

Remark 3.14
.

More generally, a linear action of
V_{G}
on a vector bundle
V_{P}\rightarrow P
induces an action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
V_{P}
. By linear, we mean that the action map

V_{G}\ltimes V_{P}\rightarrow V_{P}

is linear (over an action of
G
on
P
). A more thorough discussion (including a discussion on VB-Morita equivalences in terms of bibundles, the notion of Morita equivalence in terms of ruths, and the notion of Morita equivalence in terms of fat extensions) will appear in
[
44
]
.

It is readily verified that the
2
-term complex

\partial:C_{M}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}}V_{M}

is equivariant with respect to the actions, so we can see
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
as a cochain complex representation of
\widehat{V}_{G}
. In turn, there is a dual cochain complex representation on
29
29

29

Recall that, with our convention,
V_{M}^{*}
sits in degree
0
and
C_{M}^{*}
sits in degree
1
.

\partial^{*}:V_{M}^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}^{*}}C_{M}^{*}

and the representations can also be put together to give a representation on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})=V_{M}^{*}\otimes C_{M}
. To be clear,
\widehat{V}_{G}
acts on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
via the representation of
C_{M}
and via the representation of
V_{M}
. Denoting these actions by
\cdot_{C_{M}}
and
\cdot_{V_{M}}
, respectively, the conjugation action is given by

H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}=H_{g}\cdot_{V_{M}}h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot_{C_{M}}H_{g}^{-1}.

When using this action, we use the notation from the right hand side to avoid confusion. This representation descends to an action on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\subset\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
.
30
30

30

For
h_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
and
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
, the map
\mathbf{t}_{g}(H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g})
is given by
(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})(1+\partial h_{\mathbf{s}g})
.

Proposition 3.15
.

The inclusion map

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad h_{x}\mapsto 1+h_{x}

is equivariant with respect to the canonical cochain complex representations on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Moreover, the conjugation action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, induced by the representation on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
, coincides with the conjugation action of
\widehat{V}_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
by viewing
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
as a normal subgroupoid of
\widehat{V}_{G}
.

Proof.

The first statement is readily verified. For the second statement we do a direct computation:

\displaystyle(H_{g}\cdot(1+h_{\mathbf{s}g})\cdot H_{g}^{-1})v

\displaystyle=H_{g}(1+\partial h_{\mathbf{s}g})(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v)\cdot(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v+h_{\mathbf{s}g}(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v))\cdot H_{g}^{-1}(v)

\displaystyle=H_{g}(1+\partial h_{\mathbf{s}g})(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v)\cdot(H_{g}^{-1}(v)+h_{\mathbf{s}g}(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v)\cdot 0_{g^{-1}})

\displaystyle=v+H_{g}(\partial h_{\mathbf{s}g}(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v))\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v)\cdot 0_{g^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(1+H_{g}\cdot_{V_{M}}h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot_{C_{M}}H_{g}^{-1})v.

This proves the statement.
∎

3.4.
Fat extensions

As we discussed now the structure behind the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid that makes it into a
fat extension
, we already put here the abstract definition:

Definition 3.16
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and consider a
2
-term complex

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}.

A fat extension of
G
, with underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, consists of a Lie groupoid

F_{G}\rightrightarrows M,

that comes with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids (over
M
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Moreover, the cochain complex representation of
F_{G}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, and the two natural conjugation actions of
F_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
agree. That is, denoting by
\cdot_{V_{M}}
and
\cdot_{C_{M}}
the actions of
F_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
induced by the representations, we have that, for all
H_{g}\in F_{G}
and
