h_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot H_{g}^{-1}=H_{g}\cdot_{V_{M}}h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot_{C_{M}}H_{g}^{-1}.

We will simply write
F_{G}
for a fat extension, and the underlying groupoid
F_{G}
is called the fat groupoid of the fat extension.

To understand the structure and examples of fat extensions, it is helpful to keep the reference with VB-groupoids, and so, for now, we study the above structure in terms of VB-groupoids.

3.5.
Fat category extensions

Before we move to the examples, we observe that
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
can be recovered from
\widehat{V}_{G}
and its cochain complex representation. In fact, this leads to yet another equivalent way to encode the structure of a fat extension in terms of a
fat category extension
. This defines the intrinsic notion of weak representation as defined in
[
62
]
.

To start, observe that
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\rightrightarrows M
is a Lie category with
\mathbf{s}h_{g}=\mathbf{s}g
,
\mathbf{t}h_{g}=\mathbf{t}g
and

h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}\coloneq h_{g}\partial h_{g^{\prime}}.

This is not a “VB-category” over
G
, because this product is not linear but bilinear. The fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
acts on the vector bundle
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})
, both on the left and on the right, via the two representations on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
. We denote these two actions by
\cdot_{C_{M}}
and
\cdot_{V_{M}}
for clarity. We then get a groupoid structure on

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightrightarrows M

by setting
\mathbf{s}(h_{g},H_{g})=\mathbf{s}g
,
\mathbf{t}(h_{g},H_{g})=\mathbf{t}g
and

(h_{g},H_{g})\cdot(h_{g^{\prime}},H_{g^{\prime}})\coloneq(h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}+H_{g}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{g^{\prime}}+h_{g}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g^{\prime}},H_{g}\cdot H_{g^{\prime}}).

The connection with
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
becomes clear when associating to
(h_{g},H_{g})
the map

r_{g}h_{g}+H_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\qquad v\mapsto r_{g}h_{g}v+H_{g}v

which satisfies
\mathbf{s}_{g}(r_{g}h_{g}+H_{g})=1
.

Proposition 3.17
.

The map

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\qquad(h_{g},H_{g})\mapsto r_{g}h_{g}+H_{g}

is a surjective submersion of Lie categories. Moreover, the embedding

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(-h_{x},1+h_{x})

defines free and proper left and right actions whose quotients are both given by
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
, and the above map defines both quotient maps.

Proof.

To see that the first map is a surjective submersion follows, for example, by constructing a local section from a local section
H
of
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
:

\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad H_{g}^{\prime}\mapsto(r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}^{\prime}-H_{g}),H_{g}).

The second statement follows by realising that, whenever

r_{g}h_{g}+H_{g}=r_{g}h_{g}^{\prime}+H_{g}^{\prime}

there is a unique element
h_{\mathbf{t}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
such that

(-h_{\mathbf{t}g},1+h_{\mathbf{t}g})\cdot(h_{g},H_{g})=(h_{g}^{\prime},H_{g}^{\prime}),

namely, the unique element
h_{\mathbf{t}g}
such that
(1+h_{\mathbf{t}g})\cdot H_{g}=H_{g}^{\prime}
(and similarly for the right action). This proves the statement.
∎

Remark 3.18
.

The above statement describes an “exact sequence of Lie categories”
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Namely, while it is true that
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is the kernel, in the sense that it comprises the elements mapping to units in
\widehat{V}_{G}
, it is the kernel in a more general, more appropriate sense. Being more precise here leads to the above statement: whenever two elements coincide in the image of the map

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}

they are related under the equivalence obtained from the inclusion by
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. That is, the action map from
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\times_{M}(\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G})
to

(\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G})\times_{\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}}(\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G})

is an isomorphism (and similarly for the right action), meaning that the action is free and proper.

Remark 3.19
.

From the previous statement, or rather its proof, we saw that
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
is a quotient with respect to the (left) action of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
on
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}
given by

h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot(h_{g},H_{g})\coloneq(h_{g}-h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot H_{g},(1+h_{\mathbf{t}g})\cdot H_{g}).

Remark 3.20
.

The Lie category
\widehat{V}_{G}
also has
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
as a cochain complex representation. By representation we mean here a smooth functor to the endomorphism Lie category

\textnormal{End }E_{M}\coloneq\{E_{x}\rightarrow E_{y}\mid x,y\in M\}

where
E_{M}
is a vector bundle. It is defined exactly as in (
3.1
).

The above discussion shows that the fat category of a fat extension can be defined as follows:

Definition 3.21
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension. Consider the Lie category

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}F_{G}\rightrightarrows M

The fat category
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightrightarrows M
is defined as the quotient of

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}F_{G}

by the action induced by the inclusion
31
31

31

Notice that here we identify
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
with its image in
F_{G}
. If
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
, then this element equals
1+h_{x}
.

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}F_{G}\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(-h_{x},h_{x}).

Now, apart from the cochain complex representation, the fat category
\widehat{V}_{G}
comes with a type of “comparison” map

h:\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\qquad(\Sigma_{g},\Sigma_{g}^{\prime})\mapsto r_{g^{-1}}(\Sigma_{g}^{\prime}-\Sigma_{g})

that is equivariant for both
\widehat{V}_{G}
actions (considering the diagonal action on
\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}
). It can be interpreted as a homotopy, for, given
H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}\in F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
,
c\in(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
and
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
, we have

h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})\partial c=H_{g}\cdot c-H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot c\qquad\partial h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})v=H_{g}\cdot v-H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot v.

Moreover, the map
h
restricts to the “canonical” comparison map of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
, and it restricts to the zero map on the diagonal embedding

\Delta:\widehat{V}_{G}\hookrightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}.

This defines the structure of a fat category extension:

Definition 3.22
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and consider a
2
-term complex

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}.

A
fat category extension
of
G
, with underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, consists of a Lie category

F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightrightarrows M,

that comes with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, a short exact sequence of Lie categories (over
M
)
32
32

32

Recall that this means that
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
defines a free and proper action on
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

and a smooth map

h:F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\times_{G}F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}).

The cochain complex representation of
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
. Moreover,
h
restricts to the canonical map
h
of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
and we have
h\Delta=0
. Lastly,
h
is multiplicative, meaning that, for all
H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}\in F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
,
c\in(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
and
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})\partial c=H_{g}\cdot c-H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot c\qquad\partial h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})v=H_{g}\cdot v-H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot v

and it is equivariant with respect to the canonical left/right actions of
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
.

Remark 3.23
.

The above definition can be seen to define the intrinsic object behind a “weak representation” of
[
62
]
. Namely, as was already observed in
[
33
]
, a splitting
\Sigma
of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
can be seen as a map

G\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}

(that is not necessarily a functor). Therefore, such a map should be seen as “splitting” a fat category extension. This leads precisely to the axioms of a weak representation as in
[
62
]
. There, it is proved that the category of weak representations is equivalent to the category of (split)
2
-term ruths and to the category of VB-groupoids.

Remark 3.24
.

As we will see in Section
7
, the comparison map

h:F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\times_{G}F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{s}^{*}C_{M})

from above can be seen as the “identity map” of the fat category extensions
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
.

From the discussion in terms of VB-groupoids it is already clear that we can pass from a fat category extension
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
to
F_{G}
by taking the invertible elements of
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
. Indeed, the properties of the map
h
imply that, for all
H_{g}\in F_{G}
and
h_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot H_{g}^{-1}=h(H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot H_{g}^{-1},0_{\mathbf{s}g})=H_{g}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{\mathbf{s}g}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g}^{-1}.

So, to end this section, we conclude:

Proposition 3.25
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{Fat category extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\mapsto F_{G}

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat category extensions}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}

is inverse to it (up to canonical isomorphisms).

We now move to a more detailed discussion on examples of fat extensions.

3.6.
Examples of fat extensions

In this section we describe, for well-known constructions of VB-groupoids, the associated fat extensions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Naturally, we will see how to define the constructions on an abstract level for fat extensions.

To start, recall that we can view
2
-term cochain complexes as VB-groupoids (via the Dold-Kan correspondence). The fat extension associated to this VB-groupoid keeps track of the cochain complex itself, but it also comes with a fat groupoid, namely, the bundle of invertible homotopies:

Example 3.26
.

Notice that
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, together with its canonical cochain complex representation
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, itself comes with a short exact sequence:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

where
1_{M}
denotes the unit groupoid of
M
. This exact sequence appears when considering

C_{M}\ltimes V_{M}\rightrightarrows V_{M}

as a VB-groupoid over
1_{M}
; here,
C_{M}
acts on
V_{M}
via the differential:

c\cdot v=\partial c+v.

Its underlying
2
-term complex is
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and its fat groupoid is
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. It is now readily verified that the associated exact sequence is the one written above.

Given a single vector bundle
E_{M}
, recall that there is a slightly different fat groupoid we can associate to it: the general linear groupoid (see
3.6
).

Example 3.27
.

In Example
3.6
we already mentioned that
\textnormal{GL }E_{M}
is the fat groupoid of the pair groupoid
E_{M}\times E_{M}\rightrightarrows E_{M}
. In this case,

\textnormal{H}(E_{M},E_{M})=\textnormal{Aut }E_{M}=\{\Psi_{x}:(E_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(E_{M})_{x}\}

and the representation on
E_{M}\xrightarrow{=}E_{M}
is the canonical one: given
\Psi_{y,x}\in\textnormal{GL }E_{M}
and
e\in(E_{M})_{x}
,

\Psi_{y,x}\cdot e=\Psi_{y,x}e.

Remark 3.28
.

As mentioned in Remark
2.17
, a differential
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
defines the map

[\cdot,\partial]:\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})=\textnormal{GL }C_{M}\times_{M\times M}\textnormal{GL }V_{M}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\textnormal{pr}_{1}^{*}C_{M},\textnormal{pr}_{2}^{*}V_{M}).

We can think of this map as a type of comparison map. In this case, the zeros of the above map are exactly the cochain maps between
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
and itself. In Section
7
, we will see that we should think of this as a map of fat extensions between
\textnormal{GL }C_{M}
and
\textnormal{GL }V_{M}
. On the level of VB-groupoids, this map corresponds to the VB-groupoid map

\partial\times\partial:C_{M}\times C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}\times V_{M}.

We now discuss the continuation of Example
3.5
. More precisely, we will come to see what a
fat subextension
and a
fat quotient extension
should be abstractly. The following bundles of Lie groups will play an important role.

Definition 3.29
.

Let
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
be a
2
-term complex, and
C_{N}\rightarrow V_{N}
a subcomplex. The
restricted bundle of invertible homotopies
is the subbundle of of Lie groups of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
given by

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}\coloneq\{h_{y}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\mid y\in N,h_{y}|_{(V_{N})_{y}}\subset(C_{N})_{y}\}.

Example 3.30
.

Recall that, given a Lie groupoid
G
and a subgroupoid
H
,
J^{1}G
has the subgroupoid

J^{1}_{H}G=\{j^{1}_{y}\sigma\in J^{1}G\mid y\in N,\im\sigma|_{N}\subset H\}

which played a role in the relation between
J^{1}H
,
J^{1}G
and
J^{1}_{\mathcal{N}}(G,H)
(see Example
3.5
). In general, given a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
and a VB-subgroupoid
V_{H}
over
C_{N}\rightarrow V_{N}
,
\widehat{V}_{G}
has

\widehat{V}_{G,H}\coloneq\{H_{h}\in\widehat{V}_{G}\mid h\in H,\im H_{h}|_{(V_{N})_{\mathbf{s}h}}\subset(V_{H})_{h}\}

as a subgroupoid. It fits into an extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G,H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

and
\widehat{V}_{G,H}
comes with a cochain complex representation on

C_{M}|_{N}\rightarrow V_{M}|_{N}.

Moreover, the subcomplex
C_{N}\rightarrow V_{N}
is also a cochain complex representation of
\widehat{V}_{G,H}
, and both cochain complex representations restrict to
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}
. So, while not classifying as a fat extension,
\widehat{V}_{G,H}
carries at least a very similar structure. In turn, it induces two actual fat extensions: one recovers
\widehat{V}_{H}
as a quotient:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(C_{N},0_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G,H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

and one recovers
\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}
33
33

33

We wrote
V_{G}/V_{H}
for
V_{G}|_{H}/V_{H}
.
as a quotient:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{M}|_{N},C_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G,H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

To be clear, also the cochain complex representations of
\widehat{V}_{H}
and
\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}
are induced by the cochain complex representations of
\widehat{V}_{G,H}
.

From the previous example we can infer how to think of fat subextensions and fat quotient extensions abstractly using the following intermediate definition.

Definition 3.31
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Moreover, let
H\rightrightarrows N
be a Lie subgroupoid of
G
, and let
C_{N}\rightarrow V_{N}
be a subcomplex of
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. A
restricted fat extension
of
F_{G}
consists of a Lie subgroupoid

F_{G,H}\rightrightarrows N

of
F_{G}
that comes with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}|_{N}\rightarrow V_{M}|_{N}
, which further restricts to
C_{N}\rightarrow V_{N}
, and a short exact sequence
34
34

34

Again, we wrote

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}\coloneq\{h_{y}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\mid y\in N,\im h_{y}|_{(V_{N})_{y}}\subset(C_{N})_{y}\}.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G,H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Moreover, the cochain complex representation of
F_{G,H}
on
C_{M}|_{N}\rightarrow V_{M}|_{N}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},C_{N})}
.

We now define:

Definition 3.32
.

Given a restricted fat extension
F_{G,H}
of a fat extension
F_{G}
, we call
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{N},C_{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}/\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{N},0_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

the
fat subextension
of
F_{G,H}
, denoted
F_{H}
, and
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M}|_{N}/V_{N},C_{M}|_{N}/C_{N})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}/\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})_{(V_{M}|_{N},C_{N})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

the
fat quotient extension
of
F_{G,H}
, denoted
F_{G}/F_{H}
.

Remark 3.33
.

We can compare elements of
\widehat{V}_{H}
and
\widehat{V}_{G}
, and we can think of this as a map

\iota:\widehat{V}_{H}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{N},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\qquad(H_{h,1},H_{h,2})\mapsto r_{h^{-1}}(H_{h,1}-H_{h,2}).

We can also compare elements of
\widehat{V}_{G}
and
\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}
in a similar way

\pi:\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}|_{N}/C_{N}).

Given
H_{h,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,H}
, there is a unique
H_{h,1}\in\widehat{V}_{H}
such that

\iota(H_{h,1},H_{h,2})=0,

and, similarly, given
H_{h,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,H}
, there is a unique
H_{h,2}\in\widehat{V_{G}/V_{H}}
such that

\pi(H_{h,1},H_{h,2})=0.

The map
\iota
should be interpreted as the “inclusion” map and
\pi
as the “projection” map. These are examples of maps of fat extensions that we will discuss in Section
7
.

In the the next example we explain how the fiber product of VB-groupoids looks like in terms of fat extensions. Particular examples of interest are the pullback and direct sum constructions, but we first discuss the more general case.

Example 3.34
.

Let

G_{1}\rightarrow G\leftarrow G_{2}

be Lie groupoid maps that cleanly intersect, and let
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
be VB-groupoids over
G_{1}
and
G_{2}
, respectively. Then we can form the fiber product of VB-groupoids (see
[
13
]
):

V_{G_{1}}\times_{G}V_{G_{2}}\rightrightarrows V_{M_{1}}\times_{M}V_{M_{2}}.

This is a VB-groupoid over
G_{1}\times_{G}G_{2}\rightrightarrows M_{1}\times_{M}M_{2}
whose structure is defined “componentwise”. To understand how the fat groupoid of this VB-groupoid looks like, notice that an element of it can be decomposed into four maps that we organise into a matrix as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&r_{g_{1}}h_{12}\\
r_{g_{2}}h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}:(V_{M_{1}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\times(V_{M_{2}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{2}}\rightarrow(V_{G_{1}})_{g_{1}}\times(V_{G_{2}})_{g_{2}}.

Here,
H_{g_{1}}\in\widehat{V}_{G_{1}}^{\textnormal{cat}}
,
H_{g_{2}}\in\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}^{\textnormal{cat}}
and

h_{12}\in\textnormal{Hom}((V_{M_{2}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{2}},(C_{M_{1}})_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}})\qquad h_{21}\in\textnormal{Hom}((V_{M_{1}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}},(C_{M_{2}})_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}}).

In fact, without extra conditions, such matrices describe exactly

(V_{G_{1}}\widehat{\times}_{G}V_{G_{2}})^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightrightarrows M_{1}\times_{M}M_{2}.

When interpreted in a specific way, the product of this Lie category can really be seen as matrix multiplication:

\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}&h_{12}^{\prime}\\
h_{21}^{\prime}&H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}+h_{12}\cdot h_{21}^{\prime}&H_{g_{1}}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{12}^{\prime}+h_{12}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\\
h_{21}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{21}^{\prime}&h_{21}\cdot h_{12}^{\prime}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

We used here the notion from Section
3.5
, but it is also readily verified by a direct computation what the different products are supposed to represent. We obtain the invertible elements of this category, the elements of the fat groupoid
V_{G_{1}}\times_{G}V_{G_{2}}
, by applying the target to each entry and asking the result to be invertible:

\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{t}_{g_{1}}H_{g_{1}}&\partial h_{12}\\
\partial h_{21}&\mathbf{t}_{g_{2}}H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}:(V_{M_{1}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\times(V_{M_{2}})_{\mathbf{s}g_{2}}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{M_{1}})_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\times(V_{M_{2}})_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}}.

Notice that

\textnormal{H}(V_{M_{1}}\times_{M}V_{M_{2}},C_{M_{1}}\times_{M}C_{M_{2}})=\{\begin{pmatrix}h_{1}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&h_{2}\end{pmatrix}\mid\begin{pmatrix}1+\partial h_{1}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&1+\partial h_{2}\end{pmatrix}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}

where

h_{1}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M_{1}},C_{M_{1}})\quad h_{12}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M_{2}},C_{M_{1}})\quad h_{21}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M_{1}},C_{M_{2}})\quad h_{2}\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M_{2}},C_{M_{2}}).

The diagonal blockmatrices (those for which
h_{12}
and
h_{21}
are both zero) form the fiber product of the fat groupoids of
V_{G_{1}}
and
V_{G_{2}}
:

\widehat{V}_{G_{1}}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}\rightrightarrows M_{1}\times_{M}M_{2}

so the structure is defined componentwise. This Lie groupoid fits into an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M_{1}},C_{M_{1}})\times_{M}\textnormal{H}(V_{M_{2}},C_{M_{2}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G_{1}}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{1}\times_{G}G_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

We continue this line of thought in the next example.

As an application of the previous example, we describe the fat groupoid of a direct sum of VB-groupoids and of a pullback VB-groupoid.

Example 3.35
.

To describe the direct sum, suppose we are given two VB-groupoids
V_{G,1}\rightrightarrows V_{M,1}
and
V_{G,2}\rightrightarrows V_{M,2}
over the same base groupoid
G
. Then the fat groupoid of

V_{G,1}\oplus V_{G,2}\rightrightarrows V_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,2}

consist of matrices

\begin{pmatrix}H_{g,1}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g,2}\end{pmatrix}:(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G,1})_{g}\oplus(V_{G,2})_{g}.

Here,
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}^{\textnormal{cat}}
,
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}^{\textnormal{cat}}
and

h_{12}\in\textnormal{Hom}((V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g},(C_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g})\qquad h_{21}\in\textnormal{Hom}((V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g},(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}).

The diagonal blockmatrices constitute the fiber product of fat groupoids
\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}
. This groupoid fits into an exact sequence of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M,1},C_{M,1})\times_{M}\textnormal{H}(V_{M,2},C_{M,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

This groupoid has four natural
3
-term cochain complex representations by taking tensor products. For example, on
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},V_{M,2})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},C_{M,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},V_{M,2}).}

The actions are defined “componentwise”. Essentially,
\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}
together with its representations on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2})
and on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,2},C_{M,1})
are what constitutes the product of the upper/lower triangular blockmatrices.

From
[
14
]
we know that multiplicative tensors define homogeneous cochains on Whitney sums of VB-groupoids. In the upcoming work
[
50
]
we will argue that multiplicative tensors on VB-groupoids
V_{G,1},\dots,V_{G,n}
appear naturally as objects defined on
\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\dots\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,n}
together with its canonical representations. A first instance of this can be found in Section
7
where we introduce morphisms of fat extensions.

Example 3.36
.

To describe the pullback, let
V_{G_{2}}\rightrightarrows V_{M_{2}}
, be a VB-groupoid over
G_{2}
and
\Phi:G_{1}\rightarrow G_{2}
a groupoid map. The fat groupoid of the pullback VB-groupoid
\Phi^{*}V_{G_{2}}
is

\Phi^{*}\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}=G_{1}\times_{G_{2}}\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}\rightrightarrows M_{1}

where the multiplication of
\Phi^{*}\widehat{V}_{G_{2}}
is defined componentwise. If
\Phi
is the inclusion of a Lie subgroupoid
H
, we simply write

\widehat{V}_{G}|_{H}=\{H_{h}\in\widehat{V}_{G}\mid h\in H\}.

To conclude, we formulate the abstract definition of the above examples for fat extensions.

Definition 3.37
.

Let
F_{G_{1}}
and
F_{G_{2}}
be fat extensions of
G_{1}\rightrightarrows M_{1}
and
G_{2}\rightrightarrows M_{2}
, over
C_{M_{1}}\rightarrow V_{M_{1}}
and
C_{M_{2}}\rightarrow V_{M_{2}}
, respectively, and let

G_{1}\rightarrow G\leftarrow G_{2}

be two Lie groupoid maps that cleanly intersect. The
fat extensions of invertible blockmatrices
of
F_{G_{1}}
and
F_{G_{2}}
is the following fat extension. The fat groupoid form the invertible elements of the following fat category:

\{\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\mid H_{g_{1}}\in F_{G_{1}}^{\textnormal{cat}},h_{12}\in\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M_{2}},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M_{1}}),h_{21}\in\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M_{1}},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M_{2}}),H_{g_{2}}\in F_{G_{2}}^{\textnormal{cat}}\}

whose product is defined as

\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}&h_{12}^{\prime}\\
h_{21}^{\prime}&H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}+h_{12}\cdot h_{21}^{\prime}&H_{g_{1}}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{12}^{\prime}+h_{12}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\\
h_{21}\cdot_{V_{M}}H_{g_{1}^{\prime}}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot_{C_{M}}h_{21}^{\prime}&h_{21}\cdot h_{12}^{\prime}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot H_{g_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

Here,
h_{12}\cdot h_{21}^{\prime}=h_{12}\partial h_{21}^{\prime}
,
h_{21}\cdot h_{12}^{\prime}=h_{21}\partial h_{12}^{\prime}
, and
\cdot_{V_{M}}
and
\cdot_{C_{M}}
are the actions induced by the representations. The cochain complex representation on

\partial\times\partial:C_{M_{1}}\times_{M}C_{M_{2}}\rightarrow V_{M_{1}}\times_{M}V_{M_{2}}

is given by matrix application: given
(c_{1},c_{2})\in(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\times(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{2}}
and
(v_{1},v_{2})\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\times(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{2}}
,

\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}c_{1}\\
c_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}\cdot c_{1}+h_{12}\partial c_{2}\\
h_{21}\partial c_{1}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot c_{2}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&H_{g_{2}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}H_{g_{1}}\cdot v_{1}+\partial h_{12}v_{2}\\
\partial h_{21}v_{1}+H_{g_{2}}\cdot v_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

In the last sections we make remarks on other models of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid. In particular, we will see that dualising a fat extension should be seen as dualising its cochain complex representation.

3.7.
Other models for the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid

The fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
appeared in
[
33
]
as follows. Consider

\widetilde{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}=\{\widetilde{H}_{g}\subset(V_{G})_{g}\mid(V_{G})_{g}=\ker\mathbf{s}_{g}\oplus\widetilde{H}_{g}\}

and the following open subset of it:

\widetilde{V}_{G}=\{\widetilde{H}_{g}\in\widetilde{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\mid(V_{G})_{g}=\ker\mathbf{t}_{g}\oplus\widetilde{H}_{g}\}.

Then
\widetilde{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
is a Lie category over
M
with
\widetilde{\mathbf{s}}\widetilde{H}_{g}=\mathbf{s}g
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{t}}\widetilde{H}_{g}=\mathbf{t}g
, and

\widetilde{H}_{g}\cdot\widetilde{H}_{h}=\im\mathbf{m}(\widetilde{H}_{g},\widetilde{H}_{h})=\{v_{g}\cdot v_{h}\in(V_{G})_{gh}\mid v_{g}\in\widetilde{H}_{g},v_{h}\in\widetilde{H}_{h}\},

and
\widetilde{V}_{G}
form the invertible elements with

(\widetilde{H}_{g})^{-1}=\im\mathbf{i}(\widetilde{H}_{g}).

It is rather straightforward to see that the canonical map

\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\xrightarrow{\im}\widetilde{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}

is an isomorphism of Lie categories (that therefore restricts to an isomorphism of
\widehat{V}_{G}
to
\widetilde{V}_{G}
). Still, we discuss the proof, because some interesting things appear. For example, it becomes clear that the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
of the dual VB-groupoid
V_{G}^{*}
is isomorphic to
\widehat{V}_{G}
as groupoids.

Notice that we can think of elements of
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
as (the dual of) maps

\omega_{g}:(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}

such that precomposing with
\ell_{g}
gives the identity map, and precomposing with
r_{g}
gives a linear isomorphism.
35
35

35

As before, we wrote

\ell_{g}:(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{-0_{g}\cdot c^{-1}}(V_{G})_{g}\qquad r_{g}:(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\xrightarrow{c\cdot 0_{g}}(V_{G})_{g}.

The product of two elements
\omega_{g}
and
\omega_{h}
is given by

(\omega_{g}\cdot\omega_{h})(v_{g}\cdot v_{h})=\omega_{h}(r_{h}\omega_{g}(v_{g}))+\omega_{h}(v_{h})

(here
(v_{g},v_{h})\in V_{G}^{(2)}
is a composable element) and the inverse of
\omega_{g}
is

\omega_{g}^{-1}(v_{g}^{-1})=-(\omega_{g}r_{g})^{-1}(\omega_{g}v_{g}).

Proposition 3.38
.

The maps

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widetilde{V}_{G}\leftarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto\im H_{g}=\textnormal{ker }\omega_{g}\textnormal{ \reflectbox{$\mapsto$} }\omega_{g}

are groupoid isomorphisms.

Proof.

We construct the inverse of the first map. To do this, take for every
g\in G
an
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
. Given
\widetilde{H}_{g}\in\widetilde{V}_{G}
, we can then write, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

H_{g,1}v=v_{\mathbf{s},1}+H_{g}v

where
v_{\mathbf{s},1}\in\ker\mathbf{s}_{g}
and
H_{g}v\in\widetilde{H}_{g}
. We claim that the map

\widetilde{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad\widetilde{H}_{g}\mapsto H_{g}

is a well-defined smooth inverse of the map
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widetilde{V}_{G}
. To see that it is well-defined, take another
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
and write as above, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

H_{g,2}v=v_{\mathbf{s},2}+H_{g}^{\prime}v.

Then it follows that

H_{g}^{\prime}v-H_{g}v\in\ker\mathbf{s}_{g}\cap\widetilde{H}_{g}=0.

A similar argument shows that
\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}
must be invertible: if
\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}v=0
, then

H_{g}v\in\ker\mathbf{t}_{g}\cap\widetilde{H}_{g}=0.

The map is smooth, for once we work locally, we can take
H_{g,1}
to be in the image of a local section of the projection
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
. For a proof that the maps are groupoid maps, we refer to the proof of Proposition
3.40
.
∎

Remark 3.39
.

Notice that, in the above proof, the auxiliary elements
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
that are used to define the inverse map
\widetilde{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}
can be taken in
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
as well. In particular, we could also use a cleavage
\Sigma
to define, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
, the inverse through the equation

\Sigma_{g}v=v_{\mathbf{s}}+H_{g}v

where
v_{\mathbf{s}}\in\ker s_{g}
and
H_{g}v\in\widetilde{H}_{g}
.

The isomorphism
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widetilde{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
can be understood through a pairing that we discuss next.

3.8.
The fat pairing

We first look at the isomorphism
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widetilde{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
more carefully.

Proposition 3.40
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid and consider
\widehat{V}_{G}
and
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
. For
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
, write

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}:(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\qquad v\mapsto r_{g}(H_{g}^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v-v^{-1}).

The map

\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}

is a Lie groupoid isomorphism. Moreover, the map restricts to the canonical isomorphism
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\cong\textnormal{H}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})
from Proposition
3.13
, so the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

commutes.

Proof.

Notice that
\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}
is the unique element
\omega_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
with the property that
\omega_{g}H_{g}=0
.
36
36

36

If
\omega_{g}^{\prime}
is another element of
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
such that
\omega_{g}^{\prime}H_{g}=0
, then
\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}=\omega_{g}^{\prime}
on
\im H_{g}
, but also on
\ker\mathbf{t}_{g}
, which are elements of the form
\ell_{g}c
.
To see that the map
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
is well-defined, observe that

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}r_{g}=r_{g}(H_{g}^{-1}(\partial c)+\ell_{g}(\partial c-c))=H_{g}^{-1}\cdot c

has inverse
c\mapsto H_{g}\cdot c
.

We compute the inverse map
\omega_{g}\mapsto H_{g}^{\omega_{g}}
explicitly. Pick for this some
H_{g}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
. Then
H_{g}^{\omega_{g}}
is the unique element
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
such that

H_{g}^{\prime}v=\ell_{g}\omega_{g}(H_{g}^{\prime}v)+H_{g}(v+\partial\omega_{g}(H_{g}^{\prime}v)).

In fact, given
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
, writing
h_{g}v\in(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}
for the inverse of
-\omega_{g}(H_{g}^{\prime}v)
under
\omega_{g}r_{g}
, we have

H_{g}^{\omega_{g}}v=H_{g}^{\prime}v+r_{g}h_{g}v.

We end the proof by showing that
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
is a groupoid map. For
H_{g},H_{g^{\prime}}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
composable,

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}\cdot\omega_{g^{\prime}}^{H_{g^{\prime}}}(v_{g}\cdot v_{g^{\prime}})=(H_{g^{\prime}}^{-1}((\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v_{g})+0_{(g^{\prime})^{-1}g^{-1}}\cdot(v_{g}-H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v_{g})-v_{g^{\prime}}^{-1})\cdot 0_{g^{\prime}},

while

\omega_{gg^{\prime}}^{H_{gg^{\prime}}}(v_{g}\cdot v_{g^{\prime}})=(H_{g^{\prime}}^{-1}H_{g}^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v_{g}-v_{g^{\prime}}^{-1}v_{g}^{-1})\cdot 0_{g}\cdot 0_{g^{\prime}}.

If we replace
0_{g}
with
\pm v_{g}\pm H_{g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v_{g}
in the last equation, we see that the two expressions agree. This proves the statement.
∎

Remark 3.41
.

Given
H_{g}
and
\omega_{g}=\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}
, we constructed in the above proof a map
h_{g}
using an auxiliary element
H_{g}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
:

h_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad v\mapsto r_{g^{-1}}(\ell_{g}+H_{g}\partial)\omega_{g}(H_{g}^{\prime}v)=r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}^{\prime}v-H_{g}v).

As we mentioned already in Remark
3.39
, we could take such auxiliary elements in
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
. Given a cleavage
\Sigma
, the equation

\Sigma_{g}v=-r_{g}h_{g}v+H_{g}v

reveals a new description of the fat groupoid in terms of the associated split ruth. This will be explained in detail in Section
4.3
.

We can understand this isomorphism from Proposition
3.40
more conceptually as follows.

Proposition 3.42
.

The smooth map

\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\qquad(\omega_{g},H_{g})\mapsto\omega_{g}H_{g}

is non-degenerate in the sense that, given
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
(resp.
\omega_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
) and
h_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, there is a unique element
\omega_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
(resp.
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
) such that
\omega_{g}H_{g}=h_{\mathbf{s}g}
.

Proof.

Let
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
and
h_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. If
h_{\mathbf{s}g}=0
, we saw in Proposition
3.40
that

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}v=r_{g}(H_{g}^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v-v^{-1})

is unique with the property that

\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}H_{g}=0.

Now, if
h_{\mathbf{s}g}\neq 0
, consider

\omega_{g}v=\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}v+h_{\mathbf{s}g}(\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g})^{-1}\mathbf{t}_{g}v.

Then
\omega_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
. Indeed, the inverse of precomposition with
r_{g}
is given by

c\mapsto r_{g^{-1}}(\ell_{g}c+H_{g}\partial c)+(h_{\mathbf{s}g}\partial c)^{-1}.

The element
\omega_{g}
is unique with the property that
\omega_{g}H_{g}=h_{\mathbf{s}g}
, so we proved the statement.
∎

Remark 3.43
.

Given
V_{G,1},\dots,V_{G,n}
VB-groupoids over
G
, we can see

\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\cdots\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,n}

as a groupoid over
M
(with structure defined componentwise; see Example
3.34
). However, with this groupoid structure, the above map
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is not a groupoid map (see the discussion after this remark).
Rather, this map should be interpreted as the identity map of fat extensions, and we can interpret the map as a multiplicative tensor if we view
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}
as a groupoid. That it maps into
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is a feature of the identity map being an isomorphism. We will be more precise in Section
7
, but a more general treatment of viewing multiplicative tensors this way is a topic of our upcoming work
[
50
]
.

Using
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}\cong\widehat{V}_{G}
, the fat pairing is simply the map

\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\qquad(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})\mapsto-1+H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot H_{g}^{-1}

that describes the inverse to the (principal) action map

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}.

Remark 3.44
.

Proposition
3.40
reveals that a map of fat extensions
F_{G,1}\rightarrow F_{G,2}
(over the same base groupoid
G
) is not simply a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M,1},C_{M,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M,2},C_{M,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Indeed,
\widehat{V}_{G}
and
\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
are fat extensions, and there is a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

In fact, we should not even expect that a map of fat extensions comes with a groupoid map
F_{G,1}\rightarrow F_{G,2}
(see Section
7
). Rather, maps of fat extensions are “comparison maps” like the ones we encountered in Section
3.6
.

What Proposition
3.40
shows is that the dual of a fat extension should be seen as having the same underlying extension, but considering the dual cochain complex representation:

Definition 3.45
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The dual of
F_{G}
is the extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

together with the dual cochain complex representation
V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}
.

4.
Fat extensions and equivalent formulations

Having encountered the structure of
fat extension
in the previous section, we proceed to show how to go back and forth between fat extensions, VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths. We dedicate a whole section to the correspondence with (general linear) PB-groupoids after this section.

We call the
2
-term ruth associated to a fat extension an
invariant complex
. This type of complex is a
relative complex
as in
[
56
]
. This new point of view leads to interesting connections between the “natural candidates” for tensor product of VB-groupoids and of
2
-term ruths. As already mentioned a few times, this is the topic of the upcoming work
[
50
]
. Defining the correct notion of morphism for the category fat extensions (that yields the claimed equivalence of categories between fat extensions and VB-groupoids or
2
-term ruths) is a little more subtle, but similar to how one defines morphisms of (split)
2
-term ruths. We delay the notion of morphism of fat extensions to Section
7
, where we also go into the functorial aspects of the equivalence we start describing in this section. The reason for the postponement is that we believe the discussion of this section should help understand the functorial aspects better.

4.1.
From VB-groupoids to fat extensions

In Section
3
, we developed the notion of fat extension through the language of VB-groupoids. For referential purposes and convenience, we repeat the definition here.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and consider a
2
-term complex

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}.

A fat extension of
G
, with underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, consists of a Lie groupoid

F_{G}\rightrightarrows M,

that comes with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids (over
M
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Moreover, the cochain complex representation of
F_{G}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
, and the two natural conjugation actions of
F_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
agree.

We will simply write
F_{G}
for a fat extension, and the underlying groupoid
F_{G}
is called the fat groupoid of the fat extension. Now, in Sections
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
, we proved:

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid over
G
. Then, the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
of
V_{G}
, together with its cochain complex representation (
3.1
), is a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Similarly, we have:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid over
G
. Then, the fat category
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
of
V_{G}
, together with its cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
and the (comparison) map

h:F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\times_{G}F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{s}^{*}C_{M})

is a fat category extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

4.2.
From fat extensions to VB-groupoids

In this section we prove how to recover the VB-groupoid from a fat extension. Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension and consider the VB-groupoid associated to the cochain complex representation
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. We denote this VB-groupoid by

F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C_{M}\times_{M}F_{G}\times_{M}V_{M}\rightrightarrows V_{M}.

The source and target maps are given by
\mathbf{s}(c,H_{g},v)=v
and
\mathbf{t}(c,H_{g},v)=\partial c+H_{g}\cdot v
, and

(c_{1},H_{g},\partial c_{2}+H_{h}\cdot v)\cdot(c_{2},H_{h},v)=(c_{1}+H_{g}\cdot c_{2},H_{g}\cdot H_{h},v).

Now, to recover a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
from its fat extension
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
, observe that an element
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
defines a linear isomorphism

r_{g}+H_{g}:(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}\qquad(c,v)\mapsto c\cdot 0_{g}+H_{g}v.

The inverse is given by
((\omega_{g}r_{g})^{-1}\omega_{g},\mathbf{s}_{g})
, where
\omega_{g}=\omega_{g}^{H_{g}}\in\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
is unique with the property that
\omega_{g}H_{g}=0
(see Proposition
3.40
). This observation leads to the following statement.

Proposition 4.4
.

The VB-groupoid map

\widehat{V}_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightarrow V_{G}\qquad(c,H_{g},v)\mapsto r_{g}c+H_{g}v

is surjective, and the associated exact sequence (of VB-groupoids) is given by
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

where
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
acts trivially on
V_{M}
, and the inclusion is given by
(h_{x},v)\mapsto(-h_{x}v,1+h_{x},v)
.

Now, given again an abstract fat extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

we obtain an injective map of VB-groupoids
37
37

37

As above,
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
acts trivially on
V_{M}
. Notice that now we identified
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
with its image in
F_{G}
; if
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
, this identifies
h_{x}
with its image
1+h_{x}
.

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}\rightarrow F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\qquad(h_{x},v)\mapsto(-h_{x}v,h_{x},v).

The quotient naturally inherits the structure of a VB-groupoid over
G
that we denote by
V(F_{G})
. The resulting short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(F_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

is a short exact sequence of VB-groupoids over the extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

In particular, the underlying cochain complex of
V(F_{G})
is given by
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. We will now show that VB-groupoids and fat extensions are in a one-to-one correspondence with each other. As we delayed the discussion on morphisms of fat extensions to Section
7
, we won’t go into the functorial aspects here. However, the notion of isomorphism of fat extensions necessarily appears.

Proposition 4.5
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad V_{G}\mapsto\widehat{V}_{G}

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto V(F_{G})

is inverse to it (up to canonical isomorphisms).

Proof.

That, given a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

V(\widehat{V}_{G})\xrightarrow{\sim}V_{G}

is a straightforward consequence of Proposition
4.4
.

Given a fat extension
F_{G}
, observe that the map

F_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}(F_{G})\qquad H_{g}\mapsto(v\mapsto[0,H_{g},v])

is a Lie groupoid map that intertwines the representations on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. In the remainder of the proof, we show that this map is invertible.
38
38

38

Therefore, we obtain an isomorphism of fat extensions; see Section
7
for more details.
Notice for this that elements of
\widehat{V}(F_{G})
can be represented by maps

(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\times(F_{G})_{g}\times(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\qquad v\mapsto(hv,H_{g}^{v},v)

such that
h
is linear, and

(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad v\mapsto\partial h(v)+H_{g}^{v}\cdot v

is a well-defined linear isomorphism. We claim that, for all elements of
\widehat{V}(F_{G})
, we can take
h=0
and
H_{g}^{v}=H_{g}^{0}
. Indeed, given a representative as above, write, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v}w=h((H_{g}^{v})^{-1}\cdot w)\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M}).

Then, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
,

(h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v},\partial h(v)+H_{g}^{v}\cdot v)\cdot(h(v),H_{g}^{v},v)=(0,h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v}\cdot H_{g}^{v},v),

so
v\mapsto(0,h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v}\cdot H_{g}^{v},v)
represents the same element as
v\mapsto(h(v),H_{g}^{v},v)
in
\widehat{V}(F_{G})
. Now, for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
, we can take the unique element
h_{\mathbf{s}g}^{v}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
such that

h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v}\cdot H_{g}^{v}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}^{v}=H_{g}^{0}.

Then

(0,h_{\mathbf{t}g}^{v}\cdot H_{g}^{v},v)\cdot(h_{\mathbf{s}g}^{v},v)=(0,H_{g}^{0},v),

which shows that,
v\mapsto(0,H_{g}^{0},v)
represents the same element as
v\mapsto(h(v),H_{g}^{v},v)
in
\widehat{V}(F_{G})
. This proves the claim, so the map
F_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}(F_{G})
is indeed invertible.
∎

This result can be seen as the essential surjectivity of functors that is described in Section
7
.

4.3.
From split
2
-term ruths to fat extensions

Given a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
, we observed in Remark
3.41
that we can construct an inverse to the canonical map
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{*}
using a cleavage
\Sigma
. The key ingredient we used was the map

h_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad h_{g}=r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}-\Sigma_{g})

that satisfies

\Sigma_{g}+r_{g}h_{g}=H_{g}.

This map
h_{g}
is a homotopy, showing that the quasi-action
R_{1}(g)
and the action by
H_{g}
define the same map in cohomology: for all
v\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
, we have

R_{1}(g)v+[\partial,h_{g}]v=H_{g}\cdot v.

Defining
H_{g}
out of
h_{g}
and
\Sigma
using the above formula, we obtain another description of
\widehat{V}_{G}
in terms of the split
2
-term ruth associated to
V_{G}
. It should be seen as the fat groupoid associated to a split
2
-term ruth:

Definition 4.6
.

The fat groupoid
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
of a split
2
-term ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
is the Lie groupoid

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}=\{h_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\mid\varphi_{g}=R_{1}(g)+\partial h_{g}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}

over
M
.

Notice that, given
h_{g}\in\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
, the map

\varphi_{g}=R_{1}(g)+[\partial,h_{g}]

is a cochain map that is an isomorphism: it defines the cochain complex representation.
39
39

39

That
R_{1}(g)+h_{g}\partial
is an isomorphism as well follows from the five-lemma, for
\varphi_{g}
is homotopic to
R_{1}(g)
. Indeed,
\varphi_{g}
is therefore a quasi-isomorphism, and since
R_{1}(g)+\partial h_{g}
is an isomorphism,
R_{1}(g)+h_{g}\partial
must be as well.
To clarify, the structure maps are defined again as composition of homotopies (see Lemma
3.7
): if
h_{g}
and
h_{g^{\prime}}
are composable (i.e.
g
and
g^{\prime}
are composable), then the homotopy
h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}
is such that

\varphi_{g}\varphi_{g^{\prime}}=R_{1}(gg^{\prime})+[\partial,h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}].

That is,

\displaystyle h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\coloneq R_{2}(g,g^{\prime})+h_{g}R_{1}(g^{\prime})+R_{1}(g)h_{g^{\prime}}+h_{g}\partial h_{g^{\prime}}

\displaystyle h_{g}^{-1}

\displaystyle\coloneq-(R_{2}(g^{-1},g)+R_{1}(g^{-1})h_{g})\varphi_{g}^{-1}=-\varphi_{g}^{-1}(R_{2}(g,g^{-1})+h_{g}R_{1}(g^{-1})).

Alternatively, we may think of
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
as the open set of invertible elements of the Lie category

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}=\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\rightrightarrows M

with product defined as above. We have an isomorphism of Lie categories

h_{\Sigma}:\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}-\Sigma_{g})

and this isomorphism restricts to an isomorphism
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}
(see Remark
4.8
and Section
6.2
).

Remark 4.7
.

Thinking of
R=(\partial=R_{0},R_{1},R_{2})
as a Maurer-Cartan element (see Remark
2.11
), we can perhaps think of the above product on
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})
as a “twisting” of the trivial product

h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}=0

by
R
. The product

h_{g}\cdot h_{g^{\prime}}\coloneq h_{g}\partial h_{g^{\prime}}

we used in Section
3.5
can also be seen this way by taking
R=(\partial,0,0)
, but notice that this is not a unital ruth.

Remark 4.8
.

The map
h_{\Sigma}:\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})
we constructed above is “invariant”: given
H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
, we have

h_{\Sigma}(H_{g})-h_{\Sigma}(H_{g}^{\prime})=-h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})

where

h:\widehat{V}_{G}\times\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\qquad h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})\coloneq r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}^{\prime}-H_{g}).

Conversely,
\Sigma
can be recovered as

\Sigma_{g}=-r_{g}h_{\Sigma}(H_{g})+H_{g}

where
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
is arbitrary. That
\Sigma
is unital translates into
h_{\Sigma}(1+h_{x})=h_{x}
for all
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. We can therefore think abstractly of splittings of fat extensions as being invariant maps as above. In Section
6.2
we put some more details about these
fat splittings
.

4.4.
From fat extensions to
2
-term ruths

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Recall that we can construct an exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(F_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

of VB-groupoids, where we view
V_{M}
as a representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
trivially.

Definition 4.9
.

Given a short exact sequence of groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

over
M
,
40
40

40

All three groupoids are groupoids over
M
and the maps in the exact sequence are over the identity maps.
the subcomplex
41
41

41

The condition should be satisfied for all
(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\in G^{(\bullet)}
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{\bullet+1}\in K
with
\mathbf{s}g_{\ell-1}=\mathbf{s}k_{\ell}=\mathbf{t}g_{\ell}
.

C^{\bullet}_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G=\{f\in C^{\bullet}G\mid f(k_{1}g_{1}k_{2}^{-1},\dots,k_{\bullet}g_{\bullet}k_{\bullet+1}^{-1})=f(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})\}

of
CG
is called the
K
-relative complex
.

The relevance of this complex, that was introduced in general in
[
56
]
, becomes clear from the following characterisation.

Proposition 4.10
.

Given a short exact sequence of groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

over
M
, the induced pullback map

\Phi^{*}:CH\rightarrow CG

is an isomorphism onto
C_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G
.

Proof.

To see that
\Phi^{*}
maps into
C_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G
is readily verified by direct computation. The inverse is given by choosing a set-theoretic section
\sigma
of
G\rightarrow H
(over the identity map) and setting

C^{\bullet}_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G\rightarrow C^{\bullet}H\qquad f\mapsto((h_{1},\dots,h_{\bullet})\mapsto f(\sigma h_{1},\dots,\sigma h_{\bullet})).

The map
(h_{1},\dots,h_{\bullet})\mapsto f(\sigma h_{1},\dots,\sigma h_{\bullet})
is indeed smooth, for locally (in
H^{(\bullet)}
) we can replace
\sigma
in the
\ell
-th component with a smooth section
\sigma_{\ell}
.
∎

Returning to the setup where
F_{G}
is a fat extension, we write

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})=C_{(\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M})\textnormal{-rel}}(F_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}).

(with respect to the short exact sequence of VB-groupoids above) and we call its elements
invariant
. The reason we passed to the dual cochain complex representation is because that corresponds to the VB-complex of the VB-groupoid
F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
. Similarly, we write

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C_{(\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes C_{M}^{*})\textnormal{-rel}}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}).

As a corollary of Proposition
4.10
, we obtain:

Corollary 4.11
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Then the pullback induces an isomorphism of differential graded
CG
-modules:

C_{\textnormal{VB}}V(F_{G})^{*}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}).

To make the invariance condition more concrete, thereby also explaining the terminology, notice that the left action of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}
on
F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
is given by

(h_{\mathbf{t}g},\partial c+H_{g}\cdot v)\cdot(c,H_{g},v)=(h_{\mathbf{t}g}(H_{g}\cdot v)+c,h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot H_{g},v)

and the right action by

(4.1)

(c,H_{g},v)\cdot(h_{\mathbf{s}g},v)=(c-H_{g}\cdot(h_{\mathbf{s}g}v),H_{g}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g},v).

Working out the condition of being invariant using these explicit expressions, we obtain:

Proposition 4.12
.

The invariant cochains in
C^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
are those pairs

(f_{0},f_{1})\in C^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(F_{G};V_{M})

for which

(4.2)

f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})

and

(4.3)

f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})-f_{0}(H_{g_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})=h_{\mathbf{t}g}(H_{g,1}\cdot f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})),

where
h_{\mathbf{t}g}
is the unique element in
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
such that
h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot H_{g,1}=H_{g,1}^{\prime}
in
F_{G}
.

Proof.

Explicitly, the identification

C(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\textnormal{VB}}(F_{G}\ltimes(V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}))\qquad(f_{0},f_{1})\mapsto f

is given by

f((f_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}^{V_{M}},H_{g_{1}},f_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}^{C_{M}}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}^{C_{M}}(H_{g_{1}}^{-1}\cdot f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{n}}))-f_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}^{V_{M}}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})).

Now, the elements
f\in C_{\textnormal{VB}}(F_{G}\ltimes(V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}))
that are inside the relative complex with respect to the sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes C_{M}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}\ltimes(V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V(F_{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

are those for which

f((f_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}^{V_{M}},H_{g_{1}},f_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}^{C_{M}}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f((f_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}^{C_{M}}(H_{g_{1}}^{-1}\cdot h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}})+f_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}^{V_{M}},h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}},f_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}^{C_{M}}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

Therefore,
f_{1}
is independent of its entries:

f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})=f(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

and

\displaystyle f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})

\displaystyle=f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=f_{0}(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})-h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})).

Taking
h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}
such that
h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}}=H_{g_{1}}^{\prime}
, we obtain the desired result.
∎

Notice that the dual result is that

C_{\textnormal{VB}}V(F_{G})\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}),

but this isomorphism can be obtained as the dual of the above isomorphism.

Remark 4.13
.

Given
(f_{0},f_{1})
satisfying the conditions from Proposition
4.17
, we often write

f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})\qquad f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})

as above. We can write the invariance condition also in the following way: for all
h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

f_{0}(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})+h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})).

Yet another way is that, for all
h_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

f_{0}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})+H_{g_{1}}\cdot(h_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})).

If
C_{M}\neq 0
, then invariant cochains
(f_{0},f_{1})
are determined by the element
f_{0}\in C(F_{G};C_{M})
. For this reason, we usually write
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
as the subcomplex

C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M})=\{f_{0}\in C^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M})\mid\exists f_{1}\in C^{\bullet-1}(F_{G};V_{M})\textnormal{ such that }\eqref{eq: fat f_V_M relation to f_C_M 0}\textnormal{ and }\eqref{eq: fat f_V_M relation to f_C_M 1}\textnormal{ hold}\}

of
C(F_{G};C_{M})
. This is done mostly for notational convenience, so usually
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
is meant when
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M})
is written.

For clarity, we arrived at the following definition for abstract fat extensions:

Definition 4.14
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The
invariant complex

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})\subset C(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})

consists of pairs
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
such that, for all
h_{\mathbf{t}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,
42
42

42

Implicit from the notation is that
f_{0}
does not depend on the last
\bullet-1
entries and
f_{1}
is independent of its entries.

f_{0}(h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})+h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})).

Remark 4.15
.

If
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
for a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
, and
H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
,

h_{\mathbf{t}g}(H_{g}\cdot v)=h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})=r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}^{\prime}v-H_{g}v)

where
h_{\mathbf{t}g}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is the map

h_{\mathbf{t}g}=-1+H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot H_{g}^{-1}.

This can be verified by direct computation.

Remark 4.16
.

The invariant complex of a fat category extension is defined in exactly the same way as above.

For convenience of the reader, we write the VB-complex of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
in terms of the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
(as the invariant complex) in the next section explicitly, and we prove this fact directly.

4.5.
From VB-groupoids to
2
-term ruths using the fat groupoid

From the previous section, we see that there is a model for
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
in terms of the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{G}
. As mentioned there, it is a subcomplex

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})\subset C(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

of invariant cochains, but one can often view it as a subcomplex

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})\subset C(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})

(if
V_{M}
is not the zero bundle) which we sometimes use for notational convenience. We write here an explicit isomorphism between
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
and
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
.

Consider an element
f
of
C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
. Then we can construct elements

f_{0}\in C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})\textnormal{ and }f_{1}\in C^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}^{*})

by setting

\displaystyle f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})v

\displaystyle=f(H_{g_{1}}(\mathbf{t}H_{g_{1}})^{-1}v,g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})c

\displaystyle=f(-c^{-1},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

Notice the resemblance with the equations that appeared in the alternative proof of Proposition
2.12
in Section
2
.

Proposition 4.17
.

The map

\displaystyle C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}\rightarrow C(\widehat{V}_{G},V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*});\quad f\mapsto(f_{0},f_{1})

is a cochain map. Moreover, it maps bijectively onto
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G},V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
, i.e. the pairs
(f_{0},f_{1})
such that

(4.4)

f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})

and

(4.5)

f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})v-f_{0}(H_{g_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})v=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})h(H_{g_{1}}^{-1},(H_{g_{1}}^{\prime})^{-1})v

where

h(H_{g_{1}^{-1}},H_{g_{1}^{-1}}^{\prime}):(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}}

is given by

h(H_{g_{1}^{-1}},H_{g_{1}^{-1}}^{\prime})v=r_{g_{1}}(H_{g_{1}^{-1}}^{\prime}v-H_{g_{1}^{-1}}v).

Proof.

The map is a cochain map, for it can be seen as the pullback of the VB-groupoid map

(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rtimes\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow V_{G}\qquad(v,H_{g},c)\mapsto\ell_{g}c+H_{g}(\mathbf{t}H_{g})^{-1}v.

Notice that we view here
(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rtimes\widehat{V}_{G}
as a VB-groupoid with
\mathbf{s}(v,H_{g},c)=\partial c+H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v
,
\mathbf{t}(v,H_{g},c)=v
, and

(v,H_{g},c_{1})\cdot(\partial c_{1}+H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v,H_{h},c_{2})=(v,H_{g}\cdot H_{h},H_{h}^{-1}\cdot c_{1}+c_{2}).

We can recover
f
from
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
as follows. Given
(v_{1},\dots,v_{\bullet})\in V_{G}^{(\bullet)}
over
(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})
, we take any
(H_{g_{1}},\cdots,H_{g_{\bullet}})
and then we set

(4.6)

f(v_{1},\dots,v_{\bullet})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})\omega_{g_{1}}^{H_{g_{1}}}v_{1}+f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})\mathbf{t}v_{1}.

The map

\omega_{g_{1}}^{H_{g_{1}}}v=r_{g}(H_{g_{1}}^{-1}\mathbf{t}v-v^{-1})

is as in Proposition
3.40
: the unique map
(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
such that
\omega_{g_{1}}^{H_{g_{1}}}H_{g_{1}}=0
.
The right hand side of (
4.6
) is independent of
H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}
. So, if we are given
f_{0}\in C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})
and
f_{1}\in C^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}^{*})
such that the sum (as in the right hand side of the equation (
4.6
) above) is independent of
H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}
, then
f\in C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
can be defined by the equation (
4.6
) above. This gives rise to a one-to-one correspondence, and this last condition is exactly the condition as written in the statement.
∎

As a corollary, we obtain:

Corollary 4.18
.

The map

C_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}\rightarrow C(\widehat{V}_{G},V_{M}^{*});\quad f\mapsto f_{0}

is a cochain map. If
V_{M}\neq 0
, then the restriction to
C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
maps bijectively onto
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})
, so those elements
f_{0}\in C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G},V_{M}^{*})
for which there exists
f_{1}\in C^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{G},C_{M}^{*})
such that (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) hold.
43
43

43

If
V_{M}=0
, then the map

C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{G}\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{G},C_{M}^{*});\quad f\mapsto f_{1}

is a cochain map, and the restriction to
C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}
maps bijectively onto the elements
f_{1}\in C^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{G},C_{M}^{*})
that are independent of its arguments.

Remark 4.19
.

The VB-complex
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}
, in terms of
\widehat{V}_{G}
, is given by
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
. The latter complex consist of those pairs
(f_{0},f_{1})
such that

f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})=f_{1}(H_{g_{2}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})

and

f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})-f_{0}(H_{g_{1}}^{\prime},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})=h(H_{g_{1}},H_{g_{1}}^{\prime})f_{1}(H_{g_{2}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})

where again

h(H_{g_{1}},H_{g_{1}}^{\prime}):(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{1}}\rightarrow(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}

is given by

h(H_{g_{1}},H_{g_{1}}^{\prime})v=r_{g_{1}^{-1}}(H_{g_{1}}^{\prime}v-H_{g_{1}}v).

We complement the last remark by observing that the isomorphism

C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightarrow C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}

is particularly simple to write when using the projectable cochains:

C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G}^{*}\cong C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}

(see Section
1
for the definition).

Proposition 4.20
.

The complex
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M})
is isomorphic to
C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}
via the map

C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M})\rightarrow C_{\textnormal{proj}}V_{G}

given by

c(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})=r_{g_{1}}f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})+H_{g_{1}}f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

Proof.

The map is
CG
-linear and a bijection, for we can read the equation the other way around (using
f_{1}=\mathbf{s}c
). That the map is a cochain map, is a straightforward computation on generators.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

In this remark we will draw some parallels with the discussion presented here and the proofs of Proposition
2.12
and Proposition
4.10
. In the discussion of this section and the previous one, we employed exactly the strategy presented in the proof of Proposition
4.10
. Namely, in the proof of the latter proposition, we constructed an isomorphism
CH\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G
that is given by the pullback of a groupoid map
G\rightarrow H
(over the same base) which is a surjective submersion. In the above setup we described, we took one of the surjective VB-groupoid maps

\widehat{V}_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightarrow V_{G}\qquad(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rtimes\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow V_{G}

In general, the inverse of
CH\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{K\textnormal{-rel}}G
is given explicitly by using local sections of
G\rightarrow H
; the choice of local sections is irrelevant. In our case, the local sections are determined by local sections of
\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow G
, and this explains the appearance of them in the alternative proof of Proposition
2.12
. The proof of Proposition
2.12
using the cleavage is almost like the usage of a set-theoretic section in the proof of Proposition
4.10
in that it is a global version of the argument. Of course, for the proof of Proposition
2.12
to work, we need a smooth section, and therefore we have to pass to
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow G
. That is, we drop the invertibility assumption on the values of the section and we need to work “up to homotopy”. See also Section
6.2
, where we further clarify the step from fat extensions to the associated split
2
-term ruth.

5.
Fat extensions and general linear PB-groupoids

We dedicate this section to the correspondence between fat extension and general linear PB-groupoids. In
[
31
]
, where the notion of PB-groupoid is introduced, it is already observed that the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid is useful to construct the general linear PB-groupoid of a VB-groupoid. Following the previous section, it becomes clear that this should be interpreted as a composition of functors

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}.

The functorial aspects are not discussed in
[
31
]
, but we will do so in Section
7
.

Although the correspondence works as in
[
31
]
, we will go a slightly different route. The difference lies in that the general linear PB-groupoids we consider have a different structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid than in
[
31
]
. We use the one associated to two vector bundles
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
instead of the one associated to two
trivial
vector bundles. Moreover, the structure maps of the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid are slightly different than in
[
31
]
. These changes are done to improve the exposition.

In Appendix
A
we will discuss a related correspondence between a class of double groupoids that we call
core-transitive
, and structures that we call
core extensions
. The discussion presented in the appendix generalises
[
12
]
, but can be seen as a clarification of the remarks made at the end of that work. General linear PB-groupoids come with a gauge double groupoid when the underlying complex is regular, and such double groupoids are core-transitive. The associated core extension is exactly the fat extension. At the end of this section we mention how to think of fat extensions as double groupoids, but the details are left for the appendix.

5.1.
PB-groupoids

In analogy with VB-groupoids, a PB-groupoid is a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

where
P_{G}
and
P_{M}
are principal groupoid bundles over
G
and
M
, respectively. The compatibility between the principal bundle structures and the groupoid structures involved can be phrased as follows. The groupoids acting on
P_{G}
and on
P_{M}
are both groupoids over the same base:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

\circlearrowright

\circlearrowright

and these groupoids fit into a double groupoid over the unit groupoid
1_{N}
:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

That is, it is a strict Lie
2
-groupoid: after appropriate identifications, the structure maps of
H_{G}\rightrightarrows H_{M}
are groupoid maps over the identity (see also Remark
5.2
and Appendix
A
).

Now, the compatibility between the actions in a PB-groupoid can be phrased as the actions defining a (principal)
2
-action of the structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
be a Lie groupoid and let
H_{G}\rightrightarrows H_{M}\rightrightarrows N
be a strict Lie
2
-groupoid. A
2
-action
of
H_{G}
on
P_{G}
consists of two groupoid actions

P_{G}\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ }H_{G}\qquad P_{M}\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ }H_{M}

whose moment maps form a groupoid map

P_{G}\rightarrow 1_{N}

and whose action maps form a groupoid map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}\times_{N}H_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}\times_{N}H_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}}

where the groupoid structure of
P_{G}\times_{N}H_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}\times_{N}H_{M}
is defined componentwise. A
2
-action
P_{G}\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ }H_{G}
whose actions are principal is called a
PB-groupoid
, and we say it is over
G\rightrightarrows M
if
P_{G}/H_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}/H_{M}
is
G\rightrightarrows M
(up to isomorphism).

To end this small section, we conclude with some comments on strict Lie
2
-groupoids.

Remark 5.2
.

A useful point of view on strict Lie
2
-groupoids is as follows. Given a strict Lie
2
-groupoid
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}\rightrightarrows N
, we can think of the elements
h\in H_{2}
as abstract homotopy data between the source and target isomorphisms in
H_{1}
. In this analogy,
H_{2}
is a groupoid in two compatible ways: there is a vertical composition of homotopies, which should be compared to the fact that being homotopic is a transitive relation, and a horizontal composition of homotopies, which should be compared to Lemma
3.7
. In the main example of interest to us, the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid, we will see that the analogy can be taken literally.

A more geometric perspective is that
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
is a Lie groupoid whose source and target fibers have a compatible internal groupoid structure. The foliation by source fibers form a
family of Lie groupoids
,
44
44

44

A family of Lie groupoids is a Lie groupoid together with a groupoid map that is a surjective submersion onto some unit groupoid.
and so does the foliation by target fibers. If two elements are composable in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
, then they are both elements of a single source fiber (of
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
), and also of a single target fiber. Their product in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
, in turn, also lies in these fibers. Now, take two pairs of composable elements in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
, say, one pair in a source fiber over
x\in N
and the other pair in a target fiber over
x
. Either, we multiply first in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
and then in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
, or we multiply first in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
and then in
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
. The results are the same, and this compatibility between the two groupoid structures
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
and
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
is called the
interchange law
.

5.2.
The general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid

General linear PB-groupoids are PB-groupoids with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid. We introduce the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid of two vector bundles
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
next, but we do so in a slightly different way than in
[
31
]
. We will comment on this in Remark
5.5
. Notice that, below, the groupoid

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}=\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\rtimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

from Remark is used.

Example 5.3
.

Consider the bundle of Lie groups

\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

from Remark
3.10
; its multiplication is given by

(h_{x},\partial_{x})\cdot(h_{x}^{\prime},\partial_{x})\coloneq(h_{x}+h_{x}^{\prime}+h_{x}\partial h_{x}^{\prime},\partial_{x}).

We then set

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\coloneq\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

where
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
acts (on the left) on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
via

(\partial_{y},h_{y})\cdot(\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(\partial_{y},(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}},(1+\partial_{y}h_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}).

Following Remark
5.2
, the elements
(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})
of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
can be interpreted as pointwise homotopy data: we can interpret

\mathbf{s}(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}},\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})\qquad\mathbf{t}(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(\partial_{y},(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}},(1+\partial_{y}h_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})

as isomorphisms of complexes with respect to the differentials

(\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}\textnormal{ and }\partial_{y}

and they are homotopic via
h_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}
. The two multiplications of the two groupoid structures that
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
carries can now both be seen as composition of homotopies (see Lemma
3.7
). The groupoid structure of

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
