has the above source and target maps, and

(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\cdot(h_{y}^{\prime},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime})=(h_{y}+h_{y}^{\prime}+h_{y}\partial_{y}h_{y}^{\prime},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime}).

The groupoid multiplication of

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

is a type of (horizontal) composition of homotopies: we set
\mathbf{s}(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}
and
\mathbf{t}(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=\partial_{y}
, and

(h_{z},\partial_{z},\Psi_{z,y})\cdot(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\coloneq(h_{z}+\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}})^{-1}+h_{z}\partial_{z}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}})^{-1},\partial_{z},\Psi_{z,y}\Psi_{y,x}).

Precomposing the homotopy on the right hand side with
\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}
is indeed composition of homotopies of
h_{z}\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}
and
h_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}
. Another way of interpreting this multiplication is that it can be seen as being induced by the product of
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
:

(h_{z},\partial_{z},\Psi_{z,y})\cdot(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=((h_{z},\partial_{z})\cdot(\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}})^{-1},\partial_{z}),\Psi_{z,y}\Psi_{y,x}).

Taking
C_{M}=M\times\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
V_{M}=M\times\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, we write
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{M}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
for the resulting strict Lie
2
-groupoid.

Remark 5.4
.

In essence, the above object is a strict Lie
2
-groupoid defined from a
crossed module of Lie groupoids
as in
[
42
,
1
,
39
]
. Crossed modules and strict Lie
2
-groupoids correspond to each other. In the above case, the structure of crossed module is encoded in the Lie groupoid map from
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
to
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, and the canonical action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
on
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
(which is a
bundle of Lie groups representation
of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
on
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
). We refer to Section
5.9
and Appendix
A
for more details.

To lighten the notation, we often write
(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})
, or even just
(h,\Psi)
, for elements

(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}.

Similarly, we often write
\Psi=\Psi_{y,x}=(\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
.

Remark 5.5
.

A useful way to depict elements
(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
is using blockmatrices. Recall for this the observation we made in Remark
3.12
. There, we observed that an element
(\partial_{x},h_{x})\in\textnormal{Pert}(V_{M},C_{M})
can be represented by a matrix

\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{x}\partial_{x}&h_{x}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M}).

Notice that we keep track of the differential
\partial_{x}
separately, but we suppress it from the notation. Then, elements of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
can be represented by pairs of matrices

\left(\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{y}\partial_{y}&h_{y}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\right).

This way, the groupoid structures of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
are turned into matrix multiplications;
for example, the multiplication of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
is given by

\displaystyle\left(\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{z}\partial_{z}&h_{z}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{y}\partial_{y}&h_{y}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{z}\partial_{z}&h_{z}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{y}\partial_{y}&h_{y}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1},\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\right).

In
[
31
]
, the correct matrix to consider does not have the “
1+h_{x}\partial_{x}
-factor” in the upper left corner (of the matrices representing the elements of
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
). This is a consequence of the fact that the groupoid structure of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
we use is slightly different. We find the convention presented above more suitable for our discussion, especially with an eye on the correspondence we will set with fat extensions.

Remark 5.6
.

The previous remark already touched on it, but recall the following from
[
31
]
: the strict Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
comes with a “
2
-representation” (in
[
31
]
called “anchored
2
-representation”) on the
2
-term complex

\partial\times 1:C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}).

One way to put this is that there is a map of strict Lie
2
-groupoids

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}),V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}))_{G}.

It is an embedding determined by the base map, which is the diagonal

\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}).

Following Proposition 3.10 of
[
31
]
, it can also be seen as a linear
2
-action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
on the above
2
-term complex seen as a VB-groupoid over
1_{\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})}
:

(C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}))\ltimes(V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}))\rightrightarrows V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

(see Example
3.26
). That
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
acts “linearly” on this VB-groupoid becomes clear when using the matrix notation: the (left) action is given by applying the linear transformation

\begin{pmatrix}1+h_{y}\partial_{y}&h_{y}\\
0&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&0\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&h_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M})

(and conjugating the differential). Moreover,
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
acts on
V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
via

\Psi_{y,x}\cdot v_{x}=\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}v_{x}

(and conjugating the differential). Recall from Definition
5.1
that the key feature of these two actions defining a
2
-action is that the action map is a groupoid map.
45
45

45

Implicitly, we use that the moment maps of the two actions are compatible.
Here, the groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\times_{\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})}((C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}))\ltimes(V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\times_{\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})}(V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}))}

is used, whose structure is defined componentwise. Notice that this groupoid is a VB-groupoid over
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, and with respect to this structure the action is linear. These facts are readily verified using the matrix notation from above.

Using the differential
\partial
as moment map, we see that there is also a
2
-representation of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
on the
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The linear
2
-action is defined in an analogous way as above, and it corresponds to the identity map
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\xrightarrow{=}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
.

5.3.
General linear PB-groupoids

In this section, we define what we mean with a general linear PB-groupoid. We also discuss some important properties that we will use.

Definition 5.7
.

Let
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
be vector bundles. An abstract general linear PB-groupoid with underlying vector bundles
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
is a PB-groupoid
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
. It is called trivial if

P_{M}=\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

is the canonical principal
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
-bundle. A trivial abstract general linear PB-groupoid is called a
general linear PB-groupoid
.

To be clear, the (right) principal
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
-bundle structure on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
is given by

\Psi_{z,y}\cdot\Psi_{y,x}(=\Psi_{z,y}\cdot((\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial_{z}\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}},\Psi_{y,x}))\coloneq\Psi_{z,y}\Psi_{y,x}.

To explain the definition, we want to emphasise two features of general linear PB-groupoids (Proposition
5.9
and Remark
5.16
).

Remark 5.8
.

We first observe that the moment map of
P_{M}
in an abstract general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
is always given by conjugation in the following sense. Let
p\in P_{M}
, and let

\Psi_{y,x}=(\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

be composable with
p
. Then
1_{y}=(\partial_{y},1_{y})
is composable with
p
, and therefore also any other
\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime}=(\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
is. Since all
p^{\prime}\in P_{M}
with the same image as
p
under the projection map
P_{M}\rightarrow M
are of the form

p^{\prime}=p\cdot\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime},

the moment map is given on the fibers of the projection
P_{M}\rightarrow M
by conjugation of the differential
\partial_{y}
by an element of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
.

Now, to justify the terminology in the above definition, observe the following.

Proposition 5.9
.

Suppose
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
is an abstract general linear PB-groupoid. If
P_{M}\rightarrow M
has a global section, then
P_{G}
is isomorphic to a (trivial abstract) general linear PB-groupoid.

Proof.

If the projection map
P_{M}\rightarrow M
has a global section
\sigma
, then we can send
p\in P_{M}
to the unique
\Psi(p)=(\partial_{y},\Psi(p))\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
such that

1_{p}\cdot(0_{y},\Psi(p)^{-1})=1_{\sigma\pi p}\in P_{G}.

This turns the image of the section in
P_{M}
into the unit section of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow M
. In turn, the moment map of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
becomes the conjugation of a differential, and the action transforms into the canonical action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. This proves the statement.
∎

To be clear, from now on we always assume general linear PB-groupoids are trivial abstract general linear PB-groupoids. As expected, a fundamental example of a general linear PB-groupoid is given by a
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Example 5.10
.

Given a
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, observe that
\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})
(see Section
3.2
) acts on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
via its canonical representation. The Lie groupoid

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

then has a structure of general linear PB-groupoid given by

(h_{z},\Psi_{z,y})\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(h_{z}\cdot(\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi^{V_{M}}_{z,y})^{-1}),\Psi_{z,y}\Psi_{y,x}).

The moment map is given by conjugating the differential of
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Observe that, as is implicit from the notation,
\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi^{V_{M}}_{z,y})^{-1}
is an element of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
. Indeed,

1+\partial\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}h_{y}(\Psi^{V_{M}}_{z,y})^{-1}=\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}}(1+\partial_{y}h_{y})(\Psi^{V_{M}}_{z,y})^{-1}

where
\partial_{y}
is the (suppressed) differential in
(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})
, which satisfies

\partial_{y}=(\Psi_{z,y}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial\Psi_{z,y}^{C_{M}}

by assumption.

Remark 5.11
.

In the previous example,
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
simply inherits the above structure from the differential

\partial:M\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

through the embedding of strict Lie
2
-groupoids (a
2
-representation)

(\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows M)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}.

As
\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})
, together with its representations, carries little information about the differential (see Proposition
3.11
), the strict Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
itself does not carry information about the differential.

The next fact points towards the relation between general linear PB-groupoids, fat extensions, VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths. Namely, every general linear PB-groupoid over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
contains the above example:

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
P_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
be a general linear PB-groupoid with underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Then
46
46

46

We used
1
for both unit embeddings
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\hookrightarrow P_{G}
and
M\hookrightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
.

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\hookrightarrow P_{G}\qquad(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\mapsto 1_{1_{y}}\cdot(h_{y},\partial,\Psi_{y,x})

is an embedding of general linear PB-groupoids. Moreover, this map fits into a short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

of Lie groupoids, where
P_{G}\rightarrow G
is the projection map.

Proof.

That the map is an embedding of general linear PB-groupoids is readily verified and follows from the compatibility assumptions between the Lie groupoid structure of
P_{G}
and its principal
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
-structure. The last claim is a consequence of all elements in the kernel of
P_{G}\rightarrow G
being related to
1_{1_{y}}\in P_{G}
by a unique element
(h_{y},\partial_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
. Since then
\partial_{y}=\partial
is the differential
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
,
h_{y}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
and the result follows.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

Notice that the short exact sequence of Lie groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

is not over the identity, but over the sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}}

A general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}
comes with a cochain complex representation on

\partial\times 1:C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M}).

This cochain complex representation of
P_{G}
can be described as follows:

Definition 5.14
.

Let
P_{G}
be a general linear PB-groupoid. The cochain complex representation associated to
P_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
on

\partial\times 1:C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

is defined as follows. Given
p_{g}\in P_{G}
,
(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})\in C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})\in V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, we set

p_{g}\cdot(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})\coloneq(\mathbf{t}(p_{g})\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x}^{-1}c_{\mathbf{s}g},\mathbf{t}p_{g})\qquad p_{g}\cdot(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})\coloneq(\mathbf{t}(p_{g})\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x}^{-1}c_{\mathbf{s}g},\mathbf{t}p_{g}).

Remark 5.15
.

The cochain complex representation is “
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
-equivariant”. More precisely,
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
acts on the complex (on the right): if
(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\in C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
,
(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\in V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
\Psi_{y,x}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, then

(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\cdot\Psi_{y,x}=(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y}\Psi_{y,x})\qquad(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\cdot\Psi_{y,x}=(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y}\Psi_{y,x}).

Given
p_{g}\in P_{G}
,
(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\in C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
\Psi_{y,x}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, we therefore have

(p_{g}\cdot\Psi_{y,x})\cdot((c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\cdot\Psi_{y,x})=(p_{g}\cdot(c_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y}))\cdot\Psi_{y,x},

and similarly for
(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,y})\in V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Because of this fact, the cochain complex representation is determined by the induced cochain complex representation of the subgroupoid

F_{G}\coloneq\{p_{g}\in P_{G}\mid\mathbf{s}p_{g}=1_{\mathbf{s}g}\}\subset P_{G}.

The suggestive notation is of course to emphasise that this subgroupoid, together with the above cochain complex representation, reveals the fat extension associated to a general linear PB-groupoid.

Before we move on to give the correspondences, we make another remark which relates our setup of general linear PB-groupoids back to
[
31
]
.

Remark 5.16
.

The general linear groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, seen as a groupoid over
M
, contains the gauge groupoid of the principal
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{M}
-bundle

\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})=\textnormal{Fr }C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Fr }V_{M}=\{\phi_{x}^{C_{M}}\times\phi_{x}^{V_{M}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\ell}\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\mid x\in M\},

if it exists, that is. The frame bundle
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
has moment map given by conjugating the differential
\partial
using the frames, and the action is the canonical one. This is completely analogous to how we defined the action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. In fact, the gauge groupoids of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
are equal and given by the cochain isomorphisms
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
from Remark
2.17
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

(which is smooth only when
\partial
has constant rank). Now, as follows from
[
31
]
, a
2
-term complex of vector bundles
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is actually equivalently described by a principal
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{M}
-bundle (using the associated vector bundle construction in the other direction). Instead of making this point again, we simply encode the
2
-term complex as a map on the gauge groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Or rather, we reinterpret this data again as a type of general linear principal groupoid bundle:

\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\textnormal{ \rotatebox[origin]{90.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$} }\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}.

Then, the structure of general linear PB-groupoid over it will, as for PB-groupoids with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{M}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
, exactly describe the data of a fat extension. Moreover, whether we use
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{G}
or
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
, the constructions that appear in the equivalence will be defined in analogous ways.

If the gauge groupoids of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
(or
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
) is smooth, we can also take a smooth gauge groupoid of the total space
P_{G}
of a general linear PB-groupoid. The result is a double groupoid, and then gives rise to another way to encode the structure of a fat extension that will be explained at the end of this section.

We will first describe the correspondence between fat extensions and general linear PB-groupoids.

5.4.
From fat extensions to general linear PB-groupoids

Given a fat extension
F_{G}
, the associated general linear PB-groupoid is given as follows. We define

P_{G}\coloneq F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

where we used the canonical (left) action of
F_{G}
on
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
by using the actions of
F_{G}
on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
. We proceed to define the right
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
-action on
P_{G}
as another canonical action:

(H_{g},\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})\cdot(h_{x},(\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x})^{-1}\partial\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x},\Psi_{x,y}^{\prime})\coloneq(H_{g}\cdot(\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x}^{C_{M}}h_{x}(\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}),\Psi_{\mathbf{s}g,x}\Psi_{x,y}^{\prime}).

Using the explicit descriptions of all the structure involved, we obtain:

Proposition 5.17
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension. Then

F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

is a general linear PB-groupoid.

Remark 5.18
.

Notice the similarities with the PB-groupoid associated to the fat groupoid as explained in
[
31
]
.
Instead of defining the PB-groupoid

F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

we can set
47
47

47

Recall that
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})=\textnormal{Fr }C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{Fr }V_{M}
.

F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M}).

In exactly the same way as above, this defines a PB-groupoid with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{M}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{\ell})
. Roughly speaking, the shift in perspective is as the difference between
\textnormal{GL }E
and
\textnormal{Fr }E
for a vector bundle
E
. Instead of thinking of usual frames of
E
, namely frames modelled on Euclidean space, we think of frames modelled on its own fibers. We therefore need to consider a
\textnormal{GL}(E)
-symmetry instead of a
\textnormal{GL}(k)
-symmetry.

5.5.
From general linear PB-groupoids to fat extensions

The construction we did in the previous section gives a clear hint on how to recover the fat extension from its general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Namely, the subgroupoid we saw in Remark
5.15

(5.1)

\{p_{g}\in P_{G}\mid\mathbf{s}p_{g}=1_{\mathbf{s}g}\}\subset P_{G}

turns out to form a unique representative set for the Lie groupoid

F_{G}\coloneq P_{G}/\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\rightrightarrows M.

We used here that
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
acts freely and properly, via the unit embedding, on
P_{G}
. As observed in Remark
5.15
, the description of
F_{G}
using the representatives above makes it clear that the cochain complex representation descends to
F_{G}
: given
H_{g}=[p_{g}]\in F_{G}
, where
\mathbf{s}p_{g}=1_{\mathbf{s}g}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, then the cochain complex representation is given by

F_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto\mathbf{t}p_{g}.

The surjective submersion
P_{G}\rightarrow G
is a groupoid map, and the induced groupoid map
F_{G}\rightarrow G
is still a surjective submersion. We therefore obtain a short exact sequence of groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

The cochain complex representation of
F_{G}
induces a cochain complex representation of
H
, and then we conclude with the following corollary of Proposition
5.12
:

Corollary 5.19
.

The embedding
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\hookrightarrow P_{G}
induced by Proposition
5.12
is a Lie groupoid isomorphism onto
H
. Moreover, this map is equivariant with respect to the cochain complex representations.
48
48

48

We consider here the cochain complex representation on
\textnormal{H}\subset F_{G}
that we constructed above, and the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
.

Proof.

Its inverse is given as follows. Given
h_{x}=[p_{x}]\in\textnormal{H}
(with
\mathbf{s}p_{x}=1_{x}
), denote by
\Phi h_{x}
the unique element in
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
such that

h_{x}\cdot(\Phi h_{x},\partial,1_{x})^{-1}=1_{1_{x}}\in P_{G}.

Then, the map

\Phi:\textnormal{H}\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})

is the desired inverse.
∎

It is readily verified that we set a true one-to-one correspondence between fat extensions and general linear PB-groupoids:

Proposition 5.20
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad P_{G}\mapsto P_{G}/\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}.

is inverse to it (up to isomorphisms).

We find it worthwhile to take a moment to also describe the correspondences with VB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths. This is what we discuss next, starting with VB-groupoids.

5.6.
From VB-groupoids to general linear PB-groupoids

As we use a different strict structural Lie
2
-groupoid
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
for general linear PB-groupoids, the correspondence between VB-groupoids and general linear PB-groupoids looks slightly different than in
[
31
]
. But the shift in perspective is analogous to the one from the previous correspondence between fat extensions and general linear PB-groupoids. Instead of looking at (adapted) frames of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
modelled on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\ell}
, we will consider “frames” modelled on fibers of
C_{M}\oplus V_{M}
:

\phi_{g}:(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G})_{g}.

In fact, from the previous section, we can infer what maps we should really be considering: a composition of an isomorphism defined by an element
H_{g}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
:

(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G})_{g}\qquad(c,v)\mapsto r_{g}c+H_{g}v

with two linear isomorphisms

(C_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}.

The collection of such elements form the general linear PB-groupoid

\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

of interest. This is analogous to how one defines the (adapted) frame bundle of a VB-groupoid in
[
31
]
. More precisely, we define this general linear PB-groupoid, without using the fat groupoid, as follows.
49
49

49

See for this the older versions of the paper
[
31
]
on the arXiv.
We set

\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}=\{\phi_{g}:(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G})_{g}\mid\mathbf{s}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(C_{M})_{x}}=0,\textnormal{ }\mathbf{t}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}},\textnormal{ }\mathbf{s}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}\in\textnormal{GL}(V_{M})\}

which will be the total groupoid
P_{G}
of the general linear PB-groupoid associated to
V_{G}
. To define the structure maps of this groupoid, we use the following diagram:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ell_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{s}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{t}}

Now, we construct four maps that will describe the source and target map of
\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Namely, given
\phi_{g}\in\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}
, we write

\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{C_{M}}\coloneq r_{g^{-1}}\phi_{g}|_{(C_{M})_{x}}\qquad\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{V_{M}}\coloneq\mathbf{t}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}\qquad\phi_{\mathbf{s}g}^{V_{M}}\coloneq\mathbf{s}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}

and
50
50

50

Notice that

\ell_{g}^{-1}v=-r_{g}(v^{-1})

where
v\in\ker\mathbf{t}
.

\phi_{\mathbf{s}g}^{C_{M}}\coloneq\ell_{g}^{-1}\phi_{g}(1,-(\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{C_{M}}).

We then set
\mathbf{s}\phi_{g}=(\phi_{\mathbf{s}g}^{C_{M}},\phi_{\mathbf{s}g}^{V_{M}})
,
\mathbf{t}\phi_{g}=(\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{C_{M}},\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{V_{M}})
, and

\phi_{g}\cdot\phi_{h}=r_{h}\phi_{g}|_{(C_{M})_{x}}+\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}\cdot\phi_{h}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}\qquad\phi_{g}^{-1}=-(\phi_{g}(1,-1-(\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\partial\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{C_{M}}))^{-1}.

Realising elements
(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})
of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
as block matrices

\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&h_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M})

the (right) action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
on
\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}
is given by matrix application:

\begin{pmatrix}\phi|_{(C_{M})_{y}}&\phi|_{(V_{M})_{y}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&h_{y}\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\phi(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}&\phi h\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}+\phi\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}.

This matrix showed up in Remark
5.6
where we discussed the canonical
2
-representations of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
. For similar reasons, the action defined above defines a
2
-action, and this is the desired principal
2
-action. We therefore obtain:

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid. Then

\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

is a general linear PB-groupoid.

Remark 5.22
.

Using the fat groupoid to describe this general linear PB-groupoid, i.e. seeing it as an action groupoid

\widehat{V}_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

describes the composition of functors

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}.

As we already mentioned, this realises elements
\phi\in\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}
as compositions of maps

r_{g}\phi_{\mathbf{t}g}^{C_{M}}+H_{g}\phi_{\mathbf{s}g}^{V_{M}}:(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{M})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G})_{g}.

Working with
\textnormal{GL}(k,\ell)_{G}
instead of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
(so with
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
instead of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
), this action groupoid was used in
[
31
]
.

5.7.
From general linear PB-groupoids to VB-groupoids

Following
[
31
]
, it should not come as a surprise now that we can recover a VB-groupoid from its associated general linear PB-groupoid using a type of associated bundle construction. However, since we passed from
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
to
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, the construction will especially be reminiscent to the association of a VB-groupoid from a fat extension.

Now, given a general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}
, recall from Definition
5.14
that we can view

\partial\times 1:C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})

as a cochain complex representation of
P_{G}
. Therefore, it comes with an associated VB-groupoid that we simply denote by

P_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C_{M}\times_{M}P_{G}\times_{M}V_{M}\rightrightarrows V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M}).

Explicitly, the structure maps are as follows: the source and target maps are given, respectively, by
\mathbf{s}(c_{\mathbf{t}g},p_{g},v_{\mathbf{s}g})=(v_{\mathbf{s}g},\mathbf{s}p_{g})
,
\mathbf{t}(c_{\mathbf{t}g},p_{g},v_{\mathbf{s}g})=(\partial c_{\mathbf{t}g}+p_{g}\cdot v_{\mathbf{s}g},\mathbf{t}p_{g})
and

(c_{\mathbf{t}g},p_{g},\partial c_{\mathbf{s}g}+p_{g^{\prime}}\cdot v_{\mathbf{s}g^{\prime}})\cdot(c_{\mathbf{s}g},p_{g^{\prime}},v_{\mathbf{s}g^{\prime}})=(c_{\mathbf{t}g}+p_{g}\cdot c_{\mathbf{s}g},p_{g}\cdot p_{g^{\prime}},v_{\mathbf{s}g^{\prime}}).

The VB-groupoid inherits a natural free and proper (right
2
-) action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
:

(c_{\mathbf{t}g},p_{g},v_{\mathbf{s}g})\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})=(c_{\mathbf{t}g}-p_{g}\cdot(\mathbf{s}(p_{g})h_{y}\mathbf{s}(p_{g})^{-1}v_{\mathbf{s}g}),p_{g}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x}),v_{\mathbf{s}g}).

Since the action indeed preserves the linear structure, the result
V(P_{G})
is a VB-groupoid over
V_{M}
.

Proposition 5.23
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\qquad V_{G}\mapsto\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\qquad P_{G}\mapsto V(P_{G})

is inverse to it (up to canonical isomorphisms).

Remark 5.24
.

The above quotient can be taken in steps. Indeed, we can take a quotient of
P_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
by
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, and then by
\textnormal{H}(C_{M},V_{M})\ltimes V_{M}
(see Section
4.2
and (
4.1
) for the explicit formula of this action). This way, we can interpret the first quotient as recovering
F_{G}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
, and then the second quotient is as in Section
4.2
. Doing this, we described the composition of functors

\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}.

5.8.
The abstract ruth of a general linear PB-groupoid

We conclude by setting the correspondence between general linear PB-groupoids and
2
-term ruths. Consider the abstract ruth associated to
P_{G}\ltimes(V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
, so

C^{\bullet}(P_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C^{\bullet}(P_{G};C_{M})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(P_{G};V_{M}).

Note the abuse of notation; it would be more correct to write the complex as

C^{\bullet}(P_{G};C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})).

But if
(f_{0},f_{1})
is a cochain in this complex, then the
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
components of
f_{0}(p_{1},\dots,p_{\bullet})
and
f_{0}(p_{2},\dots,p_{\bullet})
are equal to
\mathbf{t}p_{1}
and
\mathbf{t}p_{2}
, respectively, so we simplify the notation.

If we argue through the language of fat extensions or VB-groupoids, for example, using Proposition
4.10
, we see that the following subcomplex should be considered:

Definition 5.25
.

The
GL
-equivariant
cochains

(f_{0},f_{1})\in C(P_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})=C^{\bullet}(P_{G};C_{M})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(P_{G};V_{M})

are those satisfying, for all
(p_{g_{1}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}})\in P^{(\bullet)}
,

f_{1}(p_{g_{2}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}})=f_{1}(p_{g_{2}}^{\prime},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}}^{\prime})

and, for all
(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
,

f_{0}(p_{g_{1}}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x}),\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x}))=f_{0}(p_{g_{1}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}})-p_{g_{1}}\cdot(\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}}h_{y}(\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}})^{-1}f_{1}(p_{g_{2}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}})).

The resulting subcomplex is denoted by
C_{\textnormal{GL}}(P_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
(or
C_{\textnormal{GL}}(P_{G};C_{M})
).

Remark 5.26
.

Given
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{\textnormal{GL}}(P_{G};C_{M})
, we write

f_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(p_{g_{1}},p_{g_{2}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}})\qquad f_{1}(\mathbf{t}(p_{g_{2}}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{1}(p_{g_{2}},\dots,p_{g_{\bullet}}).

The above condition then reads

f_{0}(p_{g_{1}}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})=f_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})-p_{g_{1}}\cdot(\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}}h_{y}(\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}})^{-1}f_{1}(\mathbf{s}(p_{g_{1}}),g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})).

From our previous discussions, we can infer that the above abstract ruth is isomorphic to
C_{\textnormal{VB}}V(P_{G})^{*}
and to
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})
. For example: the PB-groupoid associated to a fat extension
F_{G}
is given by

P_{G}=F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M}).

The representation on
P_{G}
is given by the pullback along the canonical groupoid map

P_{G}\rightarrow F_{G}.

This pullback then sets the desired isomorphism.

Proposition 5.27
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The pullback

C^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M});C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})

restricts to an isomorphism

C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{GL}}(F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M});C_{M}).

Remark 5.28
.

To explain the terminology “GL-equivariance”, notice that we can conjugate elements
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C_{\textnormal{GL}}(P_{G};C_{M})
to

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneq\mathbf{t}(p_{g_{1}})^{-1}f_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}_{1}(\Psi_{\mathbf{t}g_{2},x},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\coloneq\Psi_{\mathbf{t}g_{2},x}^{-1}f_{1}(\Psi_{\mathbf{t}g_{2},x},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

We can think of
(\widetilde{f}_{0},\widetilde{f}_{1})
as the result of postcomposing
(f_{0},f_{1})
with the evaluation/action map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}\times_{M}\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}

The conditions of Definition
5.25
in terms of
(\widetilde{f}_{0},\widetilde{f}_{1})
are as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{1}}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x}),g)\\
\widetilde{f}_{1}(\mathbf{s}(p_{g_{1}}\cdot(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})),g)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}(\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}})^{-1}(1+h_{y}\partial_{y})^{-1}&-(\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}h_{y}^{-1}\\
0&(\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g)\\
\widetilde{f}_{1}(\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}},g)\end{pmatrix}.

The result is therefore a complex
\widetilde{C}_{\textnormal{GL}}(P_{G};C_{M})
consisting of “GL-equivariant” cochains
(\widetilde{f}_{0},\widetilde{f}_{1})
with respect to the
2
-actions of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
on
P_{G}
and on
C_{M}\ltimes V_{M}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
(see Remark
5.6
). In terms of this complex, the differential is given by

\delta\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{1}},g_{3},\dots,g_{\bullet+1})=(-1)^{\bullet}\mathbf{s}p_{g_{1}}(\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{2}},g_{3},\dots,g_{\bullet+1})-\widetilde{f}_{0}(p_{g_{1}}\cdot p_{g_{2}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet+1}))+\textstyle\sum_{k=2}^{\bullet+1}d_{k}^{*}\widetilde{f}_{0}

where
p_{g_{2}}
is arbitrary and composable with
p_{g_{1}}
(and similarly for
\delta\widetilde{f}_{1}
).

5.9.
From general linear PB-groupoids to general linear double groupoids

There is yet another way to encode the data of a fat extension. This becomes clear from the fact that general linear PB-groupoids sometimes come with a
gauge double groupoid
.

Remark 5.29
.

Recall from Remark
5.16
that the groupoid of cochain isomorphisms

\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightrightarrows M

can be seen as the gauge groupoid of the principal groupoid bundle
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
(or
\textnormal{Fr}(C_{M},V_{M})
). Given a general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}
, what if we also take the gauge groupoid of the principal
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
-bundle
P_{G}
?

In general, given a PB-groupoid
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
with structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid
H_{G}\rightrightarrows H_{M}\rightrightarrows N
, then
P_{G}
comes with a (not necessarily smooth)
gauge double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

Doing this for a general linear PB-groupoid (also if we take a general linear PB-groupoid as in
[
31
]
), we obtain a double groupoid whose vertical base groupoid is given by
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
. A natural condition to put on the PB-groupoid so that the above gauge groupoids are smooth is to assume the moment maps to be surjective submersions onto an embedded submanifold of
N
.
51
51

51

By the properties of the moment maps, one moment map has this property if the other does.
This happens for general linear PB-groupoids precisely when the differential
\partial
is of constant rank.

Now, to be more precise, we introduce a special class of double groupoids. First of all, we use the following notation for double groupoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

Here,
G_{\rightarrow}
and
G_{\downarrow}
are Lie groupoids, and we denote their structure maps using the same arrow-index. Moreover,
G
is a Lie groupoid in two ways, over
G_{\rightarrow}
and over
G_{\downarrow}
, and we denote the groupoid structure maps of
G\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}
with indices
\rightarrow
and
G\rightrightarrows G_{\rightarrow}
with indices
\downarrow
.

Definition 5.30
.

A double groupoid is a diagram of groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

such that the structure maps of
G\rightrightarrows G_{\rightarrow}
are groupoid maps over the respective structure maps of
G_{\downarrow}\rightrightarrows M
.
52
52

52

Equivalently, the structure maps of
G\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}
are groupoid maps over the respective structure maps of
G_{\rightarrow}\rightrightarrows M
We also assume that the double source map

(\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow},\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}):G\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}G_{\rightarrow}

is a (not necessarily surjective) submersion.

In the compatibility assumption made in this definition, we see, for example, the composable arrows of
G\rightrightarrows G_{\rightarrow}
as a groupoid

G\times_{G_{\rightarrow}}G\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}

with structure defined componentwise. The compatibility of the two products is called the
interchange law
and takes the form

(g\cdot_{\rightarrow}h)\cdot_{\downarrow}(g^{\prime}\cdot_{\rightarrow}h^{\prime})=(g\cdot_{\downarrow}g^{\prime})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h\cdot_{\downarrow}h^{\prime}).

Remark 5.31
.

As in
[
60
,
45
]
, we do not require the double source map
(\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow},\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow})
to be a
surjective
submersion. However, gauge double groupoids of PB-groupoids (that come with a gauge double groupoid) have this property. Following
[
45
]
, we call double groupoids with a double source map that is a surjective submersion
full
.

In general, since the double source map
(\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow},\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow})
is a submersion, we obtain a
core groupoid
that we suggestively denote by
F\rightrightarrows M
:

Definition 5.32
.

The core groupoid of a double groupoid is given by

F\coloneq\ker\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}\cap\ker\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}\rightrightarrows M.

Explicitly, its structure maps are
\mathbf{s}(g)=\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}g=\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}g
,
\mathbf{t}(g)=\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}g=\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g
, and
53
53

53

That is, in the definition of
g\cdot h
, we right-translate
g
to be composable with
h
.

\displaystyle g\cdot h

\displaystyle=(g\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h))\cdot_{\rightarrow}h=(g\cdot_{\rightarrow}1_{\downarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}h))\cdot_{\downarrow}h

\displaystyle g^{-1}

\displaystyle=(g)_{\rightarrow}^{-1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1}=(g)_{\downarrow}^{-1}\cdot_{\rightarrow}1_{\downarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}g)^{-1}.

Moreover,
F
comes with two groupoid maps

G_{\downarrow}\xleftarrow{\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}}F\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}}G_{\rightarrow}

which are just the restriction to the target maps. We now concentrate on the following very particular case of double groupoids:

Definition 5.33
.

A double groupoid as above is called (vertically) core-transitive if the map

\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}:F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}

is a surjective submersion.
54
54

54

Of course, there is an analogous definition of horizontally core-transitive if we interchange the vertical structures with the horizontal ones.

Core-transitive double groupoids turn out to be determined by, what we will call,
core extensions
.

Definition 5.34
.

A core extension is a diagram
55
55

55

The action
G_{\downarrow}\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ H}
is a representation; that is, it acts by Lie group automorphisms (See Definition
A.1
).
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

such that the restriction of the map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
to
H
is equivariant (with respect to conjugation in
G_{\downarrow}
) and the Peiffer identity
56
56

56

The
Peiffer identity
is a name given to a condition that a
crossed module
of Lie groupoids satisfies, of which the Peiffer identity in our case is a generalisation.
holds: the conjugation action of
F
on
H
, viewing
H
as a normal subgroupoid of
F
, equals the action by
F
on
H
induced by the representation
G_{\downarrow}\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ H}
and
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
.

This is directly related to
[
12
]
, a result that is generalised in Appendix
A
. In the appendix we present a proof, and we explain the already apparent connection with fat extensions. In the rest of this section we focus on the implication that regular fat extensions (fat extensions over regular cochain complexes) are equivalently described by
general linear double groupoids
.

The general linear double groupoids that encode regular
2
-term complexes are strict Lie
2
-groupoids. Such a general linear double groupoid sits naturally inside the strict Lie
2
-groupoid of Remark
5.11
.

Example 5.35
.

Given a regular
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, we denote again by
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
its Lie groupoid of cochain isomorphisms. Consider the double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}\times_{M}\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\times_{M}\textnormal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

The groupoid structures are given as follows. The vertical groupoid structure has source and target map given, respectively, by
\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})=h_{x}
,
\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})=h_{y}
and

(h_{z},\Psi_{z,y},h_{y})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})=(h_{z},\Psi_{z,y}\Psi_{y,x},h_{x}).

The horizontal groupoid structure is given by
\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})=\Psi_{y,x}
,

\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})=((1+h_{y}\partial)\Psi_{y,x}^{C_{M}}(1+h_{x}\partial)^{-1},(1+\partial h_{y})\Psi_{y,x}^{V_{M}}(1+\partial h_{x})^{-1}),

and

(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h_{y}^{\prime},\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime},h_{x}^{\prime})=(h_{y}h_{y}^{\prime},\Psi_{y,x}^{\prime},h_{x}h_{x}^{\prime}).

The vertical groupoid structure has a normal subgroupoid of the form

\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{H}

through the embedding

\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightarrow\textnormal{H}\times_{M}\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\times_{M}\textnormal{H}\qquad(\Psi_{y,x},h_{x})\mapsto(\Psi_{y,x}h_{x}\Psi^{-1}_{y,x},\Psi_{y,x},h_{x}).

We can then take the quotient with respect to this normal subgroupoid to obtain a double groupoid (a strict Lie
2
-groupoid) of the form
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\textnormal{Aut}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

This is the general linear double groupoid associated to a
2
-term complex. It sits naturally inside of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
(seen as a strict Lie
2
-groupoid; see Remark
5.11
), for it is also this strict Lie
2
-groupoid restricted to
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
:

\textnormal{H}\ltimes\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\xrightarrow{\sim}\textnormal{H}_{\textnormal{Aut}}\qquad(h_{y},\Psi_{y,x})\mapsto[h_{y},\Psi_{y,x},0_{x}].

The reason for writing the double groupoid as above is that this description generalises to produce the general linear double groupoid of a fat extension (see Appendix
A
).

We now define:

Definition 5.36
.

Let
G
be a Lie groupoid, and let
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
be a regular
2
-term complex. A double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\textnormal{Aut}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

that is vertically core-transitive, and whose restriction to the vertical units is the strict Lie
2
-groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\textnormal{Aut}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

is called a
general linear double groupoid
of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Our observation in Remark
5.29
, together with Proposition
5.12
, reveals:

Proposition 5.37
.

The gauge double groupoid of a regular general linear PB-groupoid
P_{G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}\times_{\textnormal{GL}}P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

is a general linear double groupoid.

To go from general linear double groupoids to regular general linear PB-groupoids requires a slight generalisation (and clarification) of
[
12
]
.
57
57

57

To emphasise: most essential points are explained in
[
12
]
, especially in the last remarks made in that work.
If we consider a regular general linear PB-groupoid, then the core extension of its gauge double groupoid recovers precisely the fat extension. So, with this correspondence in mind, general fat extensions can be thought of as “core extensions” of general linear PB-groupoids. We delay the discussion now to the appendix, and proceed with making some further comments on the correspondences.

6.
Fat splittings and comments on the correspondences

Now that we went through the correspondences, we make some final comments.

6.1.
Cohomological vanishing for proper groupoids

We mention here that the cohomological vanishing theorem for
2
-term ruths of proper groupoids is straightforward using fat extensions:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension over a proper groupoid
G
. Moreover, let
\textstyle\int_{x}=\textstyle\int_{\mathbf{t}^{-1}x}d\mu(g)
be a proper normalised Haar system of
G
. Then the maps

\eta:C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M})\rightarrow C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet-1}(F_{G};C_{M})

given by

\eta f(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet-1})=(-1)^{\bullet}\textstyle\int_{\mathbf{s}g_{\bullet-1}}f(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet-1},g)

define a contraction homotopy
[\delta,\eta]=1
in degrees
\bullet\geq 2
.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward computation, entirely analogous to the proof that groupoid cohomology with trivial coefficients of
G
vanishes.
∎

6.2.
Splittings of fat extensions and trivialisations

In this section we clarify some aspects of “split” fat extensions. Naturally, this discussion comes with several clarifications of the correspondences between
2
-term ruths, VB-groupoids and fat extensions. Following Section
4.3
, we define fat splittings as follows.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. A
fat splitting
of
F_{G}
is a map

h:F_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})

that is invariant: for all
H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}\in F_{G}
,
58
58

58

Recall that
h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})

h(H_{g})-h(H_{g}^{\prime})=-h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})=-H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot H_{g}^{-1}

and unital: for all
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

h(h_{x})=h_{x}.

It is called multiplicative if

h(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})v=h(H_{g_{1}})H_{g_{2}}\cdot v-h(H_{g_{1}})\partial h(H_{g_{2}})v+H_{g_{1}}\cdot h(H_{g_{2}})v.

A disclaimer is that, although the similarity will become apparent, the multiplicativity condition is different than the multiplicativity condition for tensors (that appears in Section
7
).

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
V_{G}
be a VB-groupoid. The association

\{\textnormal{Cleavages on $V_{G}$}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat splittings on $\widehat{V}_{G}$}\}\qquad\Sigma\mapsto h_{\Sigma}(H_{g})\coloneq r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}-\Sigma_{g})

sets a one-to-one correspondence between (unital) cleavages of
V_{G}
and (unital) fat splittings. Moreover, for all
H_{g_{1}},H_{g_{2}}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
composable,

h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})v=R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2})v+H_{g_{1}}\cdot h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})v-h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})\partial h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})v+h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})H_{g_{2}}\cdot v,

so under this correspondence, flat cleavages correspond to fat multiplicative splittings.

Proof.

As mentioned before, an invariant map
h
defines a cleavage
\Sigma
by setting

\Sigma_{g}v=-r_{g}h(H_{g})v+H_{g}v

where
H_{g}
is arbitrary. It is readily verified that the two constructions are mutually inverse. The last statement follows by a direct computation:

\displaystyle r_{g_{1}g_{2}}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})v

\displaystyle+h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})H_{g_{2}}\cdot v)=H_{g_{1}}(H_{g_{2}}\cdot v-R_{1}(g_{2})v)\cdot(H_{g_{2}}v-\Sigma_{g_{2}}v)

\displaystyle+(H_{g_{1}}-\Sigma_{g_{1}})(H_{g_{2}}\cdot v)

\displaystyle=(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})v+r_{g_{2}}(H_{g_{1}}-\Sigma_{g_{1}})(H_{g_{2}}\cdot v-R_{1}(g_{2})v)-\Sigma_{g_{1}}\cdot\Sigma_{g_{2}}v

\displaystyle=r_{g_{1}g_{2}}(h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})+h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})\partial h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})-R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2}))

where we added
\pm\Sigma_{g_{1}}\cdot\Sigma_{g_{2}}v
in the second equality and
\pm\Sigma_{g_{1}g_{2}}
in the last.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

Notice that, given
h_{\Sigma}
as above, then, for all
H_{g_{1}},H_{g_{2}}\in\widehat{V}_{G}
composable,

\displaystyle h(H_{g_{1}})\partial h(H_{g_{2}})v

\displaystyle=h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})H_{g_{2}}\cdot v-h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})R_{1}(g_{2})v

\displaystyle=H_{g_{1}}\cdot h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})v-R_{1}(g_{1})h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})v.

Therefore,
h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})
equals
h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{1}})\cdot h_{\Sigma}(H_{g_{2}})
, where the product is defined as in Section
4.3
.

Remark 6.5
.

Another way to arrive at the correct notion of fat splitting is Proposition
3.17
of Section
3.5
. Namely, since

\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\cong(\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G})/H(V_{M},C_{M})\qquad H_{g}\mapsto(-h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime}),H_{g}^{\prime}),

we see that a cleavage
\Sigma
, i.e. a map
\Sigma:G\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
, can also be seen as a fat splitting
h
. Indeed,
h
defines the map

(h,1):\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}

and, by invariance, this last map descends to a map
G\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
, which is
\Sigma
if
h=h_{\Sigma}
. The failure for
h
to be multiplicative measures the failure of this last map being a Lie category map with respect to the structure of Lie category on
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})
as defined in Section
3.5
.

Remark 6.6
.

We can translate a fat splitting
h
to a map

\widetilde{h}:\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\mathbf{t}^{*}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})=\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})

by using the representation:

\widetilde{h}(H_{g})v\coloneq h(H_{g})(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v)

Then,
\widetilde{h}
satisfies the invariance condition

\widetilde{h}(H_{g})H_{g}\cdot v-\widetilde{h}(H_{g}^{\prime})H_{g}^{\prime}\cdot v=-h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})v

(such that
\widetilde{h}(h_{x})=0
for all
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
). We can rewrite the condition for
h
to be multiplicative in terms of
\widetilde{h}
as

\widetilde{h}(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})v=H_{g_{1}}\cdot\widetilde{h}(H_{g_{2}})H_{g_{1}}^{-1}\cdot v-\widetilde{h}(H_{g_{1}})\partial(H_{g_{1}}\cdot\widetilde{h}(H_{g_{2}})H_{g_{1}}^{-1}\cdot v)+\widetilde{h}(H_{g_{1}})v.

Using the following terminology, it is straightforward to see that a fat multiplicative splitting “trivialises” the associated fat extension.

Definition 6.7
.

Suppose we are given a cochain complex representation of
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The trivial fat extension is the fat extension corresponding to the fat groupoid

\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\times_{M}G\rightrightarrows M

with
\widehat{\mathbf{t}}(h_{\mathbf{t}g},g)=\mathbf{t}g
,
\widehat{\mathbf{s}}(h_{\mathbf{t}g},g)=\mathbf{s}g
, and multiplication and inversion are given by
59
59

59

Here,
G
acts on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
by conjugation.

(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}},g_{1})\cdot(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}},g_{2})=(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}\cdot(g_{1}\cdot h_{\mathbf{t}g_{2}}),g_{1}g_{2})\qquad(h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}},g_{1})^{-1}=(g_{1}^{-1}\cdot h_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}^{-1},g_{1}^{-1}).

The representation of
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\times_{M}G\rightrightarrows M
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is given by

(h_{\mathbf{t}g},g)\cdot c=(1+h_{\mathbf{t}g}\partial)(g\cdot c)\qquad(h_{\mathbf{t}g},g)\cdot v=(1+\partial h_{\mathbf{t}g})(g\cdot v).

A trivialisation of a fat extension is a cochain complex representation of
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and an isomorphism of fat extensions

F_{G}\cong\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\times_{M}G.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension. A fat multiplicative splitting
h
is equivalent to a trivialisation of
F_{G}
. More precisely, the correspondence is given by

F_{G}\cong\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})\times_{M}G\qquad H_{g}\mapsto(v\mapsto-h(H_{g})(H_{g}^{-1}\cdot v),g),

where the representation of
G
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is given by

g\cdot c\coloneq H_{g}\cdot c-h(H_{g})\partial c\qquad g\cdot v=H_{g}\cdot v-\partial h(H_{g})v.

To conclude, since the invariant cochains
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})
form an abstract ruth, a fat splitting splits the invariant cochains
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})
:

Proposition 6.9
.

The complex
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M})
is an abstract ruth. Moreover, writing
\mathcal{E}_{G}=C^{\bullet}(G;C_{M})\oplus C^{\bullet-1}(G;V_{M})
, a fat splitting
h
defines an isomorphism

\mathcal{E}_{G}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})\qquad(f_{0}^{\mathcal{E}},f_{1}^{\mathcal{E}})\mapsto(f_{0},f_{1})

by setting

\displaystyle f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\mathcal{E}}(g_{1},\dots,g_{\bullet})-h(H_{g_{1}})f_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle f_{1}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=f_{1}^{\mathcal{E}}(g_{2},\dots,g_{\bullet}).

The induced operators
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
on
\mathcal{E}_{G}
are given by

\displaystyle R_{1}(g)c

\displaystyle\coloneq H_{g}\cdot c-h(H_{g})\partial c\qquad R_{1}(g)v=H_{g}\cdot v-\partial h(H_{g})v

\displaystyle R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2})v

\displaystyle\coloneq h(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})v-h(H_{g_{1}})H_{g_{2}}\cdot v+h(H_{g_{1}})\partial h(H_{g_{2}})v-H_{g_{1}}\cdot h(H_{g_{2}})v.

Proof.

It is clear that the given map is a map of
CG
-modules. The inverse of the map is given by reading the equation the other way around (which makes sense by the properties (
4.4
) and (
4.5
)). The last statement follows from the above discussion in this section.
∎

6.3.
Fat extensions as a functor to the quasi general linear Lie
2
-groupoid

In this section, we will explain how fat extensions of
G
relate to “pseudofunctors” from
G
to a type of general linear
2
-groupoid as defined in
[
26
]
. Here, we will refer to that general linear Lie
2
-groupoid as the quasi-general linear Lie
2
-groupoid. It is a Lie
2
-groupoid that is not strict.

Remark 6.10
.

In the analogy we made in Remark
5.2
, we can relax “strictness” as follows. A Lie
2
-groupoid (so not necessarily strict) can similarly be described by a diagram
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}\rightrightarrows N
, but
H_{1}\rightrightarrows N
is only a groupoid “up to homotopy”. More precisely, we relax the conditions that
H_{2}\rightrightarrows H_{1}
and
H_{1}\rightrightarrows N
are Lie groupoids to them being possibly only Lie categories. Still,
g\in H_{1}
is assumed to have “quasi-inverses”
g^{-1}\in H_{1}
. The elements of
H_{2}
, which are invertible in the groupoid
H_{2}\rightrightarrows N
, can then again be interpreted as homotopies
h
that show two elements in
H_{1}
are homotopic. In particular, given
g\in H_{1}
, there will exist a
g^{-1}\in H_{1}
and homotopies in
H_{2}
that show
gg^{-1}
and
g^{-1}g
are homotopic to identity elements of
H_{1}
.

We will now explain what the quasi general linear groupoid is.

Example 6.11
.

Recall the general linear strict Lie
2
-groupoid from Example
5.3
. Relaxing the conditions for elements of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
to be isomorphisms to be quasi-isomorphisms (see Remark
6.10
), we obtain the quasi general linear Lie
2
-groupoid

\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\rightrightarrows M.

As observed in
[
26
]
, notice that
\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\subset\textnormal{End }C_{M}\times_{M\times M}\textnormal{End }V_{M}
is still an open subset. Indeed, given a cochain map
\Phi
, then the existence of maps

[\partial,\Phi^{-1}]=0\qquad 1-\Phi\Phi^{-1}=[\partial,h_{\Phi\Phi^{-1}}]\qquad 1-\Phi^{-1}\Phi=[\partial,h_{\Phi^{-1}\Phi}]

is equivalent to the existence of an isomorphism

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}&h_{\Phi\Phi^{-1}}\\
\partial&(\Phi^{V_{M}})^{-1}\end{pmatrix}:(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{y}\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{M})_{y}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}

(so the left column is fixed) with inverse given by

\begin{pmatrix}(\Phi^{C_{M}})^{-1}&-h_{\Phi^{-1}\Phi}\\
-\partial&\Phi^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}:(C_{M})_{y}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{y}

(where the bottom row is fixed). Alternatively,
\Phi
is a quasi-isomorphism if and only if

\ker\Phi^{C_{M}}\cap\ker\partial=0\qquad\im\Phi^{V_{M}}+\im\partial=V_{M}.

We then define
60
60

60

Here, the Lie category structure on
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\rightrightarrows\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
is given by

(\partial,h)\cdot(\partial,h^{\prime})\coloneq(\partial,h+h^{\prime}+h\partial h^{\prime}).

The Lie groupoid
\textnormal{Pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
form the invertible elements of this Lie category.

\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}\coloneq(\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\times_{M}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M}))\times_{M\times M}\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

as a fiber product of Lie categories. The product over
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
is as in Example
5.3
.

We can interpret the step to pseudofunctors efficiently using the fat category extension. A fat category extension
F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
comes with a Lie category map

F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

which defines the cochain complex representation. Now, a fat category splitting defines a map (see Section
6.2
)

R_{1}:G\rightarrow F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

which might fail to be a functor. Nevertheless, its image does indeed lie in
\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
, since, for all
g\in G
,

[\partial,R_{1}(g)]=0.

The failure for this map to define a functor can now be seen as a map

R_{2}:G^{(2)}\rightarrow\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}.

That it maps into
\textnormal{QGL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
is saying that, for all
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G^{(2)}
,

R_{1}(g_{1})R_{1}(g_{2})-R_{1}(g_{1}g_{2})=[\partial,R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2})].

The condition that, for all
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\in G^{(3)}
,

-R_{2}(g_{1}g_{2},g_{3})+R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2}g_{3})=-R_{1}(g_{1})R_{2}(g_{2},g_{3})+R_{2}(g_{1},g_{2})R_{1}(g_{3})

is then equivalent to the fact that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
together define a so-called pseudofunctor.

The functorial aspects of this construction are a bit subtle. It is proved in
[
26
]
that the category of (split)
2
-terms ruths over a fixed
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, together with
quasi-isomorphisms
of
2
-term ruths, is equivalent to the category of pseudofunctors together with lax-functors. A strict version of this correspondence is not discussed in
[
26
]
, but some more comments about this issue are made in that work.

As we already mentioned, a fat category splitting of a fat category extension turns it into a
weak representation
as in
[
62
]
, and such objects come with a natural notion of morphism that turns the correspondence into an equivalence of categories.

7.
Functorial aspects

We will introduce a morphism of fat extensions as a multiplicative tensor. This will be a first instance where we view a multiplicative tensor as an object that naturally occurs in the theory of fat extensions. This should therefore also be seen as part of the bigger project together with Joaõ Nuno Mestre and Luca Vitagliano
[
50
]
. A morphism of general linear PB-groupoids is similar in nature, but its properties can succinctly be described as
GL
-equivariance
. We introduce both types of morphisms through the language of VB-groupoids. For this reason, in introducing the correct notions of morphisms, we will come to see naturally how the respective categories are equivalent to the category of VB-groupoids.

A notational comment: throughout, we will denote a cochain map as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

but often we simply write
\Phi
. A VB-groupoid map is denoted by

\Phi=\Phi^{V_{G}}:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}.

We focus on maps over the identity map of
G
. Such a map is a groupoid map
V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
that is also linear (i.e. intertwines the scalar multiplications). It comes with a cochain map by setting

\Phi^{C_{M}}\coloneq\Phi^{V_{G}}|_{C_{M}}\qquad\Phi^{V_{M}}\coloneq\mathbf{s}\Phi^{V_{G}}|_{V_{M}}

where we used the natural inclusion

C_{M}\oplus V_{M}=V_{G}|_{M}\hookrightarrow V_{G}.

Remark 7.1
.

We restrict attention to VB-groupoid maps over the identity for exposition. In order to pass to more general maps (over general groupoid maps), notice that the category of VB-groupoids is naturally fibred, just as, for example, the category of vector bundles. That is, general VB-groupoid maps from
V_{G}
to
V_{H}
can be seen as VB-groupoid maps

V_{G}\rightarrow\varphi^{*}V_{H}

over the identity, where
\varphi:G\rightarrow H
is a Lie groupoid map, and
\varphi^{*}V_{H}
is the pullback VB-groupoid. The other categories we will consider (of fat extensions and of general linear PB-groupoids) are fibred analogously, and passing to more general morphisms is therefore straightforward and omitted.

7.1.
Morphisms of fat extensions

Our aim is now to describe, given a map of VB-groupoids
\Phi=\Phi^{V_{G}}:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
, a type of morphism
f=f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{G,1}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G,2}
that consists of exactly the same data. However, observe that there is no obvious groupoid map
\widehat{V}_{G,1}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G,2}
in general. Rather, we obtain a notion of “related” elements. We therefore aid our discussion with the following intermediate definition.

Definition 7.2
.

Given a VB-groupoid
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
, we say
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
and
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
are
\Phi
-related if the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g,1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g,2}}

commutes. In this case, we write
H_{g,1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g,2}
.

Given
(g,g^{\prime})\in G^{(2)}
,
H_{g,1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g,2}
and
H_{g^{\prime},1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g^{\prime},2}
, then also
H_{g,1}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g,2}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},2}
. But for our purposes such a notion of “multiplicative relation” is too narrow. For example, there will often exist
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
not
\Phi
-related to any
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
.
61
61

61

For example, if there is a
v\in\ker\Phi\subset(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}
and
H_{g,1}
such that
\Phi H_{g,1}v\neq 0
.
In general, however, given
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
and
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
, we can always produce the map

(7.1)

f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2}):(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad v\mapsto r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g,2}\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{V_{G}}H_{g,1}v)

that is zero precisely when
H_{g,1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g,2}
. The argument that the
\Phi
-relation is multiplicative can be generalised to the following property of
f_{\Phi}
:

Lemma 7.3
.

Given a VB-groupoid map
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
,

f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}),

defined as above in (
7.1
), satisfies, for all
(H_{g,1},H_{g^{\prime},1})\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}^{(2)}
and
(H_{g,2},H_{g^{\prime},2})\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}^{(2)}
,

f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},1},H_{g,2}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},2})v=f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})H_{g^{\prime},1}\cdot v+H_{g,2}\cdot f_{\Phi}(H_{g^{\prime},1},H_{g^{\prime},2})v.

Proof.

We compute directly:

\displaystyle r_{gg^{\prime}}f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},1},H_{g,2}\cdot H_{g^{\prime},2})v

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}(\mathbf{t}H_{g^{\prime},2}\Phi v)\cdot H_{g^{\prime},2}\Phi v-\Phi(H_{g,1}(\mathbf{t}H_{g^{\prime},1}v))\cdot\Phi(H_{g^{\prime},1}v)

\displaystyle=r_{g^{\prime}}(H_{g,2}(\Phi(\mathbf{t}H_{g^{\prime},1}v))-\Phi(H_{g,1}(\mathbf{t}H_{g^{\prime},1}v)))+

\displaystyle\phantom{=}+H_{g,2}\mathbf{t}(H_{g^{\prime},2}\Phi v-\Phi H_{g^{\prime},1}v)\cdot(H_{g^{\prime},2}\Phi v-\Phi H_{g^{\prime},1}v)

\displaystyle=r_{gg^{\prime}}f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})(H_{g^{\prime},1}\cdot v)+r_{gg^{\prime}}(H_{g,2}\cdot f_{\Phi}(H_{g^{\prime},1},H_{g^{\prime},2})v).

This proves the statement.
∎

Aside from this multiplicativity property, which generalises the multiplicativity of the
\Phi
-relation, we also need a generalisation of the following fact. If
H_{g,1}\sim_{\Phi}H_{g,2}
, then the cochain map
\Phi=(\Phi^{C_{M}},\Phi^{V_{M}})
intertwines the actions by
H_{g,1}
and
H_{g,2}
, respectively:

H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{C_{M}}c=\Phi^{C_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot c)\qquad H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{V_{M}}v=\Phi^{V_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot v).

That is, we observe next that the map
f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})
can actually be interpreted as the homotopy that measures the failure of
\Phi
to intertwine the actions by
H_{g,1}
and
H_{g,2}
:

Lemma 7.4
.

Given a VB-groupoid map
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
,

f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}),

defined as above in (
7.1
), satisfies, for all
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
and
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
,

\displaystyle f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})\partial c

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{C_{M}}c-\Phi^{C_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot c),

\displaystyle\partial f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{V_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot v).

There is one more important property that relate the map
f_{\Phi}
and the cochain map
\Phi
: invariance. To introduce it, it is helpful to understand how one recovers
\Phi^{V_{G}}
from
f_{\Phi}
and the cochain map
\Phi
.

Recall that an element, say,
H_{g,1}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
, defines a linear isomorphism

r_{g}+H_{g,1}:(C_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G,1})_{g}\qquad(c,v)\mapsto c\cdot 0_{g}+H_{g,1}v.

We then have (for future reference, we write
f=f_{\Phi}
)

(7.2)

\Phi_{g}^{V_{G}}(r_{g}c+H_{g,1}v)=r_{g}(\Phi^{C_{M}}(c)-f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v)+H_{g,2}\Phi^{V_{M}}(v).

Interpreting
f_{\Phi}
as a “correcting term” in this way points to the missing piece of information, invariance, that
f_{\Phi}
and the cochain map
\Phi
satisfy.

Lemma 7.5
.

Given a VB-groupoid map
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
,

f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{G,1}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})

defined as above in (
7.1
) satisfies, for all
H_{g,1},H_{g,1}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G,1}
and
H_{g,2},H_{g,2}^{\prime}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
,

f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v-f_{\Phi}(H_{g,1}^{\prime},H_{g,2}^{\prime})v=h_{2}(H_{g,2},H_{g,2}^{\prime})\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{C_{M}}h_{1}(H_{g,1},H_{g,1}^{\prime})v

where

h_{1}(H_{g,1},H_{g,1}^{\prime})v=r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g,1}^{\prime}v-H_{g,1}v),

and
h_{2}
is defined similarly.

Recognise that, in the above lemma,
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
are equal to
f_{\Phi}
if we take
\Phi
to be the respective identity maps. The similarity with the invariance condition that appeared in Section
4.4
is, of course, not coincidental. In Remark
7.8
we clarify this connection further.

We organise the properties that
(f,\Phi)
should satisfy, in order to set the correspondence, in the following way.

Definition 7.6
.

Consider fat extensions
F_{G,1}
and
F_{G,2}
of
G
, over
C_{M,1}\rightarrow V_{M,1}
and
C_{M,2}\rightarrow V_{M,2}
, respectively. Consider a pair
(f,\Phi)
, where
\Phi=(\Phi^{C_{M}},\Phi^{V_{M}})
are two vector bundle maps

C_{M,1}\xrightarrow{\Phi^{C_{M}}}C_{M,2}\qquad V_{M,1}\xrightarrow{\Phi^{V_{M}}}V_{M,2}

and
f
is a smooth map

f:F_{G,1}\times_{G}F_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M,1},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M,2}).

Now,
(f,\Phi)
is a morphism
F_{G,1}\rightarrow F_{G,2}
of fat extensions if it is invariant:

f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v-f(H_{g,1}^{\prime},H_{g,2}^{\prime})v=h_{2}(H_{g,2},H_{g,2}^{\prime})\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{C_{M}}h_{1}(H_{g,1},H_{g,1}^{\prime})v

and multiplicative: this means that
\Phi
is a cochain map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

that

\displaystyle f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})\partial c

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{C_{M}}c-\Phi^{C_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot c),

\displaystyle\partial f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{V_{M}}(H_{g,1}\cdot v),

and that

f(H_{g,1}\cdot H_{h,1},H_{g,2}\cdot H_{h,2})v=f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})(H_{h,1}\cdot v)+H_{g,2}\cdot f(H_{h,1},H_{h,2})v.

Remark 7.7
.

We often simplify and write a map of fat extensions
(f,\Phi)
by
f
, so we leave
\Phi
implicit from the notation. Similar as happens for the invariant cochains we described in Section
4.4
(see Remark
4.13
),
f
usually determines the cochain map
\Phi
by invariance. But, aside from suppressing
\Phi
from the notation sometimes, we keep track of
\Phi
as part of the data.

Remark 7.8
.

The point of this remark is to clarify why we used the term “multiplicative” in the definition above. In fact, we will argue here that we can think of a morphism of fat extensions as a multiplicative tensor.

To do this, notice that we can translate a map of fat extensions
f
so that it takes values in

\mathbf{t}^{*}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})=\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{t}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M}).

Indeed, simply replace
f
with

\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2}):(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}\qquad v\mapsto f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})(H_{g,1}^{-1}\cdot v).

In terms of
\widetilde{f}
, the invariance condition becomes

\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})(H_{g,1}\cdot v)-\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1}^{\prime},H_{g,2}^{\prime})(H_{g,1}^{\prime}\cdot v)=h_{2}(H_{g,2},H_{g,2}^{\prime})\Phi^{V_{M}}v-\Phi^{C_{M}}h_{1}(H_{g,1},H_{g,1}^{\prime})v,

and the two multiplicativity conditions involving
f
translate to

\displaystyle\partial\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{V_{M}}(H_{g,1}^{-1}\cdot v)-\Phi^{V_{M}}v

\displaystyle\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})\partial c

\displaystyle=H_{g,2}\cdot\Phi^{C_{M}}(H_{g,1}^{-1}\cdot c)-\Phi^{C_{M}}c

and

\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1}\cdot H_{h,1},H_{g,2}\cdot H_{h,2})v=\widetilde{f}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v+H_{g,2}\cdot\widetilde{f}(H_{h,1},H_{h,2})(H_{g,1}^{-1}\cdot v).

This way,
f
(or rather
(\widetilde{f},\Phi)
) can be thought of as an “invariant”
1
-cocycle in (the
3
-term ruth corresponding to) the cochain complex representation

\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},C_{M,2})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},V_{M,2})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},V_{M,2})

of the groupoid
F_{G,1}\times_{G}F_{G,2}
, whose groupoid structure is defined componentwise. Here, the cochain complex representation can be thought of as a tensor product of the cochain complexes

V_{M,1}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M,1}^{*}\qquad C_{M,2}\rightarrow V_{M,2}

with the action defined “componentwise”. Studying more general tensor products this way is part of the objective of the work in progress
[
50
]
.

Before we move on, we discuss the composition of morphisms.

Definition 7.9
.

Given maps of fat extensions

F_{G,1}\times_{G}F_{G,2}\xrightarrow{f_{21}}\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M,1},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M,2})\qquad F_{G,2}\times_{G}F_{G,3}\xrightarrow{f_{32}}\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M,2},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M,3}),

over cochain maps
\Phi_{21}
and
\Phi_{32}
, respectively, the composition
f_{31}=f_{32}\circ f_{21}
over
\Phi_{31}=\Phi_{32}\circ\Phi_{21}
is given by

f_{31}(H_{g,1},H_{g,3})=f_{32}(H_{g,2},H_{g,3})\Phi_{21}^{V_{M}}+\Phi_{32}^{C_{M}}f_{21}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2}),

where
H_{g,2}\in F_{G,2}
is arbitrary.

Remark 7.10
.

The identity map
F_{G}\rightarrow F_{G}
is the identity cochain map between
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
and itself, together with the map

h:F_{G}\times_{G}F_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},\mathbf{t}^{*}C_{M})\qquad h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})=h_{\mathbf{t}g}(H_{g}\cdot v),

where
h_{\mathbf{t}g}
is unique with the property that

h_{\mathbf{t}g}\cdot H_{g}=H_{g}^{\prime}.

If
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
for a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
, we recover the map

h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})=r_{g^{-1}}(H_{g}^{\prime}-H_{g})

which we already mentioned in Remark
4.15
.

The following Remark should be compared to Remark
3.33
, where we discussed the “inclusion” map of a fat subextension and the “projection” map of a fat quotient.

Remark 7.11
.

We observe here that
f^{-1}
is a left/right inverse to
f
if and only if

f^{-1}(H_{g,2},H_{g,1})=-\Phi^{-1}f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})\Phi^{-1},

where
\Phi^{-1}
