is a left/right inverse to the cochain map
\Phi
underlying
f
. In this case, the map that sends
H_{g,1}\in F_{G,1}
to the unique
H_{g,2}\in F_{G,2}
for which
62
62

62

Explicitly,
H_{g,2}
is given by
H_{g,2}^{\prime}\cdot h_{\mathbf{s}g}
, where
H_{g,2}^{\prime}\in F_{G,2}
is arbitrary, and

h_{\mathbf{s}g}\coloneq(H_{g,2}^{\prime})^{-1}\cdot f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2}^{\prime})(\Phi^{V_{M}})^{-1}.

f(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})=0

is a groupoid immersion/submersion
F_{G,1}\rightarrow F_{G,2}
that we can fit into a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Conversely, all such commutative diagrams arise in this way; that is, those for which the vertical map on the left is given by

\textnormal{H}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})\qquad h_{x}\mapsto\Phi^{C_{M}}h_{x}(\Phi^{V_{M}})^{-1}

if
\Phi^{-1}
a left inverse of
\Phi
, and

\textnormal{H}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})\rightarrow\textnormal{H}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(\Phi^{C_{M}})^{-1}h_{x}\Phi^{V_{M}}

if
\Phi^{-1}
a right inverse of
\Phi
.

There are of course many other classes of maps of interest. For example, the class of maps for which the underlying cochain map is a quasi-isomorphism. Such maps correspond to VB-Morita maps (as can be inferred from
[
25
]
). This will be further clarified and explored in
[
44
]
.

To conclude this section, we summarise:

Proposition 7.12
.

Let
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
be a VB-groupoid map. Then,

f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{G,1}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G,2},

defined as in (
7.1
), is a morphism of fat extensions. Moreover, the assignment
\Phi\mapsto f_{\Phi}
respects composition.

Proof.

Only the last statement is left to be verified. Given
\Phi_{21}
and
\Phi_{32}
VB-groupoid maps, we compute: any
H_{g,2}\in\widehat{V}_{G,2}
gives

\displaystyle r_{g}f_{31}(H_{g,1},H_{g,3})

\displaystyle=H_{g,3}(\Phi_{32}\Phi_{21}v)-\Phi_{32}\Phi_{21}(H_{g,1}v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{32}(H_{g,2}\Phi_{21}v-\Phi_{21}(H_{g,1}v))+(H_{g,3}\Phi_{32}-\Phi_{32}H_{g,2})\Phi_{21}v

\displaystyle=r_{g}\Phi_{32}f_{21}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})v+r_{g}f_{32}(H_{g,2},H_{g,3})\Phi_{21}v.

This proves the statement.
∎

Starting with a map of fat extensions
f
, the invariance of
f
makes sure the map
\Phi
, defined as in (
7.2
), is well-defined. The multiplicativity of
f
ensures this map
\Phi
is a VB-groupoid map.

We arrived at the main theorem of this work.

Theorem 7.13
.

The functor

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad V_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\quad\Phi\mapsto f_{\Phi}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

In this section we established the (fully faithful) functoriality, and in Section
4
we showed the essential surjectivity.
∎

Remark 7.14
.

The proof of
[
25
]
that the functor

\{\textnormal{Split $2$-term ruths}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}

is an equivalence can now be understood as follows. We call
\{\textnormal{Split VB-groupoids}\}
the category of VB-groupoids equipped with a cleavage. Then the above functor decomposes as

\{\textnormal{Split $2$-term ruths}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Split VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}

where the latter equivalence is given by the forgetful functor. To see how the first equivalence works, observe that, from a VB-groupoid map
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
, we can construct the map
f=f_{\Phi}
as above on
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\times_{G}\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
. So, cleavages
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
of
V_{G,1}
and
V_{G,2}
, respectively, define a map

\mu_{g}\coloneq f(\Sigma_{g,1},\Sigma_{g,2}):(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}.

In the first multiplicativity condition involving
f
(see Definition
7.6
) we can replace
f
with
\mu
, but the second condition is true only up to the commutator
[R_{2}(g,g^{\prime}),\Phi]
:
63
63

63

We simply wrote the associated split ruths of
V_{G,1}
and
V_{G,2}
using the same notation.

\mu_{gg^{\prime}}v=\mu_{g}R_{1}(g^{\prime})v+R_{1}(g)\mu_{h}v+R_{2}(g,g^{\prime})\Phi v-\Phi R_{2}(g,g^{\prime})v.

This precisely means the pair
(\Phi,\mu)
defines a ruth map, and, as above in (
7.2
), the equation

\Phi^{V_{G}}(r_{g}c+\Sigma_{g,1}v)=r_{g}(\Phi^{C_{M}}(c)-\mu_{g}v)+\Sigma_{g,2}\Phi^{V_{M}}v

sets a one-to-one correspondence between VB-groupoid maps and ruth maps.

The notions of morphisms for fat extensions and for fat category extensions are formally identical. Therefore, it is readily verified that we also obtain:

Theorem 7.15
.

The functor

\{\textnormal{Fat category extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad F_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}\rightarrow F_{G}\quad f\mapsto f|_{F_{G}\times F_{G}}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Remark 7.16
.

Similarly, we can establish equivalences of the form

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto V(F_{G})

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Split $2$-term ruths}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Split fat extensions}\}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{G}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{G}

but we will not go any further into these details here.

7.2.
Morphisms of general linear PB-groupoids

To understand maps between general linear PB-groupoids, we first go through the equivalence of categories between ordinary vector bundles and ordinary general linear principal bundles. Doing so, we will already come across some of the most essential ingredients to understand the more general case.

7.2.1.
Morphisms of general linear principal bundles

Let
E_{1}\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle of rank
k_{1}
,
E_{2}\rightarrow M
be a vector bundle of rank
k_{2}
, and consider a vector bundle map
E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
over
M
. If we then consider two frames over the same basepoint
x\in M
, one of
E_{1}
and one of
E_{2}
, we obtain the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

So, a linear map
E_{1}\rightarrow E_{2}
produces a map

f_{\Phi}:\textnormal{Fr }E_{1}\times_{M}\textnormal{Fr }E_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}})\qquad f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\coloneq\phi_{2}^{-1}\Phi\phi_{1}.

This map has an additional special property, which leads to the following definition.

Definition 7.17
.

A morphism of principal
GL
-bundles is a map

f:P_{1}\times_{M}P_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}})

that is
GL
-equivariant: given
p_{1}\in P_{1}
,
p_{2}\in P_{2}
,
\Psi_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}(k_{1})
and
\Psi_{2}\in\textnormal{GL}(k_{2})
, we have

f(p_{1}\Psi_{1},p_{2}\Psi_{2})=\Psi_{2}^{-1}f(p_{1},p_{2})\Psi_{1}.

Moreover, composition
f_{31}=f_{32}\circ f_{21}:P_{1}\rightarrow P_{3}
of morphisms
f_{21}:P_{1}\rightarrow P_{2}
and
f_{32}:P_{2}\rightarrow P_{3}
is given by

f_{31}(p_{1},p_{3})=f_{32}(p_{2},p_{3})\circ f_{21}(p_{1},p_{2})

where
p_{2}\in P_{2}
is arbitrary.

The term ”equivariant” is justified for
\textnormal{GL}(k_{1})
and
\textnormal{GL}(k_{2})
act canonically on
\textnormal{Fr }E_{1}\times_{M}\textnormal{Fr }E_{2}
and also on
\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}})
. The
GL
-equivariance condition can then literally be understood as the equivariance with respect to these actions.

Remark 7.18
.

The identity map
P\rightarrow P
is the map

P\times_{M}P\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbb{R}^{k})

which assigns to
(p_{1},p_{2})\in P\times_{M}P
the unique
\Psi\in\textnormal{GL}(k)
with the property that
p_{1}=p_{2}\Psi
. In particular,
f
is an isomorphism if and only if
f
maps into
\textnormal{GL}(k)
, for then

f^{-1}(p_{2},p_{1})=f(p_{1},p_{2})^{-1}

where the inverse on the right hand side is taken in
\textnormal{GL}(k)
. In this case, the map sending
p_{1}\in P_{1}
to the unique
p_{2}\in P_{2}
for which

f(p_{1},p_{2})=1

is an equivariant isomorphism
P_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}P_{2}
. Conversely, all such equivariant isomorphisms arise in this way.

With the correct notion of morphism of general linear principal bundles at hand, we now prove:

Proposition 7.19
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{Vector bundles}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Principal $\textnormal{GL}$-bundles}\}\qquad E\mapsto\textnormal{Fr }E\qquad\Phi\mapsto f_{\Phi}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

Suppose we are given a map

\textnormal{Fr }E_{1}\times_{M}\textnormal{Fr }E_{2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}})

that is
GL
-equivariant. We can then define a linear map
E_{1,x}\rightarrow E_{2,x}
by choosing any two frames:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{1,x}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{2,x}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

By the equivariance, this map is independent of the chosen frames. That we set a true one-to-one correspondence now is readily verified.
∎

We now move to understanding morphisms of general linear PB-groupoids. Although a natural guess arises from the above discussion, to avoid mistakes, it is especially useful to simply go through the above one-to-one correspondence in the more general setup. We start with setting the equivalence between general linear PB-groupoids and VB-groupoid as it seems the most appropriate following the above discussion. However, as we will see, the equivalence on the level of morphisms between general linear PB-groupoids and fat extensions is set in a similar way.

7.2.2.
Morphisms of general linear PB-groupoids

Recall that the total space of the associated PB-groupoid of a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
is the following space of frames:

\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}=\{\phi_{g}:(C_{M})_{x}\oplus(V_{M})_{x}\xrightarrow{\sim}(V_{G})_{g}\mid\mathbf{s}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(C_{M})_{x}}=0,\mathbf{t}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}},\mathbf{s}_{g}\phi_{g}|_{(V_{M})_{x}}\in\textnormal{GL}(V_{M})\}

Given a VB-groupoid map
V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
over
G
, and picking one such frame
\phi_{1}
for
\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G,1}
and
\phi_{2}
for
\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G,2}
, we obtain the following diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Before we move on, observe that the resulting map

(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}

is not an element of
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})
, but rather of

\textnormal{Lin}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})

where, for
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
vector bundles over
M
,

\textnormal{Lin}(E_{1},E_{2})\coloneq\{E_{1,x}\rightarrow E_{2,y}\mid x,y\in M\}

denotes the vector bundle over
M\times M
of linear maps between fibers of
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
. We often denote elements of
\textnormal{Lin}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})
by blockmatrices, and use

\textnormal{U}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})

to mean upper triangular blockmatrices. The reason for introducing this subbundle is because the maps from above are of this form, for
\mathbf{s}\phi_{2}=0
. We write the components of the above maps now (suggestively) as follows:

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})&f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\\
0&\Phi(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\end{pmatrix}(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}.

Up to conjugation by frames,
\Phi(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
is precisely the cochain map from
C_{M,1}\rightarrow V_{M,1}
to
C_{M,2}\rightarrow V_{M,2}
. In analogy with Proposition
7.19
, this does not come as a surprise. Rests to consider the map
f_{\Phi}:(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\rightarrow(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}
. To understand its essential property, we compute:

Proposition 7.20
.

Let
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
be a VB-groupoid map, and consider
\Phi(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
and
f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})
defined as above. Then, given
\Psi_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})_{M}
composable with
\phi_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
and
\Psi_{2}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})_{M}
composable with
\phi_{2}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
, we have

(7.3)

\Phi(\phi_{1}\cdot\Psi_{1},\phi_{2}\cdot\Psi_{2})=\Psi_{2}^{-1}\Phi(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\Psi_{1},

and, given
(h_{1},\Psi_{1})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})_{G}
composable with
\phi_{1}\in P_{G}
and
(h_{2},\Psi_{2})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})_{G}
composable with
\phi_{2}\in P_{G}
, we have
64
64

64

The differentials
\partial_{1}
and
\partial_{2}
that appear are the suppressed differentials in the elements
(h_{1},\Psi_{1})
and
(h_{2},\Psi_{2})
.

\displaystyle f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\Psi_{1}),\phi_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\Psi_{2}))=

(7.4)

\displaystyle=(\Psi_{2}^{C_{M}})^{-1}(1+h_{2}\partial_{2})^{-1}(f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})+\Phi^{C_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})h_{1}-h_{2}\Phi^{V_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2}))\Psi_{1}^{V_{M}}.

Proof.

We prove (
7.20
). Using the matrix notation we developed, the map
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

that we considered above is given by

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})&f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

The left hand side of (
7.20
) corresponds to the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{y_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{y_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

which is the map

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}(\phi_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\partial_{1},\Psi_{1}),\phi_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\partial_{2},\Psi_{2}))&f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\partial_{1},\Psi_{1}),\phi_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\partial_{2},\Psi_{2}))\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}(\phi_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\partial_{1},\Psi_{1}),\phi_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\partial_{2},\Psi_{2}))\end{pmatrix}.

But, on the other hand, the diagram also represents the composition

\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{2}\partial_{2})\Psi_{2}^{C_{M}}&h_{2}\Psi_{2}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{2}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})&f_{\Phi}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{1}\partial_{1})\Psi_{1}^{C_{M}}&h_{1}\Psi_{1}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{1}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}.

This proves the statement.
∎

Observe that we can view the last matrix multiplication in the above proof as describing two right
2
-actions, one of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})_{G}
and one of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})_{G}
(see Remark
5.6
). That is, these
2
-actions of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})_{G}
and of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})_{G}
— the two matrix multiplications — are well-defined
2
-actions on
\textnormal{U}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})
. The above property of such maps

\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G,1}\times_{G}\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{U}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})

can then literally be interpreted as
GL
-equivariance.

Definition 7.21
.

A morphism of general linear PB-groupoids is a map

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}&f_{\Phi}\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}:P_{G,1}\times_{G}P_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{U}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})

that is
GL
-equivariant; that is, given
(h_{1},\Psi_{1})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})_{G}
composable with
p_{1}\in P_{G}
and
(h_{2},\Psi_{2})\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})_{G}
composable with
p_{2}\in P_{G}
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}(p_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\Psi_{1}),p_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\Psi_{2}))&f_{\Phi}(p_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\Psi_{1}),p_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\Psi_{2}))\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}(p_{1}\cdot(h_{1},\Psi_{1}),p_{2}\cdot(h_{2},\Psi_{2}))\end{pmatrix}=

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{2}\partial_{2})\Psi_{2}^{C_{M}}&h_{2}\Psi_{2}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{2}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}(p_{1},p_{2})&f_{\Phi}(p_{1},p_{2})\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}(p_{1},p_{2})\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}(1+h_{1}\partial_{1})\Psi_{1}^{C_{M}}&h_{1}\Psi_{1}^{V_{M}}\\
0&\Psi_{1}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}.

Starting with a pair
(\Phi,f_{\Phi})
that is
GL
-equivariant, we can define
\Phi:V_{G,1}\rightarrow V_{G,2}
by picking, for all
g\in G
, any two frames and using the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

that
(\Phi,f_{\Phi})
induces. The resulting map
\Phi_{g}:(V_{G,1})_{g}\rightarrow(V_{G,2})_{g}
is independent of chosen frames by
GL
-equivariance. So, similar to Proposition
7.19
, we have:

Theorem 7.22
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\qquad V_{G}\mapsto\textnormal{GL}_{\mathbf{s}}V_{G}\qquad\Phi^{V_{G}}\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{C_{M}}&f_{\Phi}\\
0&\Phi^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

In this section we established the (fully faithful) functoriality, and in Section
5
we showed the essential surjectivity.
∎

Remark 7.23
.

Analogous to Remark
7.18
, the identity map
P_{G}\rightarrow P_{G}
of a PB-groupoid
P_{G}
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is the map

P_{G}\times_{G}P_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{U}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M},C_{M}\oplus V_{M})

which assigns to
(p_{1},p_{2})\in P_{G}\times_{G}P_{G}
the unique (matrix representation of)
(h,\Psi)\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})_{G}
with the property that
p_{1}=p_{2}\cdot(h,\Psi)
. In particular,
f
is an isomorphism if and only if
f
maps into
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M})
, for then

f^{-1}(p_{2},p_{1})=f(p_{1},p_{2})^{-1}

where the inverse on the right hand side is taken in
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M}\oplus V_{M})
. In this case, the map sending
p_{1}\in P_{G,1}
to the unique
p_{2}\in P_{G,2}
for which

f(p_{1},p_{2})=1

is an equivariant isomorphism
P_{1}\xrightarrow{\sim}P_{2}
. Conversely, all such equivariant isomorphisms arise in this way.

7.2.3.
Morphisms of general linear PB-groupoids using fat extensions

Given a map of general linear PB-groupoids

\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}^{C_{M}}&f_{\textnormal{PB}}\\
0&\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}^{V_{M}}\end{pmatrix}:P_{G,1}\times_{G}P_{G,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{U}(C_{M,1}\oplus V_{M,1},C_{M,2}\oplus V_{M,2})

the cochain map
\Phi
from
C_{M,1}\rightarrow V_{M,1}
to
C_{M,2}\rightarrow V_{M,2}
over
x\in M
can simply be recovered as

\Phi_{x}\coloneq\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}(1_{1_{x}},1_{1_{x}}).

But we can also pick any other elements
\Psi_{1}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})
and
\Psi_{2}\in\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2})
over
x
, for then we have

\Phi_{x}=\Psi_{2}\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}(\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2})\Psi_{1}^{-1}.

by
GL
-equivariance.
65
65

65

The identification we use here uses Proposition
5.12
.
Reading this equation the other way around, given
\Phi
, we can generate the map
\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}
from the cochain map
\Phi
by imposing
GL
-equivariance.

Now, if we realise
P_{G,1}
and
P_{G,2}
through their associated fat extensions; that is, writing

P_{G,1}=F_{G,1}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,1},V_{M,1})\qquad P_{G,2}=F_{G,2}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M,2},V_{M,2}),

then we can similarly recover the map of fat extensions as

f_{\textnormal{fat}}(H_{g,1},H_{g,2})\coloneq f_{\textnormal{PB}}(H_{g,1},1_{\mathbf{s}g},H_{g,2},1_{\mathbf{s}g}).

A straightforward computation shows that
f_{\textnormal{fat}}
is indeed a map of fat extensions. On the other hand, we can generate the map of general linear PB-groupoids
f_{\textnormal{PB}}
from the map of fat extensions
f_{\textnormal{fat}}
similar to how we generated
\Phi_{\textnormal{PB}}
from
\Phi
. We therefore obtain:

Theorem 7.24
.

The functor

\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto F_{G}\ltimes\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})\qquad f_{\textnormal{fat}}\mapsto f_{\textnormal{PB}}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Proof.

In this section we established the (fully faithful) functoriality, and in Section
5
we showed the essential surjectivity.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

It might be worthwhile to realise that, when before we used VB-groupoids, we built the map of PB-groupoids using the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

But if we go through the composition of functors

\{\textnormal{VB-groupoids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{General linear PB-groupoids}\},

we, instead, build the map of PB-groupoids as follows:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where we can think of the middle vertical map as taking the right-most horizontal maps as input. The end result is the same, because the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

commutes. The following full commutative diagram is perhaps a nice illustration of how all three different maps fit together (from left to right: a map of PB-groupoids, a map of fat extensions and a map of VB-groupoids):
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{x_{1}}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{x_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,1})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,1})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,1})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{x_{2}}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{x_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{M,2})_{\mathbf{t}g}\oplus(V_{M,2})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G,2})_{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

The first vertical map (the map of PB-groupoids) takes as input the two horizontal maps (in both rows), the second vertical map (the map of fat extensions) takes as input the right horizontal maps (in both rows), and the last vertical map is simply defined without extra input.

8.
The infinitesimal picture

We now turn to the infinitesimal theory of what we have discussed until now. As already observed in
[
32
]
, there is a fat algebroid
\widehat{V}_{A}
associated to a VB-algebroid
V_{A}
. It carries canonical representations on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
, and it is shown in
[
32
]
how one obtains the (split) ruth associated to
V_{A}
using these representations and a splitting of a canonical exact sequence:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

In this section we elaborate on these remarks, explaining the infinitesimal counterparts of the theory we have seen in the previous sections. We keep the discussion shorter, and a discussion on “PB-algebroids” (a concept which is not defined in the literature yet) is not included. We put an emphasis on describing the correspondences between VB-algebroids,
2
-term ruths, and the newly defined fat extensions. In the section after, we explain the process of differentiation.

8.1.
Notation for VB-algebroids

See, for example,
[
13
,
16
]
for more details on the theory of VB-algebroids.

We denote the structure maps of a Lie algebroid by
([\cdot,\cdot],\rho)
. Writing
\Omega^{\bullet}A=\Gamma\textstyle\bigwedge A^{*}
, we write

\displaystyle d:\Omega^{\bullet}A\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}A

for the algebroid differential (the de Rham differential using
[\cdot,\cdot]
and
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\rho}
). Then
\Omega A
becomes a (commutative) differential graded algebra with
d
and the wedge product.

For
A\Rightarrow M
a Lie algebroid, we denote a VB-algebroid over
A
by
V_{A}\Rightarrow V_{M}
.

Remark 8.1
.

We think of a VB-algebroid as a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

so that we think of the algebroid structures as being “horizontal”, while the other vector bundle structures (on
V_{A}
and
V_{M}
) are “vertical”. In line with this convention, we write
the projection maps
V_{A}\rightarrow V_{M}
and
A\rightarrow M
by
\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}
, and for the projection maps
V_{A}\rightarrow A
and
V_{M}\rightarrow M
we write
\textnormal{pr}_{\downarrow}
. Moreover, we write

\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}=\Gamma(V_{M},V_{A})\textnormal{ and }\Gamma_{\downarrow}V_{A}=\Gamma(A,V_{A})

for the spaces of sections, and similarly
\Omega_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
and
\Omega_{\downarrow}V_{A}
for the respective forms, and we write the scalar multiplications and additions of
V_{A}\rightarrow A
and
V_{M}\rightarrow M
by
h^{\lambda}_{\downarrow}
and
+_{\downarrow}
, respectively, and the scalar multiplications and additions of
V_{A}\rightarrow V_{M}
and
A\rightarrow M
by
h^{\lambda}_{\rightarrow}
and
+_{\rightarrow}
. However, we often simplify to

h^{\lambda}_{\downarrow}v=\lambda_{\downarrow}v\qquad h^{\lambda}_{\rightarrow}v=\lambda_{\rightarrow}v

In particular,
\lambda_{\downarrow}
is infinitesimally multiplicative:

\lambda^{*}_{\downarrow}[v,w]=[\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}v,\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}w]

(with
\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}v=\lambda^{-1}_{\downarrow}\circ v\circ\lambda_{\downarrow}
). That is, the scalar multiplication
\lambda_{\downarrow}
by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is an algebroid automorphism
V_{A}\rightarrow V_{A}

[
13
]
. On the other hand, the scalar multiplication
\lambda_{\rightarrow}
by
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
is a vector bundle isomorphism
V_{A}\rightarrow V_{A}
with respect to the vertical vector bundle structures. Recall that on the core

C_{M}=\ker\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}\cap\ker\textnormal{pr}_{\downarrow}\rightarrow M

of
V_{A}
the two vector bundle structures coincide, i.e. for all
c\in C_{M}
,
\lambda_{\downarrow}c=\lambda_{\rightarrow}c
.

Since
\lambda_{\downarrow}
is an algebroid map, the
1
-homogeneous cochains (with respect to
\lambda_{\downarrow}
)

(8.1)

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}=\{\omega\in\Omega_{\rightarrow}^{\bullet}V_{A}\mid\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}\omega=\lambda\cdot\omega\}\subset\Omega_{\rightarrow}^{\bullet}V_{A}

form a differential graded submodule of
\Omega V_{A}
called the linear (sub)complex. More generally, a form
\omega\in\Omega_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
is called
k
-homogeneous if

\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}\omega=\lambda^{k}\omega

and the
k
-homogeneous forms form a differential graded submodule of
\Omega V_{A}
.

Similarly, given a section
\alpha\in\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
, it is
k
-homogeneous if
66
66

66

Notice that the appearance of
\lambda_{\rightarrow}^{k}
on the right hand side is similar to the appearance of
\lambda^{k}
on the right hand side of the equation of homogeneity of functions on
V_{A}
. More precisely,
V_{A}\times\mathbb{R}\Rightarrow V_{A}
can be seen as a VB-algebroid over
0_{V_{M}}
or
0_{A}
, and
k
-homogeneous sections of these VB-algebroids are exactly the
k+1
-homogeneous functions.

\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}\alpha(=\lambda^{-1}_{\downarrow}\circ\alpha\circ\lambda_{\downarrow})=\lambda^{k}_{\rightarrow}\alpha

and we write
\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
for the linear (i.e.
0
-homogeneous) sections.

Remark 8.2
.

A word on terminology and notation is perhaps on its place. Given a function
f\in C^{\infty}V_{A}
, we have two notions of
f
being
k
-homogeneous, for it can be homogeneous “horizontally” or “vertically”:

\lambda_{\rightarrow}^{*}f=\lambda^{k}f\qquad\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}f=\lambda^{k}f.

We therefore write

C^{\infty}_{\rightarrow\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\qquad C^{\infty}_{\downarrow\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}

for horizontally linear (i.e.
1
-homogeneous) functions and vertically linear functions respectively.

Notice now that, since
67
67

67

The notation
V_{A}^{*_{V_{M}}}
is used to indicate that we dualise
V_{A}
with respect to the vector bundle structure
V_{A}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

\Omega_{\rightarrow}^{1}V_{A}=\Gamma V_{A}^{*_{V_{M}}}=C^{\infty}_{\rightarrow\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}

we can think of elements
\omega
of
\Omega_{\rightarrow}^{1}V_{A}
as linear functions on
V_{A}
with respect to the horizontal vector bundle structure. If
\omega\in\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}^{1}V_{A}
, then the induced function of
V_{A}
is also linear with respect to the vertical vector bundle structure. This means that
\omega
defines an element of
\Omega_{\downarrow}^{1}V_{A}
as well. For general
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
, we have multilinearity “horizontally” and linearity “vertically” in the sense that

\omega(\lambda_{\downarrow}v_{1}+_{\downarrow}\mu_{\downarrow}w_{1},\dots,\lambda_{\downarrow}v_{\bullet}+_{\downarrow}\mu_{\downarrow}w_{\bullet})=\lambda\omega(v_{1},\dots,v_{\bullet})+\mu\omega(w_{1},\dots,w_{\bullet}).

That is, “linearity” refers here to the vertical vector bundle structure, and it would, in line with our convention, be sensible to write
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
as
\Omega_{\rightarrow,\downarrow\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
. We avoid this cumbersome notation as it is clear from the context that, in this case, we are interested in forms on the algebroid
V_{A}\Rightarrow V_{M}
that are linear with respect to the compatible vector bundle structures on
V_{A}\rightarrow A
and
V_{M}\rightarrow M
.

In local coordinates it becomes clear that
\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
is generated, as a
C^{\infty}V_{M}
-module, by the
(-1)
-homogeneous sections, called
core sections
, and the
0
-homogeneous sections, called
linear sections
.

Remark 8.3
.

It is convenient to think about linear (or more generally homogeneous) forms as follows. The space of homogeneous sections in
\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
are naturally graded. We can think of a form
\omega\in\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
therefore as a map

\omega:\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}\otimes_{C^{\infty}V_{M}}\cdots\otimes_{C^{\infty}V_{M}}\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}\rightarrow C^{\infty}V_{M}

that additionally restricts to a map of degree
+1
between homogeneous elements (considering the total degree on the left hand side). Indeed,

\omega(\alpha_{\ell})\circ\lambda_{\downarrow}=(\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}\omega)(\lambda_{\downarrow}^{*}\alpha_{\ell})

so the functions
\omega(\alpha_{\ell})
, for
k_{\ell}
-homogeneous
\alpha_{\ell}\in\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
, are
k+\textstyle\sum_{\ell}k_{\ell}
-homogeneous if and only if
\omega
is
k
-homogeneous.

8.2.
From VB-algebroids to fat extensions

We begin with a continuation of the previous section.

8.2.1.
The appearance of the fat algebroid

From Remark
8.3
, we can conclude that
\omega\in\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
is completely determined by a map of the form

\omega_{0}:\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\times\cdots\times\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{M}.

Now,
\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
is the space of sections of a Lie algebroid
\widehat{V}_{A}
over
M
, called the fat algebroid (see
[
32
]
). This algebroid fits into a short exact sequence (of Lie algebroids)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where the projection
\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow A
is induced by the projection
V_{A}\rightarrow A
(every linear section is projectable to a section of
A
), and the inclusion is given by

\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad h_{x}\mapsto(v\mapsto h_{x}(v)+_{\downarrow}0_{\rightarrow}(v)).

As a vector bundle, the fat algebroid is given by

\widehat{V}_{A}=\{H_{a}:(V_{M})_{\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}a}\rightarrow(V_{A})_{a}\mid\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}H_{a}=1\}.

The anchor map is induced by the projection:

\widehat{\rho}H_{a}=\rho a

and the Lie bracket is induced by the bracket of
V_{A}
(the bracket of linear sections is linear).

Remark 8.4
.

In
[
32
]
the fat algebroid is written as
\hat{A}
. However, we prefer to keep track of the dependence on
V_{A}
.

The Lie bracket of the bundle of Lie algebras
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
is given by

[h,h^{\prime}]\coloneq h^{\prime}\partial h-h\partial h^{\prime}.

As is clear from its definition, it is defined for every
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Definition 8.5
.

Let

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}

be a
2
-term complex. We call the bundle of Lie algebras
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
the
bundle of infinitesimal homotopies
of the
2
-term complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Remark 8.6
.

The bundle of infinitesimal homotopies
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
comes canonically with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
: given
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
and
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
, we set

\nabla_{h}\gamma\coloneq-h\partial\gamma\qquad\nabla_{h}v\coloneq-\partial hv.

We see that, if
\omega\in\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}^{\bullet}V_{A}
, then

\omega_{0}:\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\times\overset{\bullet}{\cdots}\times\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow\Gamma V_{M}^{*}\qquad\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\coloneq\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\omega

and

\omega_{1}:\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\times\overset{\bullet-1}{\cdots}\times\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow\Gamma C_{M}^{*}\qquad\omega_{1}(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{\bullet-1})\gamma\coloneq\iota_{\gamma}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet-1}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\omega

together satisfy the following property: for all
h\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
we have

\iota_{h}\omega_{0}=\omega_{1}h.

Notice that, in the definition
\omega_{1}h
, a sign appears:

(\omega_{1}h)(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet-1}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})hv

We call
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
“invariant” forms, and in the next sections we will explain how such invariant forms constitute a
2
-term ruth
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
that is isomorphic to
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
.

8.2.2.
The fat algebroid and its canonical representations

The fat algebroid
\widehat{V}_{A}
has canonical representations on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}^{*}
68
68

68

We use here that
[\cdot,\cdot]
is a degree
+1
operator on homogeneous sections and, on homogeneous functions,
\rho_{V_{A}}(\alpha)
has degree equal to the degree of
\alpha
.

(8.2)

\nabla_{\alpha}\coloneq[\alpha,\cdot]:\Gamma C_{M}\rightarrow\Gamma C_{M}\qquad\nabla_{\alpha}\coloneq\rho_{V_{A}}\alpha:\Gamma V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow\Gamma V_{M}^{*}

and we can put them together to form a cochain complex representation on

\partial:C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}.

Dually, we also have a cochain complex representation on

V_{M}^{*}\xrightarrow{\partial^{*}}C_{M}^{*}.

We think of
V_{M}^{*}
as sitting in degree
0
and
C_{M}^{*}
as sitting in degree
1
. We denote the resulting differential graded
\Omega A
-module by

\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}=\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})\oplus\Omega^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{A};C_{M}^{*}).

In analogy with Proposition
3.15
, we have:

Proposition 8.7
.

The cochain complex representation of
\widehat{V}_{A}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
. Moreover, the action of
\widehat{V}_{A}
on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
, viewing
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
as an ideal of
\widehat{V}_{A}
, equals the representation of
\widehat{V}_{A}
on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
.

Proof.

We prove the second statement, and we do a direct computation: locally,
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
is generated by elements
f\otimes\gamma
, where
f\in\Gamma V_{M}^{*}
and
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
. Given
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
, we then have

[\alpha,f\otimes\gamma]=f\otimes\nabla_{\alpha}\gamma+\nabla_{\alpha}f\otimes\gamma.

This proves the statement.
∎

Remark 8.8
.

Given a vector bundle
E_{M}
, algebroid representations of
A
on
E_{M}
correspond to algebroid maps

A\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(E_{M}),

where
\mathfrak{gl}(E_{M})
is the algebroid of derivations

\mathfrak{gl}(E_{M})\coloneq\{D=(D,X)\mid D(fs)=fD(s)+X(f)s\}\subset\textnormal{End }E_{M}\oplus TM.

Given two vector bundles
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
, we set

\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})\coloneq\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M})\times_{TM}\mathfrak{gl}(V_{M}).

Using the action algebroid

\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\coloneq\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\rtimes\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})

associated to the representation of
\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})
on
\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})
given by

\nabla_{D}\partial\coloneq D^{V_{M}}\partial-\partial D^{C_{M}},

we see that cochain complex representations of
A
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
correspond to algebroid maps

A\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}

(see also Remark
8.28
).

8.2.3.
Fat extensions

We discussed now the structure behind the fat algebroid of a VB-algebroid that makes it into a
fat extension
. Here is the abstract definition:

Definition 8.9
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid, and consider a
2
-term complex

C_{M}\xrightarrow{\partial}V_{M}.

A fat extension of
A
, with underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, consists of a Lie algebroid

F_{A}\rightrightarrows M,

that comes with a cochain complex representation on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and a short exact sequence of Lie algebroids (over
M
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Moreover, the cochain complex representation of
F_{A}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
restricts to the canonical cochain complex representation of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
, and the two natural actions of
F_{A}
on
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
— viewing
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
as an ideal or as a representation — agree.

We will simply write
F_{A}
for a fat extension, and the underlying algebroid
F_{A}
is called the fat algebroid of the fat extension. From the above discussion, we obtain:

Proposition 8.10
.

Let
V_{A}
be a VB-algebroid over
A
. Then, the fat algebroid
\widehat{V}_{A}
of
V_{A}
, together with its cochain complex representation (
8.2
), is a fat extension of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

Remark 8.11
.

We observe here that, given a VB-algebroid
V_{A}
, the fat extension
\widehat{V}_{A}
with its dual cochain complex representation should be seen as the fat extension of the dual VB-algebroid
V_{A}^{*}\Rightarrow C_{M}^{*}
. First of all, the fat groupoid
\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}
fits into a short exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}

Then,
\widehat{V}_{A}
and
\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}
are isomorphic as Lie algebroids via
69
69

69

Here, say,
\alpha\in\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
is related to
\alpha\in\Gamma A
via the projection,
\xi_{\alpha}\in\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}^{*}
is also related to
\alpha\in\Gamma A
via the projection,
f\in\Gamma C_{M}^{*}=C^{\infty}_{\textnormal{lin}}C_{M}
and
v\in\Gamma_{\downarrow}V_{A}
.

\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}^{*};\quad\alpha\mapsto\xi_{\alpha}(f)v=f(v-_{\downarrow}\alpha\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}v).

Using the isomorphism

\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*});\quad h\mapsto-h^{*},

the short exact sequences of
\widehat{V}_{A}
and
\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}
correspond to each other.

Notice that the structure of
V_{A}^{*}\Rightarrow C_{M}^{*}
is revealed from the fat extension
\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}
as follows: given
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
(and writing
\xi_{\alpha},\xi_{\beta}\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}^{*}
as above),
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
and
f\in\Gamma V_{M}^{*}
,

\rho_{V_{A}^{*}}(\xi_{\alpha})\gamma=[\alpha,\gamma]\qquad\rho_{V_{A}^{*}}(f)\gamma=\partial^{*}(f)\gamma\qquad[\xi_{\alpha},\xi_{\beta}]=\xi_{[\alpha,\beta]}\qquad[\xi_{\alpha},f]=\rho_{V_{A}}(\alpha)f.

Starting with
V_{A}
, these equations define the algebroid structure on
V_{A}^{*}
.

8.2.4.
Extended Cartan calculus

Before we continue, we develop a type of “extended” Cartan calculus on
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
and on
\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
. We find it particularly useful to see how
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
can be described in terms of the fat algebroid
\widehat{V}_{A}
using invariant cochains.

We start with the ordinary Cartan calculus on
\Omega_{\rightarrow}V_{A}
. We have three types of operators: a differential
d
, and then for each section
\alpha\in\Gamma V_{A}
a Lie derivative
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}
and a contraction
\iota_{\alpha}
. The operators are derivations and of degree
+1
,
0
and
-1
, respectively. Together, they satisfy the Cartan calculus identities:

(8.3)

[d,\iota_{\alpha}]=\mathcal{L}_{\alpha},\quad[d,\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}]=0\quad[\iota_{\alpha},\iota_{\beta}]=0\quad[\mathcal{L}_{\alpha},\mathcal{L}_{\beta}]=\mathcal{L}_{[\alpha,\beta]}\quad[\mathcal{L}_{\alpha},\iota_{\beta}]=\iota_{[\alpha,\beta]}.

Similarly, on
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
we have a Cartan calculus if we restrict attention to linear sections (see Remark
8.3
), and see the operators as satisfying the Leibniz rule.

However, from the inclusion
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\subset\Omega V_{A}
, we can also apply contractions and Lie derivatives of (not necessarily linear) sections to linear forms
\omega
. In particular, we can do this for core sections
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
. Instead of thinking of
\iota_{\gamma}\omega
as another form of
V_{A}
, we know from Remark
8.3
that we can see it as a form of
\widehat{V}_{A}
(or even
A
). That is, we can define a contraction of the following form:

Definition 8.12
.

Let
V_{A}
be a VB-algebroid. Given
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
, we set

\iota_{\gamma}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet-1}A\subset\Omega^{\bullet-1}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\iota_{\gamma}\omega(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\coloneq\omega(\gamma,\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet}).

We think of this contraction as having degree
-1
. Notice that it is
\Omega A
-linear. Taking this point of view, we see that there is another type of contraction:

Definition 8.13
.

Let
V_{A}
be a VB-algebroid. Given
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
, we set

\iota_{v}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\iota_{v}\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\coloneq\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v.

We think of this contraction as having degree
0
. Also this map is
\Omega A
-linear. In turn, the contractions define Lie derivatives:

Definition 8.14
.

Let
V_{A}
be a VB-algebroid. Given
\gamma\in C_{M}
and
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
, we set

\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\mathcal{L}_{v}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}\widehat{V}_{A},

(of degree
0
and
+1
, respectively) given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\partial\gamma-\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\omega([\alpha_{k},\gamma],\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}\omega(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=-\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}(\rho_{V_{A}}(\alpha_{k})(\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet}))v

\displaystyle-\rho_{A}(\alpha_{k})(\omega(\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v)).

Notice that both these maps satisfy the Leibniz rule. We “extended” successfully now the Cartan calculus on
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
: the usual Cartan calculus identities (
8.3
) hold, but we should interpret equalities like
[d,\iota_{\gamma}]=\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}
as equality of maps
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega\widehat{V}_{A}
.
70
70

70

Note that
\iota_{v}
is a degree
0
operator, and
\mathcal{L}_{v}
is a degree
+1
operator, so for example
\mathcal{L}_{v}=[d,\iota_{v}]=d\iota_{v}-\iota_{v}d
.

Similarly, on
\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
we have a Cartan calculus, for
\widehat{V}_{A}
is a Lie algebroid, and
V_{M}^{*}
and
C_{M}^{*}
are representations: for
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
we have
\iota_{\alpha}
as usual and
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}=\nabla_{\alpha}
. Notice that again it makes sense to apply contractions and Lie derivatives of sections of
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
in the following sense:

Definition 8.15
.

Let
V_{A}
be a VB-algebroid. Given
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
and
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
, we set

\displaystyle\iota_{\gamma}

\displaystyle:\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet-1}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\iota_{\gamma}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})=\iota_{\gamma}\omega_{1}

\displaystyle\iota_{v}

\displaystyle:\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\iota_{v}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})=\iota_{v}\omega_{0}.

where

\displaystyle\iota_{\gamma}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet-1})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\bullet-1}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet-1})\gamma

\displaystyle\iota_{v}\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v.

Moreover,

\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}:\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}\widehat{V}_{A}\qquad\mathcal{L}_{v}:\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}\widehat{V}_{A},

are given, respectively, by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\gamma}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\partial\gamma+\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k+\bullet}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})[\alpha_{k},\gamma]

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{v}(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=-\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}(\rho_{V_{A}}(\alpha_{k})(\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet}))v

\displaystyle-\rho(\alpha_{k})(\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\widehat{\alpha}_{k},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v)).

8.3.
From fat extensions to
2
-term ruths

We will now show that, as expected,
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
defines a subcomplex of
\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
. We will denote this subcomplex by
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
and call its elements “invariant”. This way, we will introduce the abstract
2
-term ruth
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{A};C_{M})
of an abstract fat extension
F_{A}
. Notice that for
F_{A}=J^{1}A
, this complex appeared in
[
57
]
.

Consider an element
\omega
of
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
. From Remark
8.3
, we know that linear forms are determined by the induced map
71
71

71

We assume for simplicity that
V_{M}
is not the zero bundle, otherwise it is determined by a form on
A
with values in
C_{M}
.

\omega_{0}:\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\times\cdots\times\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow\Gamma V_{M}^{*}\qquad\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\coloneq\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\omega.

This map is
C^{\infty}M
-multilinear and can therefore be thought of as an element of
\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
. In fact, this defines a cochain map

\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})\qquad\omega\mapsto\omega_{0}

using the representation on
V_{M}^{*}
discussed above. Since

\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{2}}\iota_{h}\omega(v)=\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{2}}\iota_{hv}\omega,

the elements
\omega_{0}
have the property that, for all
h\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

\iota_{h}\omega_{0}=\omega_{1}h,

where
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\bullet-1}(A;C_{M}^{*})
is given by
72
72

72

Here,
\gamma\in\Gamma C_{M}
, and we use that
\iota_{\gamma}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet-1}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\omega\in C^{\infty}M
by Remark
8.3
.

\omega_{1}(\alpha_{2},\cdots,\alpha_{\bullet})\gamma\coloneq\iota_{\gamma}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{2}}\omega

and
73
73

73

To explain the sign, observe that we can think of
h^{*}
as a graded endomorphism of degree
-1
of the graded vector bundle
E_{M}=V_{M}^{*}\oplus C_{M}^{*}[-1]
. The expression
\omega h
can then be interpreted as
h^{*}\wedge\omega
, where
\wedge
is induced by the canonical pairing
\textnormal{End }E_{M}\otimes E_{M}\rightarrow E_{M}
(see, for example,
[
4
]
).

(\omega_{1}h)(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})v=(-1)^{\bullet-1}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})h(v).

We can see
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
as an element of the cochain complex representation
\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A},V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})^{\bullet}
. Using the Cartan calculus we developed in the previous section
8.2.4
, a way to write the above condition is now that, for all
h\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
and
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
,

\iota_{v}\iota_{h}=\iota_{hv}.

The map
\omega\mapsto(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
is
\Omega A
-linear, and it intertwines the contraction operators and Lie derivatives. Therefore:

Proposition 8.16
.

The map

\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

is a map differential graded
\Omega A
-modules, and it maps bijectively onto those pairs
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
such that
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{A};C_{M}^{*})
is independent of its entries
74
74

74

By independence we mean that
\omega_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})
only depends on the base sections underlying
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet}
.
and (
8.3
) holds.

Proof.

The proof is a straightforward computation using the Cartan calculus we developed. We have

\displaystyle(d\omega)_{0}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{k}}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{k}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{0}}\omega

\displaystyle+\textstyle\sum_{k<\ell}(-1)^{k+\ell}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{\ell}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{k}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{0}}\iota_{[\alpha_{k},\alpha_{\ell}]}\omega=d\omega_{0}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

and

\displaystyle(d\omega)_{1}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\gamma

\displaystyle=(-1)^{\bullet}\omega_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\partial\gamma

\displaystyle-\textstyle\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}(\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{k}}\iota_{\gamma}-\iota_{[\alpha_{k},\gamma]})\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{k}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\omega

\displaystyle+\textstyle\sum_{k<\ell}(-1)^{k+\ell}\iota_{\gamma}\iota_{\alpha_{\bullet}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{\ell}}\cdots\widehat{\iota}_{\alpha_{k}}\cdots\iota_{\alpha_{1}}\iota_{[\alpha_{k},\alpha_{\ell}]}\omega

\displaystyle=\partial^{*}(\omega_{0})(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\gamma+d\omega_{1}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\gamma.

This proves that the map
\omega\mapsto(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
is a cochain map. That it maps onto those pairs
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
satisfying (
8.3
) is a consequence of Remark
8.3
.
∎

We can immediately conclude the following.

Corollary 8.17
.

The map

\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})

is a map of differential graded
\Omega A
-modules. If
V_{M}\neq 0
, it maps bijectively onto
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
, so those forms
\omega_{0}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A},V_{M}^{*})
for which there exists
\omega_{1}\in\Omega^{\bullet-1}(\widehat{V}_{A},C_{M}^{*})
, independent of its entries, such that (
8.3
) holds.
75
75

75

If
V_{M}=0
, then the map

C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow C^{\bullet-1}(A,C_{M}^{*});\quad f\mapsto f_{1}

is a bijective cochain map.

Remark 8.18
.

Although we will usually write
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
, we do this for notational convenience, so
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
is meant when writing
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
.

To conclude, we formulate the abstract definition of invariant cochains in terms of fat extensions.

Definition 8.19
.

Let
F_{A}
be a fat extension of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The
invariant complex

\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})\subset\Omega(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})

consists of pairs
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\in\Omega(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
such that
\omega_{1}
is independent of its entries, and, for all
h\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
,

\iota_{h}\omega_{0}=h\omega_{1}.

Remark 8.20
.

Notice that the above condition can be written as

\iota_{f}\iota_{h}=\iota_{fh}

for all
f\in\Gamma C_{M}^{*}
and
h\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{C},C_{M})
.

8.4.
Fat extensions and split
2
-term ruths

In
[
32
]
it is shown how a VB-algebroid induces a split
2
-term ruth on
A
.
This goes as follows:

Remark 8.21
.

The choice of a splitting
\sigma:A\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}
defines
A
-connections
\nabla_{\alpha}=\nabla_{\sigma\alpha}
on
V_{M}^{*}
and
C_{M}^{*}
. These connections are usually not flat, but they are up to the homotopy
R_{2}\in\Omega^{2}(A;\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M}^{*},V_{M}^{*})=\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M}))
given by

R_{2}(\alpha,\beta)=\sigma[\alpha,\beta]-[\sigma\alpha,\sigma\beta].

That is,
d=\partial^{*}+d_{\nabla}+R_{2}
76
76

76

Here,
d_{\nabla}
is given by the De Rham type formulas on
\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*})
and on
\Omega(A;C_{M}^{*})
using the
A
-connections.
squares to zero:

[\partial,d_{\nabla}]=0\qquad d_{\nabla}^{2}+[\partial,R_{2}]=0\qquad[d_{\nabla},R_{2}]=0.

Although it is well-known that
\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
is isomorphic (using the splitting
\sigma
) to
\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
, we observe that this is readily verified using the canonical model of invariant cochains from the previous section. The pullback of the splitting defines a map

\sigma^{*}:\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})\rightarrow\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*}).

This is usually not a cochain map, but it restricts to one on
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})
(i.e. those pairs
(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})
for which (
8.3
) holds).

Proposition 8.22
.

Let
\sigma:A\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}
be a splitting and consider the split ruth
\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
. Then the map

\sigma^{*}:\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})\rightarrow\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

is an isomorphism of ruths.

Proof.

Indeed, for all
\omega=(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\in\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})
we have

\displaystyle([d,\sigma^{*}]\omega)_{0}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{k<\ell}(-1)^{k+\ell+\bullet}\omega_{1}(\sigma\alpha_{0},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{k}},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{\ell}},\dots,\sigma\alpha_{\bullet})R_{2}(\alpha_{k},\alpha_{\ell})

\displaystyle+\textstyle\sum_{k<\ell}(-1)^{k+\ell}\omega_{0}(R_{2}(\alpha_{k},\alpha_{\ell}),\sigma\alpha_{0},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{k}},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{\ell}},\dots,\sigma\alpha_{\bullet})

\displaystyle([d,\sigma^{*}]\omega)_{1}(\alpha_{0},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet-1})

\displaystyle=\textstyle\sum_{k<\ell}(-1)^{k+\ell}\omega_{1}(R_{2}(\alpha_{k},\alpha_{\ell}),\sigma\alpha_{0},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{k}},\dots,\widehat{\sigma\alpha_{\ell}},\dots,\sigma\alpha_{\bullet-1})

which are zero on forms satisfying (
8.3
). On the other hand, using the projection
\widehat{V}_{A}\rightarrow A
, its inverse is given by

\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})\rightarrow\Omega(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})\qquad(\omega_{0},\omega_{1})\mapsto(\omega_{0}-h(\omega_{1}),\omega_{1})

where we wrote

h:\Omega^{\bullet-1}(A;C_{M}^{*})\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})\qquad h(\omega_{1})(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})\coloneq(-1)^{\bullet-1}\omega_{1}(\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})(\alpha_{1}-\sigma\alpha_{1}).

It is readily verified that the maps are
\Omega A
-linear, so this proves the statement.
∎

Remark 8.23
.

In each step of passing to a different model

\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}\rightarrow\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})\rightarrow\Omega(A;V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

we are realizing that
\omega\in\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}
is determined by “fewer” sections, at the cost of increasing the combinatorial complexity. In the first step, we can canonically reduce from thinking of forms on
V_{A}
to forms on
\widehat{V}_{A}
. In the second step, we choose a splitting
\sigma:A\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}
, which puts us in the position to think of forms on
A
.

Remark 8.24
.

As was already observed in
[
4
]
, a splitting
\sigma:A\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}
splits the groupoid
\widehat{V}_{A}
as follows. As a vector bundle, it takes the form

\widehat{V}_{A}\cong\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\oplus A,

and its Lie bracket transforms into

[(h,\alpha),(h^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})]\coloneq(R_{2}(\alpha,\alpha^{\prime})+\nabla_{h}\alpha^{\prime}-\nabla_{h^{\prime}}\alpha+[h,h^{\prime}],[\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}]).

This should be seen as the fat extension
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{A}
associated to a split ruth
\mathcal{E}_{A}
. In
[
4
]
, after a sequence of observations about what we now recognise as the fat algebroid of a split ruth (Proposition 3.9 — Proposition 3.12), it is said that “
…there doesn’t seem to be any construction which associates to a Lie algebroid extension of
A
a representation up to homotopy, so that, applying
J^{1}(A)
one recovers the adjoint representation.
”. While that might be true, we now know that the ruth can be recovered from
J^{1}A
and its extension when we think of
J^{1}A
as a fat extension. That is, we can recover the ruth if we additionally keep track of the cochain complex representation
A\rightarrow TM
of
J^{1}A
and its compatibility with the extension.

8.5.
From fat extensions to VB-algebroids

The step we have not discussed yet is how to build the VB-algebroid out of an abstract fat extension. So, let
F_{A}
be an abstract fat extension of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Consider the VB-algebroid

F_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\Rightarrow V_{M}

associated to the cochain complex representation of
F_{A}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. That is, we consider

F_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\coloneq C_{M}\times_{M}F_{A}\times_{M}V_{M}.

as a vector bundle, and we define

[(\gamma,\alpha),(\gamma^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})]\coloneq(\nabla_{\alpha}\gamma^{\prime}-\nabla_{\alpha^{\prime}}\gamma,[\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}])

on sections
(\gamma,\alpha)=(\gamma,\alpha,1):V_{M}\rightarrow F_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
that are constant along the fibers of
V_{M}
.

Remark 8.25
.

When
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{A}
for a VB-algebroid
V_{A}
, then
\widehat{V}_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
projects onto
V_{A}
via

\widehat{V}_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\rightarrow V_{A}\qquad(\gamma,\alpha,v)\mapsto(\gamma+_{\rightarrow}0_{\downarrow}\alpha)+_{\downarrow}\alpha v=(\gamma+_{\downarrow}0_{\rightarrow}v)+_{\rightarrow}\alpha v.

As expected, the kernel can be identified with
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}
, where
V_{M}
is seen as a representation of
\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
trivially.

Following the above remark, we observe next that
F_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
contains the ideal

\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\ltimes V_{M}\hookrightarrow F_{A}\ltimes(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\qquad(h,v)\mapsto(-hv,h,v)

and then the quotient is the required VB-algebroid
V(F_{G})
:

Proposition 8.26
.

The assignment

\{\textnormal{VB-algebroids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad V_{A}\mapsto\widehat{V}_{A}

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-algebroids}\}\qquad F_{A}\mapsto V(F_{A})

is inverse to it (up to canonical isomorphisms).

8.6.
Comments on PB-algebroids and core-transitive double algebroids

The notion of PB-algebroid has not been developed in the literature yet. But the discussion in Section
5
gives a good hint on what the “PB-algebroid” of a fat extension should be. Also for algebroids we can formulate a category of (vertically/horizontally)
core-transitive
double algebroids, and such double algebroids correspond to
core extensions
. This is a slight generalisation of the result in
[
36
]
(similar to the generalisation for groupoids presented in Appendix
A
). The definition is as follows.

Definition 8.27
.

A core extension is a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{h}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

which is a short exact sequence of Lie algebroids, and the action
A_{\downarrow}\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ }\mathfrak{h}
is a
bundle of Lie algebras representation
, i.e. a Lie algebroid action
\nabla
of
A_{\downarrow}
on
\mathfrak{h}
that is a derivation with respect to the bracket of
\mathfrak{h}
:

\nabla_{\alpha}[h_{1},h_{2}]=[\nabla_{\alpha}h_{1},h_{2}]+[h_{1},\nabla_{\alpha}h_{2}].

Moreover, the map
F\rightarrow A_{\downarrow}
restricted to
\mathfrak{h}
intertwines
\nabla_{\alpha}
with
[\alpha,\cdot]
, and the
Peiffer identity
holds: the action of
F
on
\mathfrak{h}
, viewing
\mathfrak{h}\subset F
as an ideal, agrees with the action of
F
on
\mathfrak{h}
induced by
A_{\downarrow}\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ }\mathfrak{h}
and
F\rightarrow A_{\downarrow}
.

Remark 8.28
.

Although we do not discuss it in full detail, we mention here what the main objects are that define the “general linear PB-algebroid” associated to a fat extension of algebroids. Since we do not discuss “
2
-actions” (and in what way they are “principal”), we mainly hint to some analogies with our discussion in Section
5
.

If we are given a fat extension
F_{A}
of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, we can consider

F_{A}\ltimes\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})\Rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})

associated to the representation of
F_{A}
on
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
(see Remark
8.8
). This Lie algebroid carries a “
2
-action” of the strict Lie
2
-algebroid

\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{A}\Rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\Rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M}).

Following the above discussion, this strict Lie
2
-algebroid is actually determined by a
crossed module of Lie algebroids
, so a core extension as above for which
\mathfrak{h}=F
. The bundle of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{h}
in this case is

\mathfrak{pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\coloneq\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})\Rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M},V_{M})

whose bracket is given by (we write
h_{\partial}
for the value of the section
h\in\Gamma\mathfrak{pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
at
\partial
):

[h,h^{\prime}]_{\partial}\coloneq h^{\prime}_{\partial}\partial h_{\partial}-h_{\partial}\partial h^{\prime}_{\partial}.

The Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}
(see Remark
8.8
) acts on
\mathfrak{pert}(C_{M},V_{M})
as a bundle of Lie algebras representation via

(\nabla_{D}h)_{\partial}\coloneq(D^{C_{M}}h-hD^{V_{M}})_{\partial}.

The Lie algebroid map that is part of the core extension is given by

\mathfrak{pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\rightarrow\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}\qquad h\mapsto(D^{C_{M}}_{\partial}\coloneq-h_{\partial}\partial,\textnormal{ }D^{V_{M}}_{\partial}\coloneq-\partial h_{\partial}).

As in Example
5.3
, we can think of the resulting strict Lie
2
-algebroid as being of the form

\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{A}\coloneq\mathfrak{pert}(C_{M},V_{M})\ltimes\mathfrak{gl}(C_{M},V_{M})_{M}.

8.7.
Morphisms of fat extensions

We briefly introduce here the notion of morphisms of fat extensions of Lie algebroids. We do this by introducing it directly as an infinitesimally multiplicative tensor. Given two fat extensions
F_{A,1}
and
F_{A,2}
of
A
, we have a Lie algebroid

F_{A,1}\times_{A}F_{A,2}\Rightarrow M.

This Lie algebroid carries representations that are the tensor product of a representation of
F_{A,1}
with a representation of
F_{A,2}
.

Definition 8.29
.

Consider fat extensions
F_{A,1}
and
F_{A,2}
of
A
, over
C_{M,1}\rightarrow V_{M,1}
and
C_{M,2}\rightarrow V_{M,2}
, respectively. Consider linear maps
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M,2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M,2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{C_{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{V_{M}}}

and a smooth map (that is linear over
M
)

f:F_{A,1}\times_{A}F_{A,2}\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2}).

Then
(f,\Phi)
is a morphism of fat extensions if it is invariant: for all
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
and
(h_{1},h_{2})\in\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,1})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,2},C_{M,2})
,

f(h_{1},h_{2})=h_{2}\Phi^{V_{M}}-\Phi^{C_{M}}h_{1},

and infinitesimally multiplicative:
d(f,\Phi)=0
, where
d
is the differential associated to the
3
-term cochain complex representation

\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},C_{M,2})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},V_{M,2})\rightarrow\textnormal{Hom}(C_{M,1},V_{M,2})

of
F_{A,1}\rightarrow F_{A,2}
.

Remark 8.30
.

The multiplicativity conditions for a morphism
(f,\Phi)
are given more concretely by the following three conditions. The first one is that the map
\Phi
is a cochain map with respect to the differential
\partial
. The second condition realises
f(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
, for all
(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\in F_{A,1}\times_{A}F_{A,2}
, as a homotopy measuring the failure of
\Phi
to intertwine
\nabla_{\alpha_{1}}
with
\nabla_{\alpha_{2}}
:

\displaystyle f(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\partial

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\alpha_{1}}\Phi^{C_{M}}-\Phi^{C_{M}}\nabla_{\alpha_{2}}

\displaystyle\partial f(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\alpha_{1}}\Phi^{V_{M}}-\Phi^{V_{M}}\nabla_{\alpha_{2}}.

The last multiplicativity condition can be written as follows: given sections
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(F_{A,1}\times_{A}F_{A,2})
,

f([\alpha,\beta])v=\nabla_{\alpha_{2}}(f(\beta)v)-f(\beta)(\nabla_{\alpha_{1}}v)+f(\alpha)(\nabla_{\beta_{1}}v)-\nabla_{\beta_{2}}(f(\alpha)v),

where we wrote
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
and
\beta=(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
.

Composition is defined in the same way as in Definition
7.9
.

Definition 8.31
.

Given maps of fat extensions

F_{A,1}\times_{A}F_{A,2}\xrightarrow{f_{21}}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,1},C_{M,2})\qquad F_{A,2}\times_{A}F_{A,3}\xrightarrow{f_{32}}\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M,2},C_{M,3}),

over cochain maps
\Phi_{21}
and
\Phi_{32}
, respectively, the composition
f_{31}=f_{32}\circ f_{21}
over
\Phi_{31}=\Phi_{32}\circ\Phi_{21}
is given by

f_{31}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3})=f_{32}(\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})\Phi_{21}^{V_{M}}+\Phi_{32}^{C_{M}}f_{21}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}),

where
\alpha_{2}\in F_{G,2}
is arbitrary.

We can arrive at the above definition of morphism by starting with a VB-algebroid map

\Phi^{V_{A}}:V_{A,1}\rightarrow V_{A,2}

and defining a comparison map

f_{\Phi}:\widehat{V}_{A,1}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A,2}\qquad f_{\Phi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})v\coloneq(\alpha_{2}(\Phi^{V_{M}}v)-_{\downarrow}\Phi^{V_{A}}(\alpha_{1}v))-_{\rightarrow}0_{\downarrow}\alpha.

The map
\Phi^{V_{A}}
is recovered from
(f_{\Phi},\Phi)
by realising that

\Phi^{V_{A}}((\gamma+_{\rightarrow}0_{\downarrow}\alpha)+_{\downarrow}\alpha_{1}v)=((\Phi^{C_{M}}(\gamma)-f_{\Phi}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})v)+_{\rightarrow}0_{\downarrow}\alpha)+_{\downarrow}\alpha_{2}\Phi^{V_{M}}(v).

We then have:

Theorem 8.32
.

The functor

\{\textnormal{VB-algebroids}\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}\qquad V_{A}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{A}\quad\Phi\mapsto f_{\Phi}

sets an equivalence of categories.

Remark 8.33
.

Similarly, we can establish equivalences of the form

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{VB-algebroids}\}\qquad F_{A}\mapsto V(F_{A})

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Fat extensions}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Abstract $2$-term ruths}\}\qquad F_{A}\mapsto C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{A};C_{M})

\displaystyle\{\textnormal{Split $2$-term ruths}\}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\{\textnormal{Split fat extensions}\}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{A}\mapsto\widehat{\mathcal{E}}_{A}

but we will not go any further into these details here.

In the next section, we will give a detailed description of the differentiation procedure in “Fat Lie theory”. Apart from differentiating fat extensions, we will work out the details of the differentiation of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid to the fat algebroid of a VB-algebroid. The differentiation of the associated abstract
2
-term ruths is, as expected, a type of Van Est map.

9.
Fat Lie theory

In this section we explain the differentiation procedure of fat extensions. As we will see next, the differentiation procedure for fat extensions is relatively straightforward. Afterwards, we go through the details of differentiating the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid to the fat algebroid of its VB-algebroid. To do that, some particular identifications have to be made. This step is also discussed in
[
29
]
(Proposition 2.7), but we elaborate on some aspects. Afterwards, we comment on the Van Est map in this setup.

9.1.
The Fat Lie functor

To obtain the fat extension of a the VB-algebroid of a VB-groupoid, we can simply apply the Lie functor multiple times in several ways:

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. Set

F_{A}\coloneq\textnormal{Lie }F_{G}.

Moreover, consider the cochain complex representation
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
of
A
obtained by differentiation, and the exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}

which is obtained from the fat extension of
G
by differentiation. Then, together with this structure,
F_{A}
is a fat extension of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
.

The assignment is functorial, for morphisms can be differentiated as well. Following Remark
7.8
and Remark
8.30
, this should be seen as an application of a Van Est map. A general form of such Van Est maps will be discussed in
[
50
]
. Here is the special case of interest:

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
f_{G}:F_{G,1}\rightarrow F_{G,2}
be a morphism of fat extensions over
G
. Then

f_{A}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\coloneq\partial_{\epsilon=0}f(\exp(\epsilon\alpha_{1}),\exp(\epsilon\alpha_{2}))

defines a morphism of fat extensions
f_{A}:F_{A,1}\rightarrow F_{A,2}
over
A
. This defines a functor

\textnormal{Lie}:\{\textnormal{fat extensions of }G\}\rightarrow\{\textnormal{fat extensions of }A\}\qquad F_{G}\mapsto F_{A}\quad f_{G}\mapsto f_{A}.

In the next section we show that, given a VB-groupoid
V_{G}
with VB-algebroid
V_{A}
, the Lie algebroid of
\widehat{V}_{G}
can be seen as
\widehat{V}_{A}
.

9.2.
The tangent bundle of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid

We often take the kinematic approach to tangent vectors in this section. Given
M\ni x
, we write tangent vectors as

\partial_{\epsilon=0}x_{\epsilon}=\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\epsilon}|_{\epsilon=0}x_{\epsilon}\in T_{x}M

where
x_{\epsilon}
is a curve in
M
with
x_{0}=x
. We will show a precise relation relating
T\widehat{V_{G}}
and
\widehat{TV_{G}}
, where we view
TV_{G}
as a VB-groupoid over
TG
(a similar statement holds for VB-algebroids). We start with the following observation about vector bundles.

If
E\rightarrow M
and
F\rightarrow M
are vector bundles, then
T\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)
is a vector bundle both over
\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)
and over
TM
.
77
77

77

Given a vector bundle
E\rightarrow M
, we write
T_{e}E
for the fiber over
e\in E
and
(TE)_{v}
for the fiber over
v\in TM
.
We consider the vector bundle structure over
TM
, and we will show that it embeds into
\textnormal{Hom}_{TM}(TE,TF)
.

Let
\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}\in T_{\varphi}\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)
be a tangent vector of
\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)
, say, with

\varphi_{\epsilon}:E_{x_{\epsilon}}\rightarrow F_{x_{\epsilon}},

x=x_{0}
and
\varphi=\varphi_{0}:E_{x}\rightarrow F_{x}
. Write
v=\partial_{\epsilon=0}x_{\epsilon}\in T_{x}M
for the projection. Then, we define

(TE)_{v}\rightarrow(TF)_{v}\qquad\partial_{\epsilon=0}e_{\epsilon}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}e_{\epsilon}.

This map is injective and linear, so we obtained the following:

Proposition 9.3
.

Let
E
and
F
be vector bundles over
M
. Then, the map

T\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)\hookrightarrow\textnormal{Hom}_{TM}(TE,TF)\qquad\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}\mapsto(\partial_{\epsilon=0}e_{\epsilon}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}e_{\epsilon})

is a vector bundle embedding over
TM
.

This is probably easiest to see by writing the map in (standard) local coordinates. The above map over the zero section of
TM\rightarrow M
takes the following form.

Remark 9.4
.

Here, we write the above embedding for
v=0_{x}\in T_{x}M
. We decompose
78
78

78

Here, we wrote
E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}
for the space of vertical vectors, which can be identified with
E_{x}
.

(TE)_{0_{x}}=E_{x}\oplus E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}

canonically. Then,
\textnormal{Hom}((TE)_{0_{x}},(TF)_{0_{x}})
splits canonically as

\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x},F_{x})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}},F_{x})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x},F_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}},F_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}).

The image of the fiber
(T\textnormal{Hom}(E,F))_{0_{x}}
, under the embedding, can be identified with

\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x},F_{x})\oplus\textnormal{Hom}(E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}},F_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}).

Indeed, let
\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}\in T_{\varphi}\textnormal{Hom}(E,F)
such that its projection to
TM
is
0_{x}
. Plugging in

\partial_{\epsilon=0}e_{\epsilon}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}e_{\epsilon}

an element of
E_{x}
gives
\varphi:E_{x}\rightarrow F_{x}
. If we plug in a vertical vector, then, using the notation from above, we can take
x_{\epsilon}=x
, and so
\partial_{\epsilon=0}\varphi_{\epsilon}e_{\epsilon}
is again vertical. The map
(TE)_{0_{x}}\rightarrow(TF)_{0_{x}}
therefore identifies with
\varphi:E_{x}\rightarrow F_{x}
and this map
E_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}\rightarrow F_{x}^{\textnormal{vert}}
.

Now, let
V_{G}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
be a VB-groupoid, and consider the VB-groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
V_{G}\rightrightarrows V_{M}
be a VB-groupoid. Then the map

T\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{TV_{G}}\qquad\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}\mapsto(\partial_{\epsilon=0}v_{\epsilon}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}v_{\epsilon})

is an embedding of Lie groupoids. In fact, using the canonical embedding of Proposition
9.3
, there is a commutative diagram (of short exact sequences over
TM
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(TV_{M},TC_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{TV_{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

Proof.

To see that the above map is a groupoid map, observe that we can write the multiplication of
TG\rightrightarrows TM
as follows (see
[
43
]
). Given
v\in T_{g}G
and
w\in T_{h}G
composable, we can find
g_{\epsilon}
and
h_{\epsilon}
curves in
G
, such that
v=\partial_{\epsilon=0}g_{\epsilon}
and
w=\partial_{\epsilon=0}h_{\epsilon}
, while
\mathbf{s}g_{\epsilon}=\mathbf{t}h_{\epsilon}
for all (small)
\epsilon
. Then

v\cdot w=\partial_{\epsilon=0}(g_{\epsilon}\cdot h_{\epsilon}).

Using this formula, the statement is readily verified.
∎

9.3.
The fat algebroid of the fat groupoid of a VB-groupoid

In this section, we prove that there is a canonical identification

\textnormal{Lie }\widehat{V}_{G}=\widehat{V}_{A}

as vector bundles. Recall that
\widehat{V}_{G}
is an open subset of
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
, and
\widehat{V}_{G}^{\textnormal{cat}}
is the preimage of
1
(the identity section in
\textnormal{End }V_{M}
) under the surjective linear map (over
\mathbf{s}:G\rightarrow M
)

\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},V_{G})\rightarrow\textnormal{End }V_{M}\qquad H_{g}\mapsto\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}.

The tangent space
T_{H_{g}}\widehat{V}_{G}
can therefore be thought of as elements

\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}\in T_{H_{g}}\textnormal{Hom}(\mathbf{s}^{*}V_{M},V_{G})

where
H_{g_{\epsilon}}
are maps
(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{\epsilon}}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g_{\epsilon}}
, such that

\partial_{\epsilon=0}\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g_{\epsilon}}=0.

Using Proposition
9.3
, this equation can be interpreted inside of
\textnormal{End }(TV_{M})_{d\mathbf{s}X}
, where

X=\partial_{\epsilon=0}g_{\epsilon}\in T_{g}G.

Proposition 9.6
.

We have

\textnormal{Lie }\widehat{V}_{G}=\{H_{a}:(V_{M})_{\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}a}\rightarrow(V_{A})_{a}\mid\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}H_{a}=1\}=\widehat{V}_{A}

as vector bundles.

Proof.

Since

(d\widehat{\mathbf{s}})_{H_{g}}:T_{H_{g}}\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow T_{\mathbf{s}g}M\qquad\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}\mathbf{s}g_{\epsilon},

the vector
X=\partial_{\epsilon=0}g_{\epsilon}
is tangent to the source fiber if
\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}\in\ker(d\widehat{\mathbf{s}})_{H_{g}}
. In this situation, we can take
