g_{\epsilon}\in\mathbf{s}^{-1}\mathbf{s}g
. An element

(X,\partial_{\epsilon=0}v_{\epsilon})\in d\mathbf{s}^{*}TV_{M}

(here,
v_{\epsilon}\in(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g_{\epsilon}}=(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
) has then the property that
\partial_{\epsilon=0}v_{\epsilon}
is vertical. This element
\partial_{\epsilon=0}v_{\epsilon}
can now itself be thought of as an element of
(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}
. With this identification, the embedding of Proposition
9.5
produces a linear map

H_{X}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(H_{g}^{*}\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}})_{X}\qquad\partial_{\epsilon=0}v_{\epsilon}\mapsto\partial_{\epsilon=0}H_{g_{\epsilon}}v_{\epsilon}.

We can see these maps as (horizontal) linear sections of the double vector bundle
79
79

79

Notice that this makes sense as
H_{g}
has full rank.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}^{*}\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{g\}}

(the base map of the section
H_{X}
is given by
g\mapsto X\in\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}
). The linear map we constructed

(9.1)

\ker(d\widehat{\mathbf{s}})_{H_{g}}\rightarrow\{H_{X}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(H_{g}^{*}\ker d\mathbf{s}|_{(V_{G})_{g}})_{X}\mid\textnormal{pr}_{\rightarrow}H_{X}=1\}

is injective, so by a dimension count a bijection. The restriction to the units is the desired isomorphism of vector bundles. This proves the statement.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

The double vector bundle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}^{*}\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{g\}}

can be seen as the pullback along
H_{g}:(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{g}
of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{G})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{g\}}

which is a sub double vector bundle of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G.}

That is, we view
\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}}\rightarrow\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}
as a vector bundle over
(V_{G})_{g}\rightarrow\{g\}
, and then we change the base to
(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow\{g\}
using
H_{g}
.

9.4.
Right invariant vector fields of the fat groupoid

Under the correspondence

\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}V_{G}\cong\Gamma_{\rightarrow}V_{A},

\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}
corresponds to
\Gamma_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}=\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
. Linear vector fields on
V_{G}
are (horizontal) linear sections of the double vector bundle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TV_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TG}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}

so
\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
, with the induced bracket from
V_{A}
, is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}
as Lie algebras.

On the other hand, by the discussion from the last section,
\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}\cong\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}\widehat{V}_{G}
. Our goal is now to show that this isomorphism is an isomorphism of Lie algebras, i.e. that the induced bracket on
\textnormal{Lie }\widehat{V}_{G}
is the correct one (the one induced from
V_{A}
). That is, we want to show that

\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}\cong\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}\widehat{V}_{G}

is an isomorphism of Lie algebras. For the following result, see also
[
29
]
.

Proposition 9.8
.

The canonical identification of vector bundles

\textnormal{Lie }\widehat{V}_{G}\cong\widehat{V}_{A}

of Proposition
9.6
is an isomorphism of Lie algebroids.

Proof.

This is straightforward using bisections. Recall that the bisections of
\widehat{V}_{G}
can be seen as linear bisections of
V_{G}
(Remark
3.2
). The exponential map (taking right-invariant flow)
80
80

80

As usual, this should not be interpreted literally due to completeness issues.

\exp:\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}V_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Bis }V_{G}

restricts on linear sections to a map
\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{Bis }\widehat{V}_{G}
, and then the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Bis }\widehat{V}_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}\widehat{V}_{G}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\exp}

commutes. Using the formula for the Lie bracket in terms of the exponential map, we see that the brackets on
\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}
and on
\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}\widehat{V}_{G}
coincide.
∎

Remark 9.9
.

In view of the next section, we add some more details. A section
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
defines right-invariant vector fields

X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv,lin}}V_{G}\qquad\widehat{X}\in\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}\widehat{V}_{G},

both over
X\in\mathfrak{X}_{\textnormal{R-inv}}G
. The right-invariant flow of the linear vector field
X
is determined by a curve of maps

\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon}:V_{M}\rightarrow V_{G}

that are linear over a map
\varphi_{\epsilon}^{X}:M\rightarrow G
. Linearity means that
\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon}
restricts to linear maps
81
81

81

Notice that, given
v\in(V_{M})_{x}
, the curve
(\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon})_{x}v
stays within the source fiber
\mathbf{s}^{-1}\mathbf{s}v
.

(\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon})_{x}:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon}(x)}.

From the previous section, recall (
9.1
), i.e. that
\ker(d\widehat{\mathbf{s}})_{H_{g}}
can be identified with (horizontal) linear sections of the double vector bundle
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{g}^{*}\ker(d\mathbf{s})|_{(V_{G})_{g}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(d\mathbf{s})_{g}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{g\}.}

For this reason, we can see the flow of some section
\widehat{X}
of
\ker d\widehat{\mathbf{s}}
(not necessarily right-invariant) as a curve of linear maps

\varphi^{\widehat{X}}_{\epsilon}:\widehat{V}_{G}\rightarrow\widehat{V}_{G}\qquad\varphi^{\widehat{X}}_{\epsilon}(H_{g}):(V_{M})_{\mathbf{s}g}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon}(g)}

Now, if
\widehat{X}
is right-invariant, i.e. such that

\widehat{X}(H_{g}\cdot H_{h})=(dR_{H_{h}})_{H_{g}}\widehat{X}(H_{g}),

then
\widehat{X}
is determined on the identity elements of
\widehat{V}_{G}
, which defines the section
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
we started with. The flow
\varphi^{\widehat{X}}
is therefore right-invariant and determined by its values on the identity elements; these form a family of linear maps

\varphi^{\widehat{X}}_{\epsilon}:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{\varphi_{\epsilon}^{X}(x)}.

The two maps
\varphi^{\widehat{X}}_{\epsilon}
and
\varphi^{X}_{\epsilon}
coincide.

Next, we explain how the differentiation procedure leads to a Van Est map on invariant cochains.

9.5.
The Van Est map

Before we begin, we briefly mention how the differentiation procedure of abstract ruths looks like.

9.5.1.
Differentiation of abstract ruths

To emphasise, this is only a short discussion. In
[
44
]
, more details will be included.

As is recently shown in
[
15
]
, the differentiation of a Lie groupoid
G
to a Lie algebroid
A
can be thought of algebraically as follows. The differential graded algebra
CG
contains the
differentiating ideal

J_{\nu}=J+\delta J
, where

J=\textstyle\bigoplus_{\bullet\geq 0}J^{\bullet}\qquad J^{\bullet}\coloneq\{f\in C^{\bullet}G\mid f\textnormal{ vanishes to order $\geq 1$ at }\im u_{k}\textnormal{ for some }k\}.

The differential graded algebra
\Omega A
is canonically isomorphic to
(CG)_{\nu}/J_{\nu}
. The quotient map

\textnormal{VE}:(CG)_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega A

is the ordinary
Van Est map
of
[
22
]
(see
[
15
]
for more details).

Now, given a ruth
\mathcal{E}_{G}
of
G
, then
(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}
comes with a filtration
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
generated by
J_{\nu}
. The quotient

\mathcal{E}_{A}\coloneq(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}/\mathcal{F}_{\nu}

is a well-defined ruth of
A
, and accordingly there is a Van Est map

(\mathcal{E}_{G})_{\nu}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}_{A}.

There is a
Van Est theorem
(for split ruths, see
[
6
]
) which relates the cohomology of these ruths with the topology of the source fibers of
G
. This fact and other further details will be discussed in
[
44
]
. We now move to giving an explicit description of the Van Est map/theorem in terms of invariant cochains.

9.5.2.
The Van Est map of invariant cochains

Let
F_{G}
be a fat extension of a Lie groupoid
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
, and consider the associated fat extension
F_{A}
of
A
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
. The Van Est map

\textnormal{VE}:C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})_{\nu}\rightarrow C_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{A};C_{M})

is simply the restriction of the ordinary Van Est map

\textnormal{VE}:C(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})_{\nu}\rightarrow C(F_{A};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})

defined componentwise from the ordinary Van Est maps associated to the representations on
C_{M}
and
V_{M}
. Following
[
6
,
16
]
, there is a Van Est theorem:

Theorem 9.10
.

The Van Est map

\textnormal{VE}:C^{\bullet}(F_{G};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(F_{A};C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})

restricts to a map of differential graded modules

\textnormal{VE}:C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{G};C_{M})_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(F_{A};C_{M}).

Moreover, if
G
has (homologically)
\leq n
-connected fibers, then
VE
induces an isomorphism in cohomology in degrees
\leq n
, and it induces an injective map in cohomology in degree
n+1
.

Instead of proving the statement directly, we explain how the Van Est map looks like in terms of
F_{G}=\widehat{V}_{G}
. Following the discussion of the previous section, we can give a clear description of the formula in this situation.

As explained in the previous section,
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
exponentiates to a curve of linear maps
82
82

82

Here,
\epsilon>0
is small enough so that
\epsilon\alpha
is close to the zero section.

\exp_{x}(\epsilon\alpha)=\exp_{\exp(\epsilon\alpha)x}(\epsilon\alpha):(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{\exp(\epsilon\alpha)x}.

For
\bullet\geq 0
, consider the maps

\partial_{\alpha}:C^{\bullet+1}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})\qquad\partial_{\alpha}:C^{\bullet+1}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}^{*})\rightarrow C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M}^{*})

given both by

(\partial_{\alpha}f)(H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})e=\partial_{\epsilon=0}f(\exp_{1_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}}(\epsilon\alpha),H_{g_{1}},\dots,H_{g_{\bullet}})\exp_{1_{\mathbf{t}g_{1}}}(\epsilon\alpha)\cdot e.

Then the Van Est map

\textnormal{VE}:C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

is given componentwise by
83
83

83

We used
\textnormal{S}_{\bullet}
for the group of permutations on
\bullet
elements.

\textnormal{VE}f(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})=\textstyle\sum_{\sigma\in\textnormal{S}_{\bullet}}(-1)^{\sigma}\partial_{\alpha_{\sigma\bullet}}\cdots\partial_{\alpha_{\sigma 1}}f|_{M}.

Proposition 9.11
.

The Van Est map

\textnormal{VE}:C^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}\rightarrow C_{M}^{*})

restricts to a cochain map

\textnormal{VE}:C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})_{\nu}\rightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*}).

Moreover, if
G
has (homologically)
\leq n
-connected fibers, then
VE
induces an isomorphism in cohomology in degrees
\leq n
, and it induces an injective map in cohomology in degree
n+1
.

Proof.

Given
(f_{0},f_{1})\in C^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})
, we want to show that

(9.2)

\iota_{v}\iota_{h}\textnormal{VE}f_{0}=\iota_{hv}\textnormal{VE}f_{1}

for all
h\in\Gamma\textnormal{Hom}(V_{M},C_{M})
and
v\in\Gamma V_{M}
. First observe that

\exp_{x}(\epsilon h)=1+\epsilon h:(V_{M})_{x}\rightarrow(V_{G})_{1_{x}}.

In particular, as this is a bisection over the unit bisection
1:M\rightarrow G
, we have, for all
\alpha\in\Gamma\widehat{V}_{A}
, that
\partial_{h}\partial_{\alpha}=0
. Therefore, the only surviving terms
\partial_{\beta_{\bullet}}\cdots\partial_{\beta_{1}}f_{0}
in

\textnormal{VE}f_{0}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})=\textstyle\sum_{\sigma\in\textnormal{S}_{\bullet}}(-1)^{\sigma}\partial_{\alpha_{\sigma\bullet}}\cdots\partial_{\alpha_{\sigma 1}}f_{0}|_{M},

say, for
\alpha_{1}=h
, are those for which
\beta_{1}=h
. The result follows using the explicit formula of
[
22
]
:

(\partial_{\beta_{\bullet}}\cdots\partial_{\beta_{1}}f)v=\partial_{\epsilon_{\bullet}=0}\cdots\partial_{\epsilon_{1}=0}f(\exp(\epsilon_{1}\beta_{1}),\dots,\exp(\epsilon_{\bullet}\beta_{\bullet}))\exp(\epsilon_{1}\beta_{1})\cdots\exp(\epsilon_{\bullet}\beta_{\bullet})v.

The last statement is a consequence of, for example, the result of
[
16
]
and our discussion on flows, as it follows from that discussion that we have a commutative diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(C_{\textnormal{VB}}V_{G})_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};V_{M}^{*})_{\nu}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\textnormal{lin}}V_{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{A};V_{M}^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
VE
VE
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

One could also establish such a commutative diagram using instead the result of
[
6
]
.
∎

One can prove the Van Est theorem also directly using similar techniques as in
[
22
]
; this will appear in
[
44
]
.

10.
Deformation theory for Lie groupoids using the jet groupoid

We focus now on the fat extension of the jet groupoid
J^{1}G
. Recall that

\displaystyle J^{1}G

\displaystyle=\{j^{1}\sigma\mid\sigma\in\textnormal{Bis}_{\textnormal{loc}}G\}

\displaystyle=\{H_{g}:T_{\mathbf{s}g}M\rightarrow T_{g}G\mid d\mathbf{s}_{g}H_{g}=1\textnormal{ and }d\mathbf{t}_{g}H_{g}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}

\displaystyle=\{\omega_{g}:T_{g}G\rightarrow A_{\mathbf{s}g}\mid\omega_{g}\ell_{g}=1\textnormal{ and }\omega_{g}r_{g}\textnormal{ is a linear isomorphism}\}.

The deformation complex of
G
is
C_{\textnormal{proj}}TG
, which we now see is isomorphic to
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(J^{1}G;A)
. However, as we will see, it is also natural, perhaps even more natural, to work with the
jet category
whose elements are
1
-jets of sections of the source map.

We already reformulated the vanishing of proper groupoids (see Proposition
6.1
) and the Van Est theorem in terms of
C_{\textnormal{inv}}(J^{1}G;A)
(see Proposition
9.11
).
We will discuss two more reformulations, namely that of describing the deformation class of a deformation of groupoids, and for the deformation cohomology in the regular case. There are many other reformulations possible of other parts of
[
19
]
, but we won’t discuss further applications here. We think the small discussion presented here gives a good starting point for the interested reader to understand deformation theory of Lie groupoids in this setup.

Remark 10.1
.

Before we continue, notice that in
[
57
]
the deformation complex of Lie algebroids is discussed in terms of the jet algebroid
J^{1}A
. That is,
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(J^{1}A;A)
is the deformation complex of
A
(see also
[
20
]
). The dgLa structure is given as follows: given
\omega_{0}\in\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet+1}(J^{1}A;A)
and
\omega_{0}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}^{\bullet^{\prime}+1}(J^{1}A;A)
, we first define

\displaystyle(\omega_{0}\circ\omega_{0}^{\prime})(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet+\bullet^{\prime}+1})=\textstyle\sum_{\sigma}(-1)^{\sigma}\omega_{0}(\omega_{0}^{\prime}(\alpha_{\sigma 1},\dots,\alpha_{\sigma(\bullet^{\prime}+1)}),\alpha_{\sigma(1+\bullet^{\prime}+1)},\dots,\alpha_{\sigma(\bullet+\bullet^{\prime}+1)})

and then put

[\omega,\omega^{\prime}]=(-1)^{\bullet\bullet^{\prime}}\omega\circ\omega^{\prime}-\omega^{\prime}\circ\omega.

Notice that we can see elements
\omega_{0}
in degree
2
satisfying the Maurer Cartan equation
[\omega_{0},\omega_{0}]=0
as Lie brackets by setting

[\alpha,\beta]_{\omega}\coloneq\omega_{0}(d\alpha,d\beta).

The Leibniz rule follows by the existence of the form
\omega_{1}
, which can be seen as the anchor map. In fact, for any form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(J^{1}A;A)
we can define a multiderivation by

D(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{\bullet})=\omega_{0}(d\alpha_{1},\dots,d\alpha_{\bullet})

whose symbol is given by
\omega_{1}
. This way, we obtain an isomorphism of differential graded Lie algebras between
\Omega_{\textnormal{def}}A
of
[
20
]
and
\Omega_{\textnormal{inv}}(J^{1}A;A)
of
[
57
]
.

10.1.
Deformations of Lie groupoids

We define a deformation of Lie groupoids as in
[
19
]
.

Definition 10.2
.

A deformation of a Lie groupoid
G
is a Lie groupoid
G_{\mathbb{R}}\rightrightarrows M_{\mathbb{R}}
together with a Lie groupoid submersion

G_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow 1_{\mathbb{R}}

such that
G_{0}=G
(the preimage of zero).

Example 10.3
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and let
H\rightrightarrows N
be a Lie subgroupoid of
G
. Consider the deformation to the normal cone (see for instance
[
17
,
49
,
24
,
51
]
)
84
84

84

As sets,
\mathcal{D}(M,N)=M\times(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})\sqcup\mathcal{N}(M,N)
. The smooth structure is canonically defined, and it is unique with the property that
M\times(\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\})
is an embedded submanifold.

\mathcal{D}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{D}(M,N).

This is a deformation, in the above sense, of
\mathcal{N}(G,H)\rightrightarrows\mathcal{N}(M,N)
, and it is central to the linearisation problem for Lie groupoids.

Deformations can be seen as families of Lie groupoids
G_{\epsilon}\rightrightarrows M_{\epsilon}
. For the deformation theory of
G
, both
C_{\textnormal{def}}G
and
C_{\textnormal{def}}G_{\mathbb{R}}
are relevant. We can see
TG_{\mathbb{R}}
as a deformation of
TG
, and elements of
J^{1}G_{\mathbb{R}}
take the form

H_{g}:T_{\mathbf{s}g}M_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow T_{g}G_{\mathbb{R}}

that we should see as a family of maps
T_{\mathbf{s}g_{\epsilon}}M_{\epsilon}\rightarrow T_{g_{\epsilon}}G_{\epsilon}
.
85
85

85

Note that if
X\in T_{\mathbf{s}g}M
projects to
\lambda\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}
, then
H_{g}X
also projects to
\lambda\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}
.

6

10.2.
The deformation class of a deformation

Given a deformation
G_{\mathbb{R}}
of
G
, we can lift the vector field
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\in\mathfrak{X}\mathbb{R}
to a vector field
X_{\mathbb{R}}
of
M_{\mathbb{R}}
. Then, take a fat splitting
h
(see Section
6.2
) and define the map

f_{0}:J^{1}G_{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow\mathbf{t}^{*}A_{\mathbb{R}}\qquad f_{0}(H_{g})=-h(H_{g})X_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{s}g).

This defines an element of
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{1}(J^{1}G_{\mathbb{R}};A_{\mathbb{R}})
with
f_{1}=X_{\mathbb{R}}\in\mathfrak{X}M_{\mathbb{R}}
:

\displaystyle f_{0}(H_{g})-f_{0}(H_{g}^{\prime})=h(H_{g},H_{g}^{\prime})f_{1}(\mathbf{s}g).

Remark 10.4
.

It is good to realise that, using the model
C_{\textnormal{proj}}TG_{\mathbb{R}}
, and using a cleavage
\Sigma
of
TG_{\mathbb{R}}
, we can lift
X_{\mathbb{R}}
to an
\mathbf{s}
-projectable vector field
\Sigma X_{\mathbb{R}}
of
G_{\mathbb{R}}
(i.e.
g\mapsto\Sigma_{g}X_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{s}g)
) and it can directly be seen as a
1
-cochain, for it is
\mathbf{s}
-projectable. On the one hand, the
1
-cochain we write here makes it clear that, when we pass to the adjoint (split) ruth using
\Sigma
, the
1
-cochain is nothing but
X_{\mathbb{R}}\in C^{0}(G_{\mathbb{R}};TM_{\mathbb{R}})
. On the other hand, the usage of the cleavage shows that the map
f
above is more naturally defined on the fat category.

Taking
\delta f_{0}
defines a cocycle that we can restrict to a cocycle in
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{2}(J^{1}G;A)
. Indeed, since

\delta f_{0}(H_{g_{1}},g_{2})=h(H_{g_{1}})X_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{s}g_{1})-(h(H_{g_{1}}\cdot H_{g_{2}})-H_{g_{1}}\cdot h(H_{g_{2}}))X_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathbf{s}g_{2}),

where
H_{g_{2}}
is arbitrary, and
X_{\mathbb{R}}
projects to
\tfrac{\partial}{\partial t}
, the resulting element in
(A_{\mathbb{R}})_{\mathbf{t}g}
is vertical with respect to the projection to
\mathbb{R}
. Therefore, the same equation defines a cocycle in
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{2}(J^{1}G;A)
.

The resulting cohomology class is independent of the choice of vector field
X_{\mathbb{R}}
or the fat splitting
h
. The reason is that
f_{0}-f_{0}^{\prime}
(where
f_{0}^{\prime}
is defined using a different choice
X_{\mathbb{R}}^{\prime}
and
h^{\prime}
) restricts to
C_{\textnormal{inv}}^{1}(J^{1}G;A)
(the same reasoning as before applies). Since

\delta f_{0}=\delta f_{0}^{\prime}+\delta(f_{0}-f_{0}^{\prime}),

the cohomology classes defined by
f_{0}
and by
f_{0}^{\prime}
are the same.

Remark 10.5
.

As can be inferred from
[
7
,
19
]
, we can linearise a proper Lie groupoid
G
along an invariant submanifold
N\subset M
using
\mathcal{D}(G,G_{N})
and the homotopy operator
\eta
from Proposition
6.1
. The projectable complex from
[
19
]
is natural for such an application, for it is easy to see that its
1
-cocycles comprise the multiplicative vector fields. The above model, especially when put in terms of the jet category, seems to also give a natural point of view.

10.3.
Deformation cohomology in the regular case

Given a regular Lie groupoid
G
,
TG
is also regular. A VB-groupoid
V_{G}
over
G
whose underlying complex
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
is regular, comes with a short exact sequence of complexes
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker\partial}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces V_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{coker }\partial}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\partial}

all three of which we can see as cochain complex representations of
\widehat{V}_{G}
. Notice that the invariant complex of the outer two representations are canonically isomorphic to the corresponding representations of
G
. We therefore obtain a long exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\bullet}(G;\ker\partial)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{inv}}(\widehat{V}_{G};C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\bullet-1}(G;\textnormal{coker }\partial)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}

If
G=G(P)
is the Gauge groupoid of a principal bundle
P
, we recover the result that

H^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{def}}G(P)\cong H^{\bullet}(G(P);P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}),

where
P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}
is the adjoint bundle. If
G=G(\mathcal{F})
is a foliation groupoid of a foliation
\mathcal{F}
, we recover the result that

H^{\bullet}_{\textnormal{def}}G(\mathcal{F})\cong H^{\bullet-1}(G(\mathcal{F});\mathcal{N}),

where
\mathcal{N}
is the normal bundle of the foliation. Similar arguments work to prove analogous results for VB-algebroids.

Appendix A
Double groupoids and core extensions

Recall the following notation we use for double groupoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

We denote the structure maps of
G_{\rightarrow}
and
G_{\downarrow}
using the same arrow-index. We denote the groupoid structure maps of
G\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}
with indices
\rightarrow
and
G\rightrightarrows G_{\rightarrow}
with indices
\downarrow
.

The core groupoid is given by

F\coloneq\ker\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}\cap\ker\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}\rightrightarrows M,

whose structure maps are given by
\mathbf{s}(g)=\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}g=\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}g
,
\mathbf{t}(g)=\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}g=\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g
, and
86
86

86

That is, we right-translate
g
to be composable with
h
.

\displaystyle g\cdot h

\displaystyle=(g\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h))\cdot_{\rightarrow}h=(g\cdot_{\rightarrow}1_{\downarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}h))\cdot_{\downarrow}h

\displaystyle g^{-1}

\displaystyle=(g)_{\rightarrow}^{-1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1}=(g)_{\downarrow}^{-1}\cdot_{\rightarrow}1_{\downarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}g)^{-1}.

It comes with two groupoid maps

G_{\downarrow}\xleftarrow{\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}}F\xrightarrow{\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}}G_{\rightarrow}.

Then, (vertically) core-transitive double groupoids are those for which the map

\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}:F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}

is a surjective submersion.

Following
[
12
]
(or rather generalising their result) we will show that this type of double groupoid can be described in a much more efficient way using, what we call here, a
core extension
.

A.1.
From core-transitive double groupoids to core extensions

Let
G
be a vertically core-transitive double groupoid. Then
G
comes with a bundle of Lie groups suggestively denoted by
H
, which is the kernel of the map
F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}
. Notice that there is a natural conjugation action of
G_{\downarrow}
on
H
. Namely, for
g\in G_{\downarrow}
and
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}
, such that
\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}g=x
, we can set

g\cdot h_{x}\coloneq 1_{\rightarrow}(g)\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g^{-1}).

Notice that this action defines a “bundle of Lie groups representation” on the bundle of Lie groups
H
in the following sense.

Definition A.1
.

Let
G\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and consider a bundle of Lie groups
\textnormal{H}\rightrightarrows M
. An action of
G
on
H
is called a
bundle of Lie groups representation
if the action is by Lie group automorphisms. That is, given
g\in G
and
h_{x},h_{x}^{\prime}\in\textnormal{H}
with
\mathbf{s}g=x
,

g\cdot(h_{x}\cdot h_{x}^{\prime})=(g\cdot h_{x})\cdot(g\cdot h_{x}^{\prime}).

We actually defined now three structures from a vertically core-transitive double groupoid
G
— a short exact sequence of Lie groupoids
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

a Lie groupoid map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
, and a bundle of Lie groups representation
G_{\downarrow}\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ H}
. We summarise this data into a diagram as follows
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

There are two compatibility assumptions between the three structures described above that are reminiscent of the axioms of a so-called
crossed module
of Lie groupoids. Firstly, the map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
restricted to
H
is
equivariant
. By this we mean that the map turns the action of
G_{\downarrow}
on
H
into the usual conjugation of
G_{\downarrow}
:
87
87

87

Notice that
H
maps into the isotropy of
G_{\downarrow}
.

\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}(g\cdot h_{x})=g\cdot(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{x})\cdot g^{-1}.

Secondly, the map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
defines an action of
F
on
H
. The following lemma shows that this action equals the conjugation action of
F
on
H
(viewing
\textnormal{H}\subset F
as a normal subgroupoid).

Lemma A.2
.

Given a vertically core-transitive double groupoid
G
, we have for all
g\in F
and
h_{x}\in\textnormal{H}
with
\mathbf{s}g=x
,
88
88

88

On the right hand side the product of
F
is used.

1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1}=g\cdot h\cdot g^{-1}.

We call this the Peiffer identity.

Proof.

This follows by the interchange law. We do a direct computation:

\displaystyle g\cdot h_{x}\cdot g^{-1}

\displaystyle=(g\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}((g)_{\rightarrow}^{-1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1})

\displaystyle=(g\cdot_{\rightarrow}(g)_{\rightarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1}=1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1}.

This proves the statement.
∎

The terminology is chosen to reflect that the identity closely resembles the Peiffer identity for crossed modules of Lie groupoids. That is, the structure we arrived at is both a generalisation of that of core diagrams in
[
12
]
and that of crossed modules (see, for example,
[
42
,
1
,
39
]
). We call it a
core extension
in analogy with our definition of fat extension.

Definition A.3
.

A core extension is a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

such that the restriction of the map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
to
H
is equivariant (with respect to conjugation in
G_{\downarrow}
) and the Peiffer identity holds: the conjugation action of
F
on
H
, viewing
H
as a normal subgroupoid of
F
, equals the action by
F
on
H
induced by the bundle of Lie groups representation
G_{\downarrow}\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ H}
and
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
.

We usually simply write
F
for a core extension. Notice that, in a core extension
F
, the orbits of
F
are the same as the orbits of
G_{\rightarrow}
.

Since a double groupoid might be both horizontally and vertically core-transitive, we sometimes call the associated core extension of a vertically core-transitive double groupoid the
vertical core extension
.

Remark A.4
.

It is worthwhile to observe that a strict Lie
2
-groupoid
H_{G}\rightrightarrows H_{M}\rightrightarrows N
is always vertically core-transitive. Writing
F_{H}
for its core, the above exact sequence for
H_{G}
is given by
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

So, the vertical core extension of a strict Lie
2
-groupoid is completely determined by a bundle of Lie groups
F_{H}\rightrightarrows N
and a Lie groupoid
H_{M}\rightrightarrows N
, together with a Lie groupoid map
F_{H}\rightarrow H_{M}
, and a bundle of Lie groups representation
H_{M}\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}F_{H}
. This recovers the concept of crossed module of Lie groupoids. This definition is used in, for example,
[
42
,
1
,
39
]
. As we will see in the next section, the one-to-one correspondence we set between vertically core-transitive double groupoids and core extensions reduces to a one-to-one correspondence between strict Lie
2
-groupoids and crossed modules of Lie groupoids
(F_{H},H_{M})
(where
F_{H}
is a bundle of Lie groups). See also Remark
A.10
.

A.2.
From core extensions to core-transitive double groupoids

In this section we show that we can recover a vertically core-transitive double groupoid from its vertical core extension. As we already mentioned before, this sets a one-to-one correspondence. For a large and essential part, this was proved already in
[
12
]
(see especially the last comments in that work). There, the focus lies on transitive double groupoids.
89
89

89

In
[
12
]
, transitive groupoids are called locally trivial groupoids.
These are double groupoids such that all four groupoids defining the double groupoid are assumed to be transitive. Additionally, they are assumed to be horizontally core-transitive and vertically core-transitive. However, it is observed at the end of the work
[
12
]
that generalisations should be possible for vertically core-transitive double groupoids that satisfy another assumption. Writing
G
for the double groupoid as before, the assumption is written in
[
12
]
as follows: all elements of
G
can be written as

g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})^{-1}_{\downarrow}

where
g_{1},g_{3}\in F
and
g_{2}\in G_{\downarrow}
. However, essentially, this assumption is equivalent to the vertically core-transitive assumption:
90
90

90

A similar remark was already made in
[
36
]
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
G
be a double groupoid. Then
G
is vertically core-transitive if and only if

F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\rightarrow G\qquad(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\mapsto g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})^{-1}_{\downarrow}

is a surjective submersion.

Proof.

A local section
\sigma
of
\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}:F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}
defines a local section of the above map by setting

g\mapsto(g\cdot_{\downarrow}\sigma(\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}g)\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}g)^{-1},\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}g,\sigma(\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}g)).

Given
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\in F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F
, a local section of
F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}
through
g_{3}
defines (around the element
g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1}\in G
) a local section through
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
. A local section
\sigma
of the above map defines a local section of
F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}
by setting

g\mapsto\textnormal{pr}_{1}\sigma 1_{\downarrow}(g)=\textnormal{pr}_{3}\sigma 1_{\downarrow}(g).

This proves the statement.
∎

That is, in Remark 4.3 of
[
12
]
, assumptions (4.3.1) and (4.3.2) are equivalent. The relevance of the above lemma becomes clear after the following observation. The pullback groupoid of
G_{\downarrow}
along the source map of
F
defines a double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

The vertical groupoid structure takes the form
\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=g_{1}
,
\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=g_{3}
, and

(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3},g_{4},g_{5})=(g_{1},g_{2}g_{4},g_{5}).

The horizontal groupoid structure
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}
is given by
\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g_{1})g_{2}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}g_{3})^{-1}
,
\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow}(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})=g_{2}
, and

(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})=(g_{1}h_{1},(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})^{-1}g_{2}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{3})=h_{2},g_{3}h_{3}).

A direct computation shows:

Lemma A.6
.

Let
G
be a vertically core-transitive double groupoid. Then the map

F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\rightarrow G\qquad(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})\mapsto g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})^{-1}_{\downarrow}

is a map of double groupoids that is a surjective submersion.

Proof.

That the map intertwines the vertical products is readily verified. We do a direct computation to verify that the map intertwines the horizontal products. Suppose
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
and
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})
in
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F
are horizontally composable. Then, first of all,

\displaystyle(g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})

\displaystyle\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})

\displaystyle=((g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1}))\cdot_{\rightarrow}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}((1_{\rightarrow}((\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})^{-1}g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1}))

\displaystyle=(g_{1}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}((1_{\rightarrow}((\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})^{-1}g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})),

where in the first equality we replaced
g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})
with

g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}((\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})^{-1}g_{2})

and used the interchange law. We now use that
(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{1})^{-1}g_{2}=h_{2}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{3})^{-1}
and conclude:

\displaystyle(g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})

\displaystyle\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(g_{1}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}((1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{3})^{-1}\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})\cdot_{\rightarrow}(1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1}))

\displaystyle=(g_{1}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}((g_{3}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(\mathbf{t}_{\rightarrow}h_{3}))^{-1}_{\downarrow}\cdot_{\rightarrow}(h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(g_{1}h_{1})\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(h_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3}h_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1}.

That the map is a surjective submersion was already proved in Lemma
A.5
.
∎

Now, we can realise
G
as a quotient of
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F
by the horizontal kernel of the above map.

Remark A.7
.

Given a map of double groupoids
\Phi:G\rightarrow G^{\prime}
that is a surjective submersion, notice that its horizontal kernel
K\coloneq\ker_{\rightarrow}\Phi
can be seen as a double subgroupoid of
G
. Here,

\ker_{\rightarrow}\Phi\coloneq\{g\in G\mid\Phi(g)=\im 1_{\rightarrow}\}.

This is a consequence of the interchange law. We can then see the resulting exact sequence
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}

as a short “horizontally” exact sequence of double groupoids. More precisely, all maps in the exact sequence are maps of double groupoids, but the sequence is only exact with respect to the horizontal products.

The map
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\rightarrow G
from Lemma
A.5
, seen as a groupoid map of the horizontal products, has kernel isomorphic to the action groupoid

G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{H},

where the action is given by

g\cdot h_{x}=1_{\rightarrow}(g)\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g)^{-1}.

Its structure of double groupoid is
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

where
G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}\textnormal{H}\rightrightarrows G_{\downarrow}
is seen as a bundle of Lie groups.

Proposition A.8
.

Consider the map
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\rightarrow G
from Lemma
A.6
. This map, together with the map

G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightarrow F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F\qquad(g,h_{x})\mapsto(1_{\rightarrow}(g)\cdot_{\downarrow}h_{x}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g)^{-1},g,h_{x})

fit into a short horizontally exact sequence of double Lie groupoids (see Remark
A.7
)
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

Proof.

The horizontal kernel consists of elements
(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3})
satisfying

g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1}=1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2}),

so
g_{1},g_{3}\in H
, and

g_{1}=1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}g_{3}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})^{-1}.

This proves that the horizontal kernel can indeed be identified with
G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}
as above. The map of Lemma
A.6
induces an isomorphism of double groupoids

G(F)\rightarrow G\qquad[g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}]\mapsto g_{1}\cdot_{\downarrow}1_{\rightarrow}(g_{2})\cdot_{\downarrow}(g_{3})_{\downarrow}^{-1},

where
G(F)
is the double groupoid

G(F)\coloneq(F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F)/(G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H})\rightrightarrows F/\textnormal{H}=G_{\rightarrow}

over
G_{\downarrow}\rightrightarrows M
. This proves the statement.
∎

Now, the structure being used to define

G(F)=(F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F)/(G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H})

uses precisely the data underlying the vertical core extension of
G
. To ensure
G(F)
is defined for any core extension
F
, the compatibility assumptions are used in the following way. Firstly, the action groupoid
G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightrightarrows\textnormal{H}
is a double groupoid over
G_{\downarrow}
precisely because the action defines a bundle of Lie groups representation (see Definition
A.1
). The equivariance condition makes sure that the embedding

G_{\downarrow}\ltimes\textnormal{H}\rightarrow F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F

is a map of double groupoids. The Peiffer identity ensures this groupoid is actually normal inside of
F\times_{M}G_{\downarrow}\times_{M}F
. We now conclude:

Theorem A.9
.

The association

\{\textnormal{Vertically core-transitive double Lie groupoids}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\textnormal{Vertical core extensions}\}\qquad G\mapsto F

sets a one-to-one correspondence, and the assignment

\{\textnormal{Vertical core extensions}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\textnormal{Vertically core-transitive double Lie groupoids}\}\qquad F\mapsto G(F)

is inverse to it (up to canonical isomorphisms).

Proof.

We already showed that, given
G
a vertically core-transitive double groupoid,
G
and
G(F)
are isomorphic, where
F
is the vertical core extension of
G
. So, it remains to observe that, given a core extension
F
, the core groupoid of
G(F)
can be identified with
F\ni g
as the classes of elements represented by
[g,1_{\mathbf{s}g},1_{\mathbf{s}g}]
. That we recover the core extension is now readily verified.
∎

Remark A.10
.

Restricting attention to strict Lie
2
-groupoids yields an interesting consequence: they correspond to crossed modules of Lie groupoids
(F_{H},H_{M})
for which
F_{H}
is a bundle of Lie groups (see Remark
A.4
).

The whole discussion also applies to horizontally core-transitive double Lie groupoids (by interchanging horizontal and vertical structures everywhere). From our discussion it follows that
double core-transitive groupoids
(so double groupoids that are both horizontally and vertically core-transitive) are isomorphic to either of the two double groupoids that its core extensions define. However, starting with two core extensions, it is not immediate that the two resulting double groupoids one constructs are isomorphic. Therefore, an extra compatibility assumption between the two core extensions appears. This leads to the following definition:

Definition A.11
.

A
double core extension
, or core diagram, is a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\downarrow}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

of exact sequences such that
\textnormal{H}_{\downarrow}
and
\textnormal{H}_{\rightarrow}
commute elementwise in
F
: for all
h_{x,\downarrow}\in\textnormal{H}_{\downarrow}
and
h_{x,\rightarrow}\in\textnormal{H}_{\rightarrow}
we have that

h_{x,\downarrow}h_{x,\rightarrow}=h_{x,\rightarrow}h_{x,\downarrow}

as elements of
F
.

Remark A.12
.

The above compatibility condition (of “commuting elementwise”) is written in
[
12
]
. We did not specify the actions in the above definition, for specifying the actions in a double core extension is unnecessary (this should be compared to Lemma 2.6 of
[
12
]
). Indeed, given two core extensions
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\downarrow}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

\circlearrowright
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\circlearrowleft

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

satisfying that elements of
\textnormal{H}_{\downarrow}
and
\textnormal{H}_{\rightarrow}
commute elementwise in
F
, then, for example, the action of
G_{\rightarrow}
on
H_{\rightarrow}
is given by

g\cdot h_{x}=\widetilde{g}\cdot h_{x}\cdot\widetilde{g}^{-1}

where
\widetilde{g}\in F
is arbitrary with the property that
\widetilde{g}
maps to
g
under the map
F\rightarrow G_{\rightarrow}
. That this action is well-defined uses the compatibility between the two core extensions. The newly defined actions in terms of lifts therefore determine the actions that are part of the data of the given core extensions. As equivariance and the Peiffer identity are immediate from this new description of the action, a double core extension can be defined as in Definition
A.11
.

From our discussion we can conclude (this result was already proved in
[
36
]
):

Corollary A.13
.

There is a one-to-one correspondence

\{\textnormal{Double core-transitive groupoids}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{\textnormal{Double core extensions}\}\qquad G\mapsto F.

Remark A.14
.

In
[
12
]
, the focus lies on “double transitive groupoids”, so horizontally and vertically transitive (and core-transitive) double groupoids. To recover the main result of
[
12
]
on double transitive groupoids, recall that, in a core extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

the orbits of
F
are equal to the orbits of
G_{\rightarrow}
. Therefore,
F
is transitive if and only if
G_{\rightarrow}
is, and we call core extensions for which this holds transitive. The resulting double groupoid is vertically transitive, and this sets an equivalence between transitive vertical core extensions and vertically transitive (that are also vertically core-transitive) double groupoids. Using Corollary
A.13
, this reproduces the result of
[
12
]
on double transitive groupoids (namely that they correspond to double transitive core extensions).

Remark A.15
.

A class of examples of double groupoids which are usually not (core-)transitive are given by commuting groupoid actions
G

\circlearrowright

P

\circlearrowright

H
. Given such commuting actions, we have a double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\ltimes P\rtimes H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P\rtimes H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\ltimes P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

The core is the unit groupoid
1_{P}
, and the core data consists further of the unit embeddings

G\ltimes P\hookleftarrow P\hookrightarrow P\rtimes H.

These maps carry no information about the actions. In this case, rather than considering the core, we can view
G\ltimes P\rtimes H
as a groupoid over
P
, and there are two Lie groupoid maps

G\leftarrow G\ltimes P\rtimes H\rightarrow H.

It is well-known that there is a natural way to set an equivalence between left/right principal bibundles (commutating actions, one of which is principal) and such diagrams
G\leftarrow K\rightarrow H
where one is a Morita map (see
[
28
]
).

We now go back to PB-groupoids, and show that PB-groupoids (that come with a gauge double groupoid) can be efficiently described using core extensions.

A.3.
PB-groupoids and core extensions

Principal groupoid bundles do not always come with a smooth gauge groupoid. A typical assumption that guarantees the smoothness of the gauge groupoid is that the moment map is a surjective submersion (see e.g.
[
28
]
). General linear PB-groupoids come with a gauge double groupoid if the differential has constant rank. We focus here on the following class of PB-groupoids:

Definition A.16
.

let
P_{G}
be a PB-groupoid. If the moment map(s) of
P_{G}
are surjective submersions onto a smooth embedded submanifold, then we call
P_{G}
a
PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid
.

To be clear, PB-groupoids with gauge double groupoid really come with a gauge double groupoid:

Proposition A.17
.

Let
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
be a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid. Then

P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M}

is a Lie groupoid with
\mathbf{s}[p_{g},p_{h}]\coloneq[\mathbf{s}p_{g},\mathbf{s}p_{h}]
,
\mathbf{t}[p_{g},p_{h}]=[\mathbf{t}p_{g},\mathbf{t}p_{h}]
, and

[p_{g},p_{h}]\cdot[p_{g^{\prime}},p_{h^{\prime}}]\coloneq[p_{g}\cdot p_{g^{\prime}},p_{h}\cdot p_{h^{\prime}}]\qquad[p_{g},p_{h}]^{-1}=[p_{g}^{-1},p_{h}^{-1}].

(here,
\mathbf{s}p_{g}=\mathbf{t}p_{g^{\prime}}
and
\mathbf{s}p_{h}=\mathbf{t}p_{h^{\prime}}
). Moreover,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

is a full double groupoid, called the gauge double groupoid of
P_{G}
.

Proof.

We prove here that the above double groupoid is full. That is, the double source map

(\mathbf{s}_{\rightarrow},\mathbf{s}_{\downarrow}):P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}\rightarrow(P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M})\times_{M}G

is a surjective submersion. Indeed, to construct a section through
[p_{g},p_{h}]\in P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}
, first pick a local section
\sigma
of
P_{M}\rightarrow M
. Then, take a local section
\sigma
of
P_{G}\rightarrow G
, which is projectable to the local section
\sigma
of
P_{M}\rightarrow M
, and goes through

p_{h}\cdot 1_{\Psi(\sigma(\mathbf{s}h),\mathbf{s}p_{h})^{-1}},

where we used

\Psi:P_{M}\times_{M}P_{M}\xleftarrow{\sim}P_{M}\times_{N}H_{M}\rightarrow H_{M}.

Lastly, we pick a local section
\sigma_{\mu}
of the moment map
\mu:P_{G}\rightarrow N
through
p_{g}
(defined on an open set in the image of
\mu
). This way, we can build the local section

(P_{M}\times_{N}P_{M})\times_{M}G\rightarrow P_{G}\times_{N}P_{G}\qquad(p_{x},p_{\mathbf{s}k},k)\mapsto(\sigma_{\mu}(\mu p_{x})\cdot 1_{\Psi(\mathbf{s}\sigma_{\mu}(\mu p_{x}),p_{x})},\sigma(k)\cdot 1_{\Psi(\sigma(\mathbf{s}k),p_{\mathbf{s}k})})

which induces the desired well-defined local section through
[p_{g},p_{h}]\in P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}
.
∎

We will argue next that the gauge groupoid of a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid is always vertically core-transitive. Basically, this follows from the fact that the structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid is always vertically core-transitive (see Remark
A.4
).

Lemma A.18
.

Let
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
be a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid, and denote by
H_{G}\rightrightarrows H_{M}\rightrightarrows N
its structural strict Lie
2
-groupoid. Then its gauge double groupoid
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}

is vertically core-transitive.

Proof.

We write
F_{H}
for the core of
H_{G}
and
F
for the core of the gauge double groupoid. Note that

F=\{[p_{g},q_{1_{\mathbf{s}g}}]\in P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}\mid\mathbf{s}p=\mathbf{s}q\in P_{M}\}.

Now, let
\sigma
be a (local) section of
P_{G}\times_{N}P_{G}\rightarrow G\times G
that is
\mathbf{s}\times\mathbf{s}
-projectable to a section of
P_{M}\times_{N}P_{M}\rightarrow M\times M
, and consider a section of
\mathbf{t}_{\downarrow}:F_{H}\rightarrow N
. Define

a:G\times G\xrightarrow{\sigma}P_{G}\times_{N}P_{G}\rightarrow N\rightarrow F_{H}.

Given
[p_{g},q_{1_{\mathbf{s}g}}]\in F
, we take
\sigma
defined on an open set of
(g,1_{\mathbf{s}g})
, and such that
\sigma(g,1_{\mathbf{s}g})=(p_{g},q_{1_{\mathbf{s}g}})
. Then the pointwise multiplication map

\sigma\cdot a:G\times G\rightarrow P_{G}\times_{H_{G}}P_{G}

defines through

G\hookrightarrow G\times 1\xrightarrow{\sigma\cdot a}F

the desired section of
F\rightarrow G
through
[p_{g},q_{1_{\mathbf{s}g}}]\in F
. This proves the statement.
∎

In turn, a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid comes with a vertical core extension. We can describe it as follows.

Definition A.19
.

Let
P_{G}\rightrightarrows P_{M}
be a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid. The core extension of
P_{G}
is the principal groupoid bundle
P_{M}
together with the core extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1.}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{M}\times_{H_{M}}P_{M}}

\circlearrowright

Remark A.20
.

In the above discussion, the core groupoid
F
is isomorphic to

P_{G}/H_{M}\rightrightarrows P_{M}/H_{M}=M

where
H_{M}
acts on
P_{G}
via the unit. The isomorphism is given by

P_{G}/H_{M}\rightarrow F\qquad[p]\mapsto[p,1_{\mathbf{s}p}].

This should be compared with Section
5.5
.

If
P_{G}
is a regular general linear PB-groupoid, then the gauge groupoid associated to the principal groupoid bundle
P_{M}=\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
is the Lie groupoid
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
. To tie everything together, we end this section with the following result.

Proposition A.21
.

Let
F_{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, and let
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
be a regular
2
-term complex. Consider the canonical (conjugation) action
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})\textnormal{ }\rotatebox[origin]{270.0}{\large$\circlearrowright$}\textnormal{ H}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Then
F_{G}
defines a fat extension of
G
over
C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M}
if and only if
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1,}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})}

\circlearrowright

defines a core extension.

Proof.

The equivariance condition is equivalent to
F_{G}
restricting to the canonical cochain complex representation, and the Peiffer identity translates to the fact that the two natural conjugation actions of
F_{G}
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
agree. This proves the statement.
∎

Remark A.22
.

That the action in a core extension
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
H
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\rightarrow}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{\downarrow}}

\circlearrowright

defines a bundle of Lie groups representation uses, of course, the group structure of
G_{\downarrow}
explicitly. But the equivariance and Peiffer identity use the groupoid structure
G_{\downarrow}
in terms of the groupoid map
F\rightarrow G_{\downarrow}
only.

While the regularity of the complex in the above statement is used to ensure that
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
is a smooth Lie groupoid, this is irrelevant for the description of a fat extension
F_{G}
. The reason is that we can “bypass”
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
by embedding
\textnormal{Aut}(C_{M}\rightarrow V_{M})
into, for example,
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. Although the conjugation action of
\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
on
\textnormal{H}(V_{M},C_{M})
is not well-defined, it is well-defined on the image of
F_{G}\rightarrow\textnormal{GL}(C_{M},V_{M})
. So, a fat extension is, in essence, a type of core extensions, but only when interpreting the concept in such a more general form.

In general, the gauge double groupoid of a PB-groupoid with gauge double groupoid is encoded by a core extension. But the above remark suggests that, at least, general linear PB-groupoids are also encoded by a type of “core extension”: fat extensions. Investigating this definition for PB-groupoids in more general setups (in the style of the previous remark) seems an interesting direction that will be pursued elsewhere.
